# Chapter 1. Udaypur: Historical Memory and the Making of a Place
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 49)
What's in a name? One Udaipur is in Rajasthan — behold its fortune. Another Udaipur is in Madhya Pradesh — come and behold its misfortune too.
C01-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 2 (Hindi original)
This place's fame is from the Neelkantheshwar temple, which was built by Maharaj Udayaditya, who came in the generation after King Bhoj. The name of this town, Udaipur, too is connected with him.
C01-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 18 (Hindi original)
On 6 January 2021, the title of my second post was — "Say Not Udaipur but Astpur [Sunset-town]."
C01-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 22 (Hindi original)
The king Udayaditya, after whose name this city was named, is the 12th king of the Paramara dynasty.

C01-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 129 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6

C01-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 150 (Hindi original)
its splendour would have been that of the temple-city of the ancient Dasharna kingdom.
C01-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 163 (Hindi original)

A ruined little town of eight thousand people, existing on the margins, which for several past decades had wanted to say something.

It was a small place situated five hundred kilometres from Dhar, the capital of the Paramaras, on their border — which certainly had strategic importance in the sense that it touched the territory of the Kalachuri dynasty. Udaipur became an important centre situated on the frontier of the two dynasties because there were sandstone quarries here.

This too was printed in the seventh and tenth reports of the survey (1874–75 and 1876–77) by Major General Alexander Cunningham, the first head of the Archaeological Survey of India. J. Bühler mentioned it in the first volume of *Epigraphia Indica*, 1888.
C01-JAGTA-EN-020   ≈ p. 139 (Hindi original)
in this same series a total of 11 inscriptions are found from Udaipur, which tell of the political, economic and social conditions of this period.

C01-JAGTA-EN-026   ≈ p. 32 (Hindi original)   also Ch 10
And one most important question — despite all this, how did this temple survive? Why could it not meet the same fate that was meted out to the Vijay Mandir in Vidisha? Had the local citizens who resisted the invaders prevailed, so that after only minor breakage this temple was saved? Someone's courage must indeed have clashed with those religious ignoramuses (*jahil*) here; it must have some name — it is another matter that that name and face are absent from history.

C01-JAGTA-EN-038   ≈ p. 164 (Hindi original)

About Udaipur no such details are found. No one wrote its details.
C01-JAGTA-EN-040   ≈ p. 8 (Hindi original)

C01-JAGTA-EN-042   ≈ p. 19 (Hindi original)
Now this question must arise in your mind — how did this temple survive?
This question must indeed arise in your mind — how did this temple survive? Who stopped its fate from becoming like that of Vidisha's Bija Mandal? Was it because of the organized resistance of the local people of Udaipur and this region that only the images could be broken, and those plunderers were driven away from here — who roamed the whole country for centuries devastating our honour-points, and whom the historians of free India adorned in books by calling them sultans and badshahs!
C01-JAGTA-EN-043   ≈ p. 41 (Hindi original); also at ≈ p. 41 (Hindi original)   also Ch 10
But Dr. Narayan Vyas, after looking at that image carefully, on the way back told [us] that it is the image of a yaksha.
C01-JAGTA-EN-044   ≈ p. 111 (Hindi original)
Udaipur is a poem written on stones, which the Paramara kings sang in praise of their adored Lord Shiva. In composing this praise, three years' time was taken, and thousands of artisans, having come from who-knows-where, had displayed the very best of their art.
C01-JAGTA-EN-045   ≈ p. 121 (Hindi original)

C01-JAGTA-EN-048   ≈ p. 131 (Hindi original)
As far as Udaipur is concerned, it is evident from its very name that it is related to King Udayadity. The fact that no inscription of the earlier Paramara kings is found here tells us that this town came into existence only in Udayadity's time.

## Geo-Heritage of Udaypur, Vidisha District, Madhya Pradesh: The Third Eye (GEOHER, 24)
The town's character as a village has remained intact, offering a picturesque glimpse into rural life where tradition and simplicity harmonize seamlessly.

C01-GEOHER-003   p. 20

C01-GEOHER-004   p. 21

ii. It has the 11th century CE Udayaditya Parmar Neelkantheshwar Mahadev Temple, also, which is also protected by the Archaeological Survey of India.
C01-GEOHER-005   p. 151
The Udaypur Prashasti, a stone inscription in two parts currently kept in Gwalior Museum, mentions the construction of this lake by King Udayaditya. The year mentioned is 1059 CE. It mentions the foundation year of Udaypur, Udayeshwar temple, and Udaysagar Lake as the same.
C01-GEOHER-006   p. 74   also Ch 6
It is said to have been built by King Udayaditya when he established the city.
C01-GEOHER-007   p. 7   also Ch 7

C01-GEOHER-008   p. 12
Another oral tradition also states that the hilly region was the territory of the Gonds. Udayaditya conquered it and renamed it Udaypur.
C01-GEOHER-009   p. 13
this was the ancient pilgrimage site, which was visited by pilgrims from faraway places in Rajasthan. It is recorded in the stone inscriptions found on the hillock.
C01-GEOHER-010   p. 18   also Ch 6, Ch 8
The local folklore talks about a local chief by the name of Bhringraj, and the village was called Bhringrajpur.
C01-GEOHER-011   p. 46
the team interacted with the village locals, who they recalled that Udaypur once had water from “chappan baoli, bavan kuae and saadhe baarah talab” (56 step-wells, 52 wells and 12 and half lakes).
C01-GEOHER-012   p. 74   also Ch 7, Ch 8

C01-GEOHER-013   p. 74

C01-GEOHER-014   p. 75   also Ch 9

C01-GEOHER-015   p. 126   also Ch 6

C01-GEOHER-016   p. 7

C01-GEOHER-017   p. 11   also Ch 5
We find People of all religions and faiths staying in the village, the fine antiquities of the Jains, the Mohammadan, Hindu temples, mahals, large havelis, etc.
C01-GEOHER-018   p. 46   also Ch 9
This part of Madhya Pradesh is historically the most significant area in Central India. It is home to Bhimbetka and Sanchi, both of which are UNESCO-recognised world heritage monuments. Looking at the unique history of Vidisha from the time of Buddha and Ashoka, this area has an immense archaeological treasure.
Heritage Hill of Udaypur, because of its proximity to so many archaeological sites, needs to be publicised as a geo-heritage reserve. however, this plan must be in harmony with the larger Tourism Development Plan of this 11th-century CE Parmar capital city.

Which dynasty he belonged to is lost in history?
C01-GEOHER-022   p. 46

C01-GANGULY-012   p. 267

Gunpowder was heaped in and around the temple, but the priests, by secretly introducing streams of water upon them, rendered the ammunition ineffective. The Sultan took it for a miracle, and revoked his order.

## Corpus Inscriptionum Indicarum, Vol. VII, Part 2: Inscriptions of the Paramāras — Trivedi, Harihar Vithal (ed.) (CII, 13)
The last line of the record expresses the purpose, in which the temple is mentioned by the name Udayésvaradeva, evidently after the name of its constructor.
C01-CII-001   p. 113   also Ch 6
The only geographical name mentioned in the record is Udayapura, which is said to be the capital town of the Nēmaka dynasty (1. 6), which was subordinate to Udayāditya. This place still bears this name, as we have already seen.
C01-CII-002   p. 139   also Ch 6
It was probably situated in the vicinity of Udaipur. Pañchamukha, which too appears to be the name of a village from the context, cannot as well be identified. However, taking Pañchamukha as denoting Śiva, we may suggest that Udaipur itself may have been intended by the expression Pañchamukhapattana.
C01-CII-003   p. 281   also Ch 6

C01-CII-004   p. 285

C01-CII-005   p. 285   also Ch 6
denoted the town or village (pattana) after the temple of Śiva, i.e., Nilakanthésvara at Udaipur itself. Thus we have to take the same place denoted by two names, which is rather curious.
C01-CII-006   p. 288
the importance of the inscription lies in the fact that it supplies the earliest known date for the king Udayāditya, who, according to the prasasti found at the same place, rescued the earth (i.e., the Paramāra kingdom), as the primeval boar

the inscription is important as it gives a complete and exhaustive genealogy of the Paramāra house from the earliest ruler down to Udayāditya. It does not bear any date nor is the purpose shown in the existing portion. It is a prasasti, intended to be put in a part of the temple, as stated above.

## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 13)
Udayāditya extended patronage to art and architecture. He founded the city of Udayapur which is located in modern day Vidisha. In this city, he constructed several temples, the most famous being the temple of Udayeśvara.
C01-PANDE-001   p. 8   also Ch 4
G. Buhler, "Udepur Prasasti of the Kings of Malva" in Epigraphia Indica vol. 1
C01-PANDE-002   p. 92   also Ch 6
The legend regarding this masjid is that the Emperor Aurangzeb, on his return from the Dakhin, passed this place, and seeing this temple, ordered its demolition and the construction of a masjid with its materials.
C01-PANDE-003   p. 90
Whatever the credit attaching to this legend, certain it is that at the present day the temple is frequented by both Hindus and Muhammadans, and the lingam within equally reverenced by both.
C01-PANDE-004   p. 90   also Ch 9
According to local tradition, in 1325 CE, after the conquest of Udayapur, Sultan Muhammad Tughlaq had given orders to raze the temple. However, the temple was not razed and only a part of the Vedic-hall on the west was converted to a mosque
C01-PANDE-005   p. 91
Near the temple is a house called Ghariyālon Kā Makān, i.e. the house of the time-keepers of the temple, who claim to have been there for centuries.
C01-PANDE-006   p. 92
The Udayeśvara temple at Udayapur, situated in the Malwa region of Madhya Pradesh, ranks as one of the most important early medieval temples of North India. It is a Śaiva temple built in the second half of the 11th century CE by Udayāditya Paramāra who was a scion of the illustrious Paramāra dynasty that ruled over Malwa.
C01-PANDE-007   p. 8   also Ch 5
This temple is one of the few in north India that has survived the onslaught of Islamic invasions and one of the best specimens of central Indian medieval architecture.
C01-PANDE-008   p. 91
"The beautiful temple of Udayeswara is the great attraction of Udaypur.
C01-PANDE-009   p. 91
This temple is a living one, where right up to the 21st century, there are the usual morning and evening pūja and aarti.

This brass cover was presented by Khanderao Appaji, a general of Mahadji Scindia, the local ruler, in … 1775 CE.

## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 11)
One of the many old Sanskrit inscriptions on this temple records that the Paramara king Udayaditya of Malwa founded a town, built a temple of Shiva and excavated a tank, and designated all the three works after his own name as Udayapura, Udayeshvara and Udayasamudra respectively.
C01-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 115   also Ch 4, Ch 6
This town lies 4 miles by a metalled road to the east of the Bareth Station on the Central Railway between Bina and Bhilsa. It can also be visited from the next station Basoda, whence it is about 8 miles by a fair weather road.
C01-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 114

A curious human figure fixed up near the pinnacle of the spire is variously interpreted as that of the architect, who designed … the edifice or as of the royal builder climbing up to heaven in virtue of the religious merit which he earned by constructing such a magnificent temple.

H. 737-39 (A.D. 1336-38) as known from the two Persian inscriptions related to this mosque.
C01-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 117   also Ch 10
by a woman out of her earning made by grinding flour for the workmen employed for the construction of the Udayeshvara temple. This tradition is, however, belied by the style of the architecture of the temple which shows that it is a much later structure.
C01-MADHYABHARAT-006   p. 118   also Ch 7
In the long array of these sacred structures the Udayeshwar temple stands supreme representing the best of what the ancient architects of Madhya Bharat could achieve. Fortunately it had escaped from the ravages of the idol breakers.

## Temples of India (TEMPLES-IND, 1)

# Chapter 2. The Betwa Valley: Geography, Geology and Natural Environment
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 59)
The Betwa or Betrawati is a river of great antiquity. The Betwa rises from the main Vindhyan range in the extreme south-west (23°2' N., 77°20' E) of Raisen district. It is the third largest river of Malwa region. After a course of 96 km, it enters the District 6 km, south of Vidisha.
C02-GAZ-001   p. 18
The entire District lies in the drainage basin of the Yamuna. The general slope is from south to north in which direction all the major streams of the District flow. The drainage area of these streams is separated by two sub-water divides which run from south to north in the District. Between these two sub-water divides is the Betwa Basin proper.
C02-GAZ-002   p. 18
It leaves the District after a course of about 112 km. after its confluence with the Kethan and forms the boundary between Guna, Shivpuri, Jhansi and Jalaun districts on its left and Sagar, Jhansi, Tikamgarh and Hamirpur on the right. The important tributaries of the river are the Kaliasot, Ajoar, Besh, Bah, Sagar, Kethan, Orr, etc., joining it on the left bank, and the Richhan, Dabar, Nion, Parasri, Bina, Jamni, Dhasan and Birma on the right bank. Of these the Dhasan is a very significant stream of Bundelkhand region. Among the tributaries the Besh, Sahodra, Bah, Sagar Kethan, Nion, Parasri, and Bina flow in Vidisha District. Beshnagar in Vidisha District is a place of great archaeological importance along this river.
C02-GAZ-003   p. 19
The last of the significant tributaries joining the Betwa on its left is Kethan. It rises from the hill of Sakhlon between Murdnagar and Sironj town. The river is being tapped for a medium size irrigation tank. After a north-easterly course of 35 km., it enters Guna district. From near Bahadurpur in Guna it turns to the east and forms the northern most part of Vidisha District boundary where it joins the Betwa.
C02-GAZ-004   p. 20
The Nion rises from the Garhi range, opposite the source of the Bina in Raisen and flows to the north-west. It drains the southern part of Vidisha District and joins the Betwa near Sumer.
The Keotan river is an important stream rising from the Mahuakhera hill of the Eastern (Teonda) range and joining the Betwa on its right bank. The river's length is entirely confined to the District.
C02-GAZ-005   p. 20
Vidisha District extends between the latitude 23° 21' and 24° 22' north and longitude 77° 15'30" and 78° 18' east. The District is situated in the central part of the State and eastern part of the fertile Malwa region. The shape of this District is more or less elliptical and the longer axis lies from N.W. to S.E. with slight projections on the north, north west, south and southwest.
C02-GAZ-006   p. 13
The District of Vidisha lies on the Vidhyachal plateau, off the main Vidhyachal range, which throws several spurs towards the north and northeast. The plateau slopes towards the north and is drained by a number of rivers of Malwa and Bundelkhand. These rivers have formed their valleys between the spur fanges. Most of Vidisha lies in the valley of the Betwa river, which flows from south to north. This valley is bordered by the Garhi-Teonda range in the east and Ganari-Raghoghar range in the west. Both these ranges form part of the ranges of Vidhyachal on the Malwa plateau and extend from south to north.
C02-GAZ-007   p. 16
The geological strata of this area differs from the rest of the District in as much as the trap cover has been removed by the erosion from a large area and the substrata are rich in hard sedimentary rocks, sandstone and limestone of very high quality. However, variations in the colour, texture and quality may be found. The hills and slopes are covered with forests. Gyaraspur and Udaipur are located amidst west side foothills of this range.
C02-GAZ-008   p. 17
The different geological formations occurring in Vidisha (Bhilsa) District are (i) Alluvium, (ii) Laterite and (iii) Deccan Trap (Malwa Trap) with inter-trapean beds.
Alluvium occurs over a large part of the area particularly along the course of streams like the Betwa, Sagar, Besh, etc. It consists mainly of a yellow or grey-brown sandy clay and contains a large proportion of Kankar as seen near Israbar, Basoda, Imibia, Garla, etc. Along the Betwa river, it often froms steep cliffs in the northern part of the area.
C02-GAZ-009   p. 20–21
The Deccan Trap (Malwa Trap) is the most widespread rock formation in this District. These rocks are horizontal or nearly horizontal, the dark Lava flows lend to the countryside a terraced appearance. The principal rock is a basalt having vesicles filled with zeolite, agate, calcite, etc.
The inter-trapeans, mainly impure crystalline limestone and cacareous chert, occur as residual blocks and boulders scattered over the surface, as well as discontinuous outcrops amidst the Deccan Trap.
C02-GAZ-010   p. 21
Seismically Vidisha District is located in a zone where earthquakes have been felt with slight intensity in the past. Although no epicentre of earthquake of any consequence has been located in the District, it has experienced fringe effects of the great earthquakes originating in the Himalayan Boundary Fault Zone and the Rann of Kutch.
C02-GAZ-011   p. 23
The Mixed forests on Vindhyan sandstones and resultant soils are almost all without teak (Tactona grandis) in Shamshabad and Gyaraspur ranges. The general quality of the mixed crop is comparatively poor. The height growth is invariably below 12.49 metres. The soil is generally shallow sandy loam. However, in well-drained soils along water-courses height-growth is better.
C02-GAZ-012   p. 26
The soil in the District is generally kali-matti called black cotton soil. It is named mar in local dialect. It is very good for wheat. But its utility is reduced considerably if kans is allowed to grow on it. It is a rich black loamy soil with a high degree of fertility and extraordinary power of retaining moisture.
C02-GAZ-013   p. 98
The monsoon sets in generally during June and continues till the end of September. The year may be divided into four seasons. The cold season from December to February is followed by the hot season till mid-June. The period from mid-June to about the end of September constitutes the south-west monsoon season. October and November may be termed the post-monsoon or retreating monsoon season.
C02-GAZ-014   p. 30
The climate of the District is generally dry except during the south-west monsoon season. The nights are generally pleasant even in summer and justify the praise by the Mughals for Shab-i-Malwa famous throughout India. The summer is less intense than other places of North India and the winter is not very cold.
C02-GAZ-015   p. 30
The average annual rainfall in the District is 1,229.9 mm. The rainfall in the District generally increases from the north-west to the southeast. About 92 per cent of the annual rainfall in the District is received during the south-west monsoon months, June to September, July being the rainiest month.
C02-GAZ-016   p. 30
In the 50 years period from 1901 to 1950, highest annual rainfall amounting to 149 per cent of the normal had occurred in 1948. The lowest annual rainfall which was 50 per cent of the normal occurred in 1905.
C02-GAZ-017   p. 30
Considering the rainfall at individual stations, more than two consecutive years of such low rainfall had occurred at two stations viz., Vidisha and Basoda. Low rainfall, 20 per cent less than the normal, occurred in seven years at Vidisha and in four years at Basoda, consecutive years of such low rainfall occurred thrice at Vidisha and only once at Basoda during the 50 years period.
C02-GAZ-018   p. 31
This number varies from 47 at Basoda to 56 at Mohammadgarh. The heaviest rainfall recorded in 24 hours at any station in the District was 360.2 mm. at Basoda on the 31st July, 1951.
C02-GAZ-019   p. 31
Except during the south-west monsoon season when the relative humidity is above 70 per cent, the air is generally dry over the District. The driest part of the year is the summer season when in afternoons the relative humidity is less than 20 per cent.
During the monsoon season the skies are generally heavily clouded or overcast. In the rest of the year skies are mostly clear or lightly clouded.
C02-GAZ-020   p. 31
However, after February temperatures increase steadily till May, which is generally the hottest month with the mean daily maximum temperature at about 40°C and the mean daily minimum at about 26°C. On individual days during the summer season the maximum temperature may sometimes go above 44°C. With the onset of the south-west monsoon in the District by about mid-June there is appreciable drop in temperatures. After the withdrawal of the monsoon by about the end of September, there is a slight increase in the day temperature but the nights become progressively cooler. After October both the day and night temperatures steadily decrease till January which is the coldest month with the mean daily maximum temperature at about 25°C., and the mean daily minimum at about 10·0°C. The District is affected by cold waves during the cold season in association with western disturbances passing across the northern part of India and the minimum temperature may drop down occasionally to a degree or so above the freezing point of water and frosts may occur.
C02-GAZ-021   p. 31
During the monsoon season depressions originating in the Bay of Bengal move in some westerly direction and reach the District or its neighbourhood causing widespread heavy rain on gusty winds. Thunderstorms occur during the period from January to October, the highest incidence being in the early monsoon season. These may be associated with hail in winter and pre-monsoon months. Duststorms occur occasionally during the summer.
C02-GAZ-022   p. 32

C02-GAZ-026   p. 358
The forests cover about two-fifths of the total area in the District. They mostly occupy the hill ranges and steep slopes both on the trap formations and on the Vindhyan-sandstones. A few patches of vegetation are also to be found along the water courses.
C02-GAZ-027   p. 24
By far the most extensive forests are predominated by khair (Acacia catechu) in the Dry Mixed Deciduous forests. These occur on lateritic soils (Rakad) covering almost the entire Sironj range except Chitawar block, and small portions of Lateri range. Khair (Acacia catechu) forms more than 50 per cent of the crop and frequently occurs almost pure in association with palas (Butea monosperma).
C02-GAZ-028   p. 26
Dendrocalamus strictus is the only species of bamboos occurring in the District. It is found in a narrow belt fairly well-grown on either side of the Dudhia river in Tinsia and Chamarkondal blocks of Lateri range. In Sironj range these are localised in a small patch near Samal Kheri in Garoth block.
C02-GAZ-029   p. 26
This type is found in dry exposed localities where soil is superficial due to heavy grazing and fires are frequent. The height growth is usually below 12.29 metres. The quality of the crop corresponds to All India Quality V. Teak forms about 40 to 60 per cent of the crop. The ground-cover consists of thick shrubs and grasses.
C02-GAZ-030   p. 26
The extension of cultivation, the destruction of forest areas, and the absence of scientific management of the game in the past, adversely affected the wild life in the District. The ex-rulers and jagirdars shot the game for fun as and when they liked in their respective jurisdictions, though the general public was debarred from shooting in the Reserves. After the formation of Madhya Bharat in 1948, most of the Reserved forests were opened for permit holders. However, the poaching activities increased during the fifties. The increased number of gun licences and the use of jeep-cars made it easy to kill animals without a sense of adventure and for easy economic gains. However, it is now being suppressed by the Government with greater zeal.
C02-GAZ-031   p. 27   also Ch 12
There are in all 315 Grass Reserves varying in size from 0.2 to 283.3 hectares scattered all over Sironj and Lateri ranges, which are auctioned annually for the cutting of grasses. These consists usually of open understocked forests, both of teak and miscellaneous species. The common grasses found are gunher (Themeda quadrivalvis), phuli (Apluda aristata), paonia (Sehima sulkata), kusul (Heteropogan contortus), kail and machol.
Various plants which some medicinal property or the other is attributed grow in the District. In most of the cases the medicinal property is only a matter of local knowledge and no commercial exploitation is done except for Harra, Aonla and Behara which form the famous Triphala. Some edible gums are obtained from dhaora (Aogoissus latifolia), and khair (Acacia catechu). Mahua (Mahnuca latifolia) is another tree of great value.
C02-GAZ-032   p. 27
The birds met within the District are similar to those found in the adjoining districts. Pea-fowl or mor (Pavo cristatus), red jungle-fowl or jungle-mungi (Gallus ferrugineus), bhat titar (Pterocles exustus), greyfowl or bater … (Coturnis communis), and grey pigeon (Harial), are usually found. In the winter season the migratory water fowls, e. g., geese and ducks like pochards and teals are very common.
C02-GAZ-033   p. 28–29

C02-GAZ-034   p. 28
The zoological types in the category of mammals found in the District are Indian hare or Khargosh (Lepus ruficaudatus), black or kala hiran (Antelona cervicaprea) which has become very rare mostly due to indiscriminate poaching, porcupine, squirrel, beavers, four-horned antilope (Tetracerus quadricornis), chinkara (Gazella bennettii), barking deer (Carvus mutjac), sambhar (Carvus unicolor), chital (Carvus axis), pig, boar, tiger, (Felis tigris), leopard (Felis pardus), nilgai (Boselaphus tragocamelus), and monkey (Semnopithecus entehus).
C02-GAZ-035   p. 28
The fish fauna of Vidisha District comprises some of the important varieties, viz., Katla (Catla catla), rohu (Labeo rohita), kalbasu (Labeo calbasu), Labeo jubratus, gol (Labeo gonius) Labeo bata, mirgal (Cirrhina mirgala) (Barbus tor), Barbus serana, saul (Ophiocephalus marulinus), Attu, Mystus seenghaia, etc.
Amongst the acquatic species crabs, turtles, snails, liverworts, beetles, and water crickets are very common.
C02-GAZ-036   p. 30
Major part of the forests, about 60 per cent, produce timber and fuel wood, the principal forest produce of the District. Other produce of the local forests include bamboo, gum, tendu leaves, lac, honey, wax, catechu (katha,) grass and bones, etc.
C02-GAZ-037   p. 117

Bhubhushi (Eragrostis tenella)

C02-GAZ-039   p. 378
Its cover in the valleys is a rich black soil because of the silt deposits mostly derived from the basalt rocks. Mostly these have been taken for cultivation and the tract is known as one of the major wheat growing tracts of Malwa.
C02-GAZ-040   p. 17
The District provides inexhaustible reserves of building materials.
C02-GAZ-041   p. 21
A great thickness of the upper Vindhyan sandstones covers a large area of the District. These sandstones form excellent building material and are extensively quarried. Most of the quarries are in Kaimur or Rewa sandstones. The Kaimur sandstone is usually the strongest and most durable material. It is usually utilised for building massive structures. The sandstone being softer is used for the purpose of delicate carvings for which the carvers from Bhopal Division are conveniently situated with regard to transport facilities.
C02-GAZ-042   p. 22   also Ch 8
Good supply of groundwater is always somewhat problematical in the Deccan trap country. The chances of locating sufficient groundwater lie in the porous inter-trappean beds or in the underlying rocks, where the trap is thin. The storage of water in the rock itself depends on the nature and frequency of fissures, presence of dykes and mostly on the degree of weathering and disintegration undergone.
Due to the impervious nature of the rock, storage reservoirs are likely to be successful depending upon the local topography and the rainfall. In a general way shallow wells in the trap country yield all the water needed except in the summer season of a drought year. Within the traps, the water level is found to vary in depth from 6 to 12 m.
C02-GAZ-043   p. 23   also Ch 7
There are occurrences of laterite of highly ferruginous composition spread over an area in the District. The laterite cappings were tapped at places as a source of iron-ore for the indigenous furnaces.
C02-GAZ-044   p. 23   also Ch 8
Recuperation Because of its rich black cotton soil, the District has a considerable power of quick recuperation and, thus over all toning in economy was felt in this decade.
C02-GAZ-045   p. 68
This comparative richness of the Vidisha cultivator is apparently due to the rich soil and the bountiful nature. The tract was sparsely cultivated not more than 60 years ago and Captain Luard, the author of the Gwalior State Gazetteer, had many misgivings when in 1907 he observed, "Ample room for the extension of cultivation exists; but it is impossible to hope for any great increase in this direction unless the population rises considerably especially in Malwa where owing to very sparse population the finest soil is lying fallow."1 Conditions have now changed entirely after Independence and Vidisha has the highest per capita net sown area in the State, viz., 1.94 acres.
C02-GAZ-046   p. 91
The duab formed by the Bah river in the north and the Betwa in the south has the richest cultivated area of Vidisha tahsil. Small patches of good land are available near Shamshabad in the west and around Nateran in the east. The tract between the Kethan and Nateran forms the major portion of Kurwai tahsil where also the lands are quite rich though not as good as the plains of Vidisha and Basoda tahsils. The entire central tract is very rich because of the silt deposits and has been one of the major wheat growing districts of the erstwhile Gwalior State and now of Madhya Pradesh.
C02-GAZ-047   p. 99
Apart from this, building construction was also important in Vidisha. Regarding lime-stone work and sandstone, it is stated that “the buildings in Bhilsa, Gyaraspur and many other places all testify to its extensive use from an early period”.
C02-GAZ-048   p. 121   also Ch 7
Vidisha District at present, except for the fine sand, has no mineral resources of any importance. All over the District are mines and quarries of minor minerals like clay, sandstone, murram, chhuimitii, etc.
C02-GAZ-049   p. 125
Fine sandstone is found in many places of Vidisha District. The buildings in Vidisha, Gyaraspur and many other places all testify to its extensive use from an early period. The stone used consists of both sandstone and limestone.
C02-GAZ-050   p. 125   also Ch 7
Economic activity in this District as in most of the other districts in the State, revolves round agriculture. It is in the production of rabi crops that the District predominates.
C02-GAZ-051   p. 185   also Ch 8
Vidisha District has been endowed with rich soils of the Malwa belt and is the centre of trade for its quality gram and wheat. This has given better economic deal to both the agricultural and the business community of this area. Although the majority of the people lead a simple frugal way of life, yet the District bears a look of agricultural prosperity.
C02-GAZ-052   p. 192   also Ch 8
Vidisha has a water-supply scheme run by the Public Health Engineering Department, the source being the river Betwa. No other town in the District has water works at present. In all other places people utilise water from wells. In a few villages people take water from rivers or nalas also.
C02-GAZ-053   p. 247
It seems that the river Bes was dammed here and the water carried on to irrigate the fields through a canal. Quite an intricate network of canals must have been constructed of which that found on the Kham Baba site is but one. It seems that this excavated canal had been a storage canal as indicated by the cross wall joining the south and north walls.
C02-GAZ-054   p. 330   also Ch 3
The interesting object of the place is its fort built of the gnesis rock, which is abundantly found in the neighbourhood. The fort stands on a small hill located to the east of the town. Most of the houses of this town are also built of the same gnesis rock and are roofed with big slabs.
C02-GAZ-055   p. 338
Land Utilization

C02-GAZ-060   p. 380–381   also Ch 8
## Geo-Heritage of Udaypur, Vidisha District, Madhya Pradesh: The Third Eye (GEOHER, 30)
Udaypur is a village panchayat in Ganj Basoda Tehsil of the Vidisha district. As rivers in this landscape are absent, the region is dependent on a network of surface water bodies such as lakes and ponds, formed from natural depressions on the ground.
C02-GEOHER-001   p. 7   also Ch 12
Kewtan River, one of the tributaries of the Betwa River, flows from south to north and merges with the Betwa River at Kotha Barrage dam. The river is situated at a distance of 1.8 km from the site of Shiva Pindi and just 1 km from the village settlement.
C02-GEOHER-002   p. 26
The major part of this report shows that physical rockes are largely sedimentary. Most of the terrain around Udaypur, and the surrounding area are sandstone. This is also seen in the widespread sandstone quarrying carried out all around the project area.
C02-GEOHER-003   p. 14
Geological Formations- Understanding the composition, structure, and evolution of Udaypur's rock formations, including its butte and mesa landforms, caves, and sedimentary layers. The study analyzes how natural forces such as erosion, weathering, and tectonic activity have shaped the terrain.
C02-GEOHER-004   p. 16
Amidst the eastern part of the Vindhyanchal range is located a village named Udaypur with a total geographical area of 1966 hectares (19.66 km²). The area lies between coordinates ranging from 78°2'E 23°55'N and 78°8'E 23°48'N. The area falls in the survey of India Toposheet number 55I/1. The elevation ranges in the area varies from 340 to 480m above MSL. It has an average elevation of 410 meters.
C02-GEOHER-005   p. 21
Vidisha is on the Vindhyanchal plateau, which has a number of spurs that point to the north and northeast. Vidisha district forms part of the Malwa plateau and the Vindhya hill range with undulating topography. The district is primarily an agricultural district occupying the Betwa Basin valley, having a predominantly agricultural economy.
C02-GEOHER-006   p. 21
The maximum part of Udaypur Village is covered by the Sandstone and quaternary deposits, and most of the landforms in the area are the landforms of these rocks. The geomorphologic features of the area have been identified in the Landsat satellite image and demarcated using the GIS platform. This hill region shows the Butte Formation, which is a very small, steep-sided, flat-topped plateau. Additionally, the south side of the village and the site show an undulated topography with moderately dissected hills all over the southern landscape.
C02-GEOHER-007   p. 25
It is commonly assumed that the geomorphic evolution of tablelands, where durable caprock overlies weaker formations, proceeds via escarpment retreat, which leaves residual tabular hills (mesas) in front of the preceding scarps. These, in turn, reduced in extent towards buttes and then the pinnacles in the final stage. In this paper, we use empirical evidence from the Udaypur Sandstone Tableland in Vidisha to show that different pathways of mesa decay exist and that various scenarios are possible.
Depending on mesa morphology and the degree of its dissection, penultimate stages may include classic buttes, a series of small, isolated crags, or rock labyrinths (rock cities) subject to slow lateral outward displacement.
C02-GEOHER-008   p. 27
The tableland of Udaypur village lies in the Basoda Tehsil of the Vidisha district. It owes its existence to the extensive presence of flat-lying sedimentary rocks, sandstones are medium to coarse-grained, typically cross-bedded. They are separated by thin(up to 4 cm thickness) horizons of poorly cemented, fine-grained sandstones, glauconites, marls, and claystones. These fine-grained sediments are more prone to weathering and, therefore, play an important role in the evolution of
C02-GEOHER-009   p. 31
The mesa of Udaypur is a two-story one, with the lower cliff line an intermediate bench, and the upper cliff line in caprock 10-25 m high. Where these cliff lines join, their height reaches 36 m. Valleys on the Udaypur mesa are invariably dry and lack channels.
Udaypur Hill is an isolated hill rising some 100 m above the surrounding flatland and an excellent example of a classic butte. Its nearly level, bare-rock summit surface 45 m long and 35 m wide, is bounded by continuous cliff lines, reaching a height of up to 35 m.
C02-GEOHER-010   p. 35
The ‘model’ trajectory, from a mesa through a butte to a pinnacle, is represented by Udaypur Hill as the penultimate stage and a cylindrical hill as the ultimate stage. A butte is likely to develop from a moderately fragmented but deeply dissected mesa, where caprock is thick, marginal slopes are nearly vertical cliffs, and continuous (although not necessarily frequent) rock falls detach large volumes of rock, reducing the caprock towards a butte, perhaps of smaller magnitude as suggested by the small size of boulders on the lower slopes of Udaypur Hill, will reduce its extent even further, towards a pinnacle. A pinnacle, however, decays mostly via small-scale block detachments and grain-by-grain disintegration.
C02-GEOHER-011   p. 36
Gully Erosion: Gully erosion is a natural process where concentrated runoff water flows down a slope, carving out channels in the soil and rock. The curvature seen in the site could indeed indicate the flow of water over time. As the water flows, it carries sediment and debris, gradually cutting through the softer material and exposing the underlying moraine.
3. Differential Weathering: Different types of rock and soil erode at different rates. If the moraine material is more resistant to weathering compared to the surrounding rocks, it would have remained intact while the softer material around it eroded away, forming a pathway.
C02-GEOHER-012   p. 107
A large hill (Shiva Pindi) is made up of sedimentary terrain, and the maximum area of Udaypur has sedimentary structures.
C02-GEOHER-013   p. 128
Butte (Small Rock Hill): The elevation level of this small hill, shaped like a tall monolithic rock, is slightly higher than the larger hill, which is at 582.31 meters above MSL. The sharp chisel marks on the entire hill show strong … wind-weathering and erosion effects. A thick bed of 60-100 cm forms the bottom of the hill, while the thin bed is at the top level.
C02-GEOHER-014   p. 130–131
Mesa (Large hill/ Shiva Pindi): With an overall perimeter of 577 meters and a topmost elevation level of 567.66 meters, the large hill forms the skyline of this particular region. Marks of aerial erosion, wind weathering, and water erosion with colour transitions between black and brown shades formed the outer appearance of the hill. Irregular vegetation growth and roots of shrubs and bushes embedded into the hill's horizontal bedding layers, destroying the natural appearance and fading this geological marvel's charm.
C02-GEOHER-015   p. 132
In Udaypur, at the hill Shiva Pindi, we found both types of caves, natural and man-made. Natural caves are formed by the dissolution of limestone. Most caves are created when slowly moving water dissolves, or eats away at limestone rock, creating spaces, caverns, and tunnel-like passages.
C02-GEOHER-016   p. 135
CAVE I: On the western side, a narrow-channeled cave having an extended inner depth of 9 meters towards the right of the entrance and is currently occupied by thousands of bats. The mouth of the cave's entrance is 3.1 meters in length and 2.6 meters in width, with the floor of the entry passageway covered in mounds of bats' excreta called guano. The depth of the guano layer, as measured during the on-site visit, came out to be 500-600 mm.
C02-GEOHER-017   p. 136
Cave IV: Cave IV is on the extreme southern-eastern part of the hill and is the 3rd biggest cave of all the other caves. It's also inaccessible on foot. The survey team couldn't reach there but observed it from the foothill. To get a clear idea about this cave, INTACH Bhopal chapter conducted a drone survey, and only then did they have a brief idea about it. The drone footage reveals that this cave is slightly higher above the other caves and further ahead of Cave III. There's a possibility that it's also inhabited by some scavenger birds.
C02-GEOHER-018   p. 140
The entire area is covered with dense Van Tulsi and local grasses. Trees like Akola, Cactus, Neem, Bel, Tamarind, Palash, and Dodai are native to this region. Red ants, Vultures, Squirrels, Monkeys, Pigeons, Bees, and Parrots are seen in abundance.
C02-GEOHER-019   p. 12
Walking up the hill, which rises almost 270 meters from the level of Udaypur village, we come across interesting geological formations such as caves made by nature. These are deep caves which house thousands of Bats and other birds, animals, and reptiles who have been staying here for millions of years.
C02-GEOHER-020   p. 20
Due to intensive human intervention by way of grazing and illegal tree cutting the flora of this terrain is quite disturbed.
It is time that planning is carried out about the reintroduction of the natural species of this ecosystem.
C02-GEOHER-021   p. 37
The caves around the hill are home to thousands of bats, which have their own ecosystem. A note on the bat excreta, called Guana, deposited by thousands of bats, is also added as a technical note.
C02-GEOHER-022   p. 37
Udaypur hosts over 58 bird species, including iconic vultures like the Oriental White-backed, Long-billed, and Red-headed. During winter, its water bodies attract migratory birds like the Sarus Crane and Painted Stork. Representing the Indo-Malayan Tropical Dry Zone, Udaypur is vital for conserving 33 of the 59 bird species identified in this biome by Bird Life International.
C02-GEOHER-023   p. 40
About the flora of this area, a very interesting aspect was the finding about lichens' deposition over the fortification wall and other parts of the hillock. Lichens are a different world altogether, and it takes hundreds of years of time for these to grow on stones.
The pictures of lichens on the hillock are in Annexure 9 and beyond, along with the names of these lichens. Support of the National Institute of Lichens was taken to identify and document these lichens found on the hill. A study of these microflora can also help in identifying the age of these locations that we intend to study.
C02-GEOHER-024   p. 43   also Ch 11
This site is very rich from an archaeological and architectural heritage point of view. It's also a storehouse, micro flora and fauna, particularly Bryophytes and lichens. They need to be studied more systematically using the services of the expert agencies, such as the Birbal Sahni Institute of Paleobotany or Lichens Park in Uttarakhand, for this research.
C02-GEOHER-025   p. 143
On top of the hill, fragments of Amlakas, decorative slabs, and pillars of red sandstone are found. The use of white sandstone is also seen, but is comparatively less in number.
C02-GEOHER-026   p. 13
Udaypur is covered with 75% of black cotton soil. The other 25% is red-yellow mixed soils derived from the sandstone and shale; laterite, Vindhyan sandstones, Basalts, raw metals, and clay are also found. Udaypur village produces Soybean, Pigeon pea, and Maize in Kharif and Wheat, Gram, and Lentil in Rabi season. The Kharif area is 90% rainfed, and the Rabi area is 34% rainfed.
C02-GEOHER-027   p. 23   also Ch 8
Water attained paramount importance in the undulating plains of Central India due to its semi-arid climatic conditions. Because of this, the collection, preservation, and use of water became one of the defining characteristics to trace human settlements in the Udaypur region. From the smallest village to the largest city, one finds a consistent pattern in the relationship between depressions of land where water gets collected to form small lakes (talav) or ponds (talavadi) and the higher ground or mound (tekro) that is inhabited by people. This relationship between water and settlement has consistently dictated the organization and character of the built form. Often, the highest point of the mound is occupied by a temple that marks the center of the settlement, while the streets snake along the flow of water to drain it out from high ground into surrounding agricultural areas supporting the settlement. These manmade lines of the street connect with the natural flow and mark the movement of people and water through the landscape.
C02-GEOHER-028   p. 73   also Ch 7
A strong pattern and proximity can be observed in the location of water Kund suggesting that they were intentionally built on the top of hill to sustain the habitants of the fortress inside the boundary wall, prevalent in the region. Subsequently, these step wells which also served as resting places for guards and priests became magnets for life and incentivized the formation of settlements around them. At the same time, they acted as markers of an underground aquifer as their source was a hidden water table. The talav and Udaysagar became central pillars essential to this settlement pattern and bound them to the surface and depth of the earth.
C02-GEOHER-029   p. 76   also Ch 7
The material used for constructing the fort wall of Udaypur is sandstone, with differences in shape and size from region to region. Stone is specifically used, keeping in mind the wall is located on such a steep terrain that helps to let the storm/rain water run off without causing any damage to the fort walls.
C02-GEOHER-030   p. 97   also Ch 7
## Long-term historic changes in climatic variables of Betwa Basin, India — Shakti Suryavanshi, Ashish Pandey, Umesh Chandra Chaube, Nitin Joshi (BETWA-CLIM, 20)
Betwa Basin is located in Central India and lies between 77°10'E and 80°20'E (longitude) and 22°54' and 26°05'N (latitude) (Fig. 1). The total area of the basin is about 43,500 km\( ^{2} \) and the total length of the river from its origin to its confluence with the Yamuna River is 590 km, out of which 232 km lies in Madhya Pradesh and the rest 358 km in Uttar Pradesh.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-001   p. 4
For maximum temperature, five out of 12 stations showed a decreasing trend in monsoon season whereas almost all other stations showed an increasing trend in winter and no trend in summer season. For minimum temperature, only two stations of the basin showed a decreasing trend in monsoon and all other stations exhibited a significant increase in winter and summer season. The increase of winter temperature may adversely affect the
growth of Rabi crop (wheat and mustard) in the study area.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-002   p. 3
The long-term trend analysis showed several of the meteorological stations to exhibit a decreasing trend in annual and seasonal precipitation in the study area. Seasonal and yearly numbers of rainy days are decreased. However, onset of effective monsoon (except for Shivpuri and Tikamgarh stations) did not show any trend during the study period.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-003   p. 3
The climate of the Betwa basin is moderate, mostly dry except during the southwest monsoons (Chaube et al. 2011). The average annual rainfall varies from 700 to 1,200 mm with an average annual rainfall of 1,138 mm, the average annual evaporation losses are of the order of 1,830 mm, and the average annual runoff is about 13,430 million cubic meters (MCM), out of which nearly 80 % occurs in monsoon (Chaube 1988). The daily mean temperature ranges from a maximum of 42.3 °C to a minimum of 8.1 °C. The daily mean relative humidity varies from a minimum of 18 % (April and May) to a maximum of 90 % (August).
C02-BETWA-CLIM-004   p. 4
For seasonal analysis, the water year (i.e., 1 June to 31 May) was classified into three seasons, each of 4 months' duration. Season 1 corresponds to the monsoon season (June to September); season 2 corresponds to the winter season (October to January), and season 3 corresponds to the summer season (February to May).
C02-BETWA-CLIM-005   p. 11
It can be seen that, for season 1 (monsoon season), four stations (Basoda, Lalitpur, Sehore, Vidisha) exhibited a persistence whereas Berasia and Sehore exhibited a significantly decreasing trend with slope value of -1.69 and -5.46, respectively. However, Vidisha station exhibited an increasing trend with slope value of 2.79.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-006   p. 12
Based on the average value of MK test statistics Z for the basin, it can be concluded that no significant trend was observed in the summer, winter, monsoon and yearly rainfall in the basin.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-007   p. 12
For season 2 (i.e., winter rainy days), only Sironj station exhibited a significant persistence whereas, four stations (Bhopal, Hamirpur, Shivpuri and Tikamgarh) exhibited a significant decreasing trend. In the case of season 3 (summer rainy days), only Begumganj station exhibited a significant persistence whereas, no significant
trend was observed at any other station. In the case of yearly rainy days, Basoda station showed a significant persistence and Begumganj, Hamirpur, Tikamgarh, Shivpuri and Korwai exhibited a significant decreasing trend.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-008   p. 13
value of MK test statistics Z for the basin, it can be concluded that no significant trend was observed in the summer, winter, monsoon and yearly rain days in the basin.
OEM is an important agro-climatic variable for the planning of agricultural operations (land preparation, sowing, etc.) in
Kharif crops. OEM is defined on the basis of rainfall and evopotranspiration. Ashok Raj (1979) gave a criterion for estimation of the OEM. For OEM to occur, the following criteria should be satisfied: (1) first day rainfall in the 7-day spells should be more than evaporation of that particular day; (2) a day with more than 3 mm of rainfall is considered to be a rainy day; (3) total rainfall during the 7-day spell is more than (3× PET + 10) mm; and (4) at least 4 out of 7 days are rainy days.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-009   p. 14   also Ch 8
In the case of season 2, all stations except Chhatarpur and Hamirpur stations, showed a significant persistence whereas all the station showed a significant increasing trend. For season 3, none of the stations showed a significant persistence whereas, all the station showed a significant increasing trend. Like maximum temperature, the slope and relative change values for all the stations is quite low.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-010   p. 15
Based on the average value of MK test statistics Z for the basin, it can be concluded that a significant increasing trend is observed in summer and winter season minimum temperature in the basin.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-011   p. 15
In the case of season 2 (i.e., winter season), nine stations—Bhopal, Chhatarpur, Guna, Jhansi, Raisen, Vidisha, Sagar, Tikamgarh and Shivpuri—showed a significant persistence, and except for Jalaun station all 11 stations showed an increasing trend. In the case of season 3 (summer season), none of the stations showed a significant persistence and trend.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-012   p. 15
Based on the average value of MK
test statistics, it was observed that no significant trend was observed in rainy season and summer season maximum temperature, whereas an increasing trend was observed in the winter season maximum temperature in the basin.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-013   p. 15
However, Shivpuri and Tikamgarh stations showed a significant increasing trend with the slope values of 1.14 and 1.07, respectively (Fig. 4). Increasing trend in the OEM indicates that onset of monsoon in these two station is delayed. Based on the average value of MK test statistics for the basin, it can be concluded that no significant trend is observed in OEM in the basin.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-014   p. 15
Minimum temperature is found to be increased for all stations in summer season. It was also observed that for winter season both maximum and minimum temperatures are increasing for all stations. This temperature increase may lead to a significant change in the growth stages and water use of winter wheat crop.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-015   p. 17   also Ch 8
It was found that except for the summer rainfall, no trend was observed in seasonal and yearly rainfall. In the case of summer rainfall, a decreasing trend was observed for five stations. Decrease in rainfall may lead to low crop productivity in the Betwa river basin.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-016   p. 17
In the case of NRD, most of the stations do not show any significant trend however, few stations such as Tikamgarh, Begumganj, Khurai showed decreasing trend in NRD. For these stations, since there is no trend in rainfall but a decreasing trend in number of days was observed. This indicates that at these locations the average rainfall intensity is increasing. This increase in the average rainfall intensity would have a significant impact on run-off as well as on ground water recharge.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-017   p. 17
Betwa basin, though historically important, continues to be highly underdeveloped due to lack of irrigation facilities, the main stay of the people in the area, being agriculture. The area is prone to famines and droughts, particularly the upper and middle regions.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-018   p. 4
Effect of increase in the winter season temperature would be more significant for stations like Tikamgarh and Shivpuri where an increasing trend in OEM was observed. Since OEM is delayed, Kharif crop sowing period has to be delayed, and increasing trend in winter temperature would lead to increase in water demand. Therefore, additional water needs to be
supplied to meet the increasing demand. Delay in Kharif crop will also affect the Rabi crop.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-019   p. 17   also Ch 8
For OEM, only Tikamgarh and Shivpuri stations showed an increasing trend; increasing trend in OEM means that onset of monsoon is delayed, and this delay could significantly affect the planning of Kharif crop.
C02-BETWA-CLIM-020   p. 17   also Ch 8
## Streamflow of the Betwa River under the Combined Effect of LU-LC and Climate Change — Kumar, Amit; Singh, Raghvender Pratap; Dubey, Swatantra Kumar; Gaurav, Kumar (BETWA-FLOW, 15)
We observed that the combined effect of climate and LU-LC change resulted in the reduction in the mean monsoon stream flow of the Betwa River by 16% during 2001–2020 as compared to 1982–2000. In all four CMIP6 climatic scenarios (SSP126, SSP245, SSP370, and SSP585), the mean monsoon stream flow is expected to decrease by 39–47% and 31–47% during 2023–2060 and 2061–2100, respectively as compared to the observed time period 1982–2020. Furthermore, average monsoon rainfall in the catchment will decrease by 30–35% during 2023–2060 and 23–30% during 2061–2100 with respect to 1982–2020.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-001   p. 1
The Betwa River originates from Raisen district of Madhya Pradesh and joins the Yamuna River in Hamirpur district of Uttar Pradesh, India. The upstream of this river is largely intermittent and becomes perennial in the downstream (85 km) before its confluence to the Yamuna River  \( [21] \) . We have selected the upper reach of the Betwa River to conduct this study. The Upper Betwa flows through a single thread channel having a total length of 127 km and a catchment area of 9322 km \( ^{2} \) . It flows through semi-arid to dry sub-humid climatic regions in Central India  \( [21] \) .
C02-BETWA-FLOW-002   p. 2
Figure 9a illustrates the streamflow hydrograph of UBRC, which indicates that the rising limb begins in June that starts to fall after September. Figure 9b shows the spatial contribution of ISMR in the catchment. We observed that the entire UBRC receives more than 90% rainfall during the ISMR. The streamflow of the UBRC is relatively low in pre- and post-monsoon periods. It could be due to the limited contribution of sub-surface flow to the streamflow.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-003   p. 10
We observed that the mean streamflow has decreased by 16% during 2001–2020 compared to 1982–2000. The maximum and minimum streamflow during 1982–2000 is 1079 and  \( 139 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \) , which has decreased during 2001–2020 to 673 and  \( 127 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \)  respectively.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-004   p. 10
The annual average flow during 1982–2000 was  \( 312 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \) , which has decreased to  \( 281 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \)  during 2001–2020. We have analysed the seasonal variability of streamflow based on the SPI and SDI categorization. We observed that during the dry years, the mean monsoon streamflow of the Betwa River is  \( 371 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \) , and  \( 688 \, m^{3}s^{-1} \)  during the wet years.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-005   p. 12
- A transition from wetter to drier hydro-climatic conditions is evident in the upper Betwa River catchment, which affects the streamflow of the catchment.
- The combined effect of climate and LU-LC change has resulted in the mean monsoon streamflow of Betwa River to decrease by 16% during 2001–2020 as compared to 1982–2000.
- The mean monsoon streamflow is likely to decrease in all four future climate scenarios. It is projected to decrease by about 39–47% (2023-2060) and 31–41% (2061–2100) in the catchment with respect to the streamflow values observed during 1982–2020.
- Water scarcity in the catchment is likely to aggravate due to landuse modification and climate change under four different CMIP6 future climate scenarios.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-006   p. 13
The topography of the catchment varies from 376–669 m above mean sea level (Figure 1). The elevation is relatively high in the western part (451–669 m) of the catchment as compared to the eastern part (376–550 m).
C02-BETWA-FLOW-007   p. 2
We have selected the Upper reach of the Betwa River to simulate the stream flow under the projected climate and LU-LC change scenarios. This catchment has experienced a decrease in the mean monsoon rainfall, an increase in the extreme rainfall events, mean temperature, and LU-LC change  \( [7,20] \) . The frequency of meteorological dry events has increased after 2000 and is projected to intensify further during 2023–2060 and 2061–2100  \( [20] \) .
C02-BETWA-FLOW-009   p. 2
It has a very dynamic temperature and rainfall pattern. The catchment receives maximum temperature (40–45 °C) in the summer season (March to May) and minimum temperature up to 2 °C in the winter (November to February). During the monsoon (June to September), the catchment receives about 92% of its total rainfall.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-010   p. 2
Figure 8 shows that according to SPI from 2001–2020, the dry events have increased by 15% whereas wet and normal events have decreased by 4% and 11% respectively with respect to 1980–2000. At the same time, according to SDI, dry and wet events in the catchment have increased by 4%, whereas normal events have decreased by 9%.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-011   p. 9
The cumulative rainfall during the Indian summer monsoon shows a declining trend of 2 mm from 1980–2020 (Figure 6).
C02-BETWA-FLOW-012   p. 9
The simulation result suggests that the mean monsoon streamflow is likely to decrease in all four future climate scenarios. It is projected to decrease by about 39–47% (2023–2060) and 31–41% (2061–2100) in the catchment with respect to the … streamflow values observed during 1982–2020. From the same baseline period, the mean monsoon rainfall is expected to decrease by 30–35% during 2023–2060 and 23–30% during 2061–2100 (Figure 11).
C02-BETWA-FLOW-013   p. 10–11
During this period, agricultural land (76%) predominates in the UBRC, followed by forest (14%), urban (3%), open land (4%), and water body (2%). Figure 5 shows that from 1990–2020, the total area under agricultural land, built-up, and water bodies has increased by 3%, 2%, and 1%, … respectively, in the catchment. However, at the same time, forest and open land have diminished by 4% and 2%.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-014   p. 7–8   also Ch 8
Specifically, increase in agricultural land and urban area could be related to the rapid increase of population in the region. As per census data of India, the population in the catchment has increased by 60% from 1991 to 2011 (https://censusindia.gov.in/census.website/; accessed on 8 September 2021). In addition, increase in the area of water bodies can be attributed to the construction of dam in the catchment during 2011–2013.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-015   p. 8   also Ch 12
Under the current and projected circumstances, we expect agricultural water demand to increase in the catchment. Since agriculture in the catchment is primarily rain-fed  \( [50] \)  therefore, any deviation in the rainfall will eventually affect the yield of crops. Mall et al.  \( [52] \)  also found that the monsoon rainfall has a high correlation (0.8) with Kharif crop production anomalies.
C02-BETWA-FLOW-016   p. 12   also Ch 8

## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 1)
(ii) North-Eastern plateau—(Bhilsa district falling in line with the Bhopal territory): This is a narrow out-stretch of the great central plateau of Malwa made distinctive by the wooded ranges of the Vindhyas encircling it from east, north and south.
In ancient economy this tract had always had a significance of its own. With the fertile plateau of Malwa proper easily accessible towards the west and with its proximity to the fertile Gangetic plains of the east, the region about Bhilsa was always prosperous and flourishing a fact which is amply reflected in the richness of its monuments second to none in the whole of Madhya Bharat.

## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 1)
"It covered a valley which presents the most remarkable feature that, though it is so extensive, only two breaks occur in its wall of hills—one a little more than one hundred, the other about five hundred wide. Both of them were spanned by very remarkable dams, consisting of an earthen central band, faced on both sides, outer and inner, with immense blocks of stone, laid one on the other without mortar, but fitting so truly as to be water-tight, the other two faces sloping inwards from the base. The lesser opening was closed by a band 87 feet in height, and 300 ft. thick at the bases, or even more; the greater, by one in places 40 ft. high, and about 100 ft. broad on the top; and, though the first mentioned is now a complete wreck, the latter is intact and still continues to turn the river Kaliasot into the Betwa, and from its top the old bed of the stream is recognisable.

# Chapter 3. Before Udaypur: Prehistory, Early Vidisha and the Historical Landscape of Central India
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 64)
\( ^{1} \)  Chalcolithic tools, similar to those of Eran and Nagda were found in an ancient mound of the District These consist of black painted red and black and red grey wares belonging to 1000·700 B. C. A few microlithic blades of crystalline quarter were
also picked up from there. A few painted potteries of black and red and red polished ware were discovered just immediately over the chalcolithic layer, signifying the Aryan influence after the chalcolithic period.
C03-GAZ-001   p. 33
A few palaeolithic tools, including choppers, handaxes, cleavers, scrapers, bifaces, discs, etc., discovered in the valleys of the Betwa, Bina, Dhasan and Keothan give evidence of the palaeolithic culture which the
Prehistory district must have possessed.
C03-GAZ-002   p. 33
Excavations in the Kham Baba area brought to light remains of a huge temple, measuring, roughly 30 X 30 m.
During 1963–65 different spots around Vidisha were explored by the Archaeological Survey of India, Central Circle and a regular sequence of cultures from the Chalcolithic through early historical times upto the early Mediaeval period was found.
C03-GAZ-003   p. 333
Vidisha has a hoary past, with a rich archaeology and history. The archaeological wealth scattered all over the District, speaks of the glory that was Vidisha. It has been referred to in the Epics, the Puranas and in the Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical literature. The religious tolerance of the age is reflected here in the numerous Brahmanical, Buddhist and Jain remains, which coexisted. The Heliodoros Pillar or the Garuddhwaja at Besnagar, that was ancient Vidisha of the Sunga period, the Udayagiri caves of the Gupta period, the magnificent temples at Gyaraspur, Udayapur and Badoh symbolise the rich cultural heritage of this District.
C03-GAZ-004   p. 5   also Ch 7
This District is also closely associated with Emperor Asoka. It was at Vidisha that Asoka married Devi, a banker's daughter. Her son, Mahendra,
C03-GAZ-005   p. 5
The earliest reference of Vidisha is contained in the Ramayana by Valmiki. It is stated there that Shatrughana's son Shatrughati was placed in charge of Vidisha. The latter place has been mentioned in the Mahabharat.
The historicity of the ancient city of Besnagar, three kilometres from modern Vidisha and identified with ancient Vidisha, goes back to some centuries before the birth of Christ. Besnagar figures prominently in Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical literature in various forms such as Vessanagar, Vaisyanagar, etc. Tradition connects the town with Raja Rukmangada who, neglecting his own wife for the Apsara Visva, named the town Vishvanagar after her \( ^{3} \) .
C03-GAZ-006   p. 13
The Brihata Samhita also makes a mention of Vidisha.
On the destruction of Besnagar, located on the western side of the Betwa sometime after 7th century A.D., a new town sprang up on the eastern bank of the river. This new town was known as Bhailaswamin or Bhillaswamin, so named after the god sun, to whom a temple was dedicated here. The name of the place was later corrupted to Bhilsa, when Al Beruni visited the town in 11th century A.D.
C03-GAZ-007   p. 14
In Sanskrit literature eastern Malwa, including the Vidisha region, was referred to as Akara, that rose to a great height of cultural superiority in the Buddhist and the subsequent period. The brightest era, of course, lasted from Asoka's reign to the Imperial Guptas. Sanchi stood in symbolic relation to Buddhism. Equally interesting is the Heliodorus pillar at Besnagar which gives ample evidence of Brahmanical influence coexisting with Buddhism in this area.
C03-GAZ-008   p. 34
In fact, the magnificent Stupa of Sanchi was built largely by the donations from the business community of Vidisha, though by general belief Asoka erected the grand cupola.
C03-GAZ-009   p. 34
Recently a monastery type of building has been excavated near the Sanchi Stupa, which is stated to have been constructed for her residence.\(^{1}\) It is said that before sailing for Ceylon Mahendra came to visit his mother at Besnagar. The mother took her son to a “Chaitya giri” which, by popular belief, was none other than the Sanchi Stupa.
Recent excavation has revealed the possibility of a large scale fire in Vidisha which is attributed by some historians to Chandragupta Maurya. The reason is not known why he tried to destroy the whole town by a devastating fire.
C03-GAZ-010   p. 35
While on his way from Pataliputra to that place he met Devi, a banker's daughter of Vidisha or Besnagar of the Sakya clan and married her. Her son Mahendra, and daughter Sanghamitra are famous in history as their father's religious ambassadors to Ceylon. They are known to have carried a twig of the original Bodhi tree and led a Buddhist mission to that county. Devi never visited Pataliputra. She stayed at Besnagar and embraced Buddhism afterwards.
C03-GAZ-011   p. 35
Sometime during the weak rule of the former, Vidisha, like Tripuri, Mahishmti and many other contemporary towns, might have emerged as an independent City State, temporarily. The proof of this lies in the discovery of a few copper coins with signs of three Brahmi letters, read as Vedisa or Veddasa (Sanskrit Vidisa). The characters are similar to those of the inscriptions of Asoka. \( ^{2} \)  On the basis of stratigraphy and palaeography the coins may be dated to the 3rd and 2nd century B. C.
C03-GAZ-012   p. 35
As stated before, Pushyamitra came to power after slaying his weak master. The Sungas ruled originally as feudatories of the Mauryas at Vidisha. Pushyamitra tried to reinforce his position by marrying his son Agnimitra to Princess Malavika of Vidarbha.
C03-GAZ-013   p. 35
Actually Pushyamitra performed not one, but two Asvamedha sacrifices to herald the victory of Brahmanism over the Buddhist faith.\(^{2}\) One of the Asvamedha sacrifices was probably performed at Vidisha itself, as evidenced by the recent excavations\(^{3}\). Pushyamitra ruled for about 36 years (187-151 B.C.) and at the end of an eventful life left for his son Agnimitra a vast territory, including the cities of Pataliputra, Ayodhya, Vidisha and probably Jalandhar and Sakala.\(^{4}\) There is a reference in the Malavikagnimitram to the newly formed independent kingdom in the region of Vidarbha.
C03-GAZ-014   p. 36
During the Sunga regime Vidisha became the capital of Akara or eastern Malwa, and one of the most flourishing cities of India.
Agnimitra served as his father's Viceroy from his headquarters at Vidisha. It is significant to note that though the capital of the Sunga kings was at Pataliputra, still Pushyamitra and Agnimitra were called the rulers of Vidisha.\(^{1}\)
C03-GAZ-015   p. 36
The temple, mentioned above, must have thus existed in the same age.\(^{2}\) The discovery of an elliptical temple of Vishnu just on the site of the later Bhagavata temple supplies the earliest archaeological evidence of Vaishnava culture. This temple is believed to have been existing in Vidisha at least in the 3rd century B.C.\(^{3}\) Another inscription in early Brahmi characters, dated in the 12th year of King Bhagvata, has been discovered on a fragment of a stone pillar at Vidisha. It records the setting up of a flagstaff in honour of Lord Vishnu, in the temple at Vidisha, by a private individual named Gautamiputra.\(^{4}\)
C03-GAZ-016   p. 37
During his reign the Greek king Antialcidas of Taxila sent Dion's son Heliodorus, as his personal ambassador to the Sunga court at Vidisha. A part of this information is inscribed on a lofty Garuda Pillar, locally named as Khambaba. The pillar with the inscription was discovered near the village of Basnagar or Bes, that is ancient Vidisha. The epigraph records the erection of this pillar in the fourteenth year of king Bhagabhadra's reign by Heliodorus, who himself was a Vaishnava, as a Garuda-dhwaja to a temple of Lord Vasudeva.
C03-GAZ-017   p. 37   also Ch 6
Incidentally it may be mentioned that the earliest punch marked coins were also found in this region. By general belief the earliest coins of this type existed as early as in the 5th century B. C. At least the use of such coins can be established in Vidisha during the 4th century B. C.
C03-GAZ-018   p. 37
Sometime after the 7th century A. D. the old town of Besnagar, on the western side of the Betwa, seems to have been completely devastated by a tremendous deluge, or by some tragic political event. The population deserted the site of the old town and travelled southwards to settle on the eastern bank of the river. The city, thus founded, was known as Bhilsa.
C03-GAZ-019   p. 42
In olden times Vesnagar (Besnagar) was situated between the rivers Bes (Halali) and the Vetrawati (Betwa). Tradition connects the town with Raja Rukmangad, who was the ruler of the place. His son, Dharmaraj, was a famous educationist of his times.
C03-GAZ-020   p. 253
We find the glory of Vidisha described in the works of Kalidasa, Bhasa and Dandi. The hero of the drama Malavikagminitra, Agnimitra Sunga, was the ruler of Vidisha. Pushyamitra had performed Ashvamedha Yagnya at Vidisha. The coins discovered at this place illustrate that the ruler was a great patron of arts.
C03-GAZ-021   p. 254
In the main hall a beautiful statue of Kuber Yaksha, 12 feet in height, has been displayed. In the second hall and the two verandahs, the stone sculptures from first century B. C. to twelfth century A. D. have been displayed. In the third hall, the stone-age implements and the objects discovered during the excavations at Harappa, Mohenjodaro, Ujjain, Pawaya, Vidisha and Besnagar have been arranged.
C03-GAZ-022   p. 284
The Buddhist literature states that Prince Ashoka halted here for sometime on his way from Pataliputra to assume the charge of Viceroyary of the Ujjain Province. His associations with
the place were further sweetened by his marriage with Devi, a beautiful daughter of a local banker. Then the city appeals to have been a centre of business and wealth. The business community of Besnagar donated liberally for the erection of monuments at Sanchi and this fact is corroborated by the inscriptions or records engraved on various monuments at Sanchi giving out the names of donors from Wessanagara.
C03-GAZ-023   p. 326
The town of Bhilsa of the subsequent period is said to have been founded, and flourished at the cost of the old city of Besnagar, from where it received its material for its outer walls, built of square stones.
C03-GAZ-024   p. 326
It lies in the fork between the rivers, the Betwa and the Bes.
The village is generally identified with the ancient city of Wessanagara (Vaishyanagari) or Vidisha.
C03-GAZ-025   p. 326   also Ch 2

Each elephant is depicted as bearing a relic-casket. A tray of offerings is shown in the hand of each horseman.

The pillar has two parts viz., the shaft and the capital, each of which is a monolith.
