occur in the legends on these seals and these indicate that the sealing is connected with Yajnashala. The name of the data is given as Timitra which appears to be a Sanskritised form of the Greek Demetrius who instituted the sacrifice.
C03-GAZ-036   p. 332
One of the cuttings, located on the confluence of the rivers Bes
Excavations
1963-64 and
1964-65
and Betwa, revealed ruins belonging to six periods of habitation, ranging in chronology from prior to the Northern Black Polished ware to post-Gupta times.
C03-GAZ-037   p. 333
Its method of construction is criss-cross, that is the whole width of the wall had been constructed by making thin walls of about 1 m. thickness and further strengthened by still thinner cross walls, the hollow apartments having been packed with small rubble, brickbats and pebbles. By adopting this device the construction becomes less expensive and the wall stronger.
C03-GAZ-038   p. 334   also Ch 7
the fortification wall of the ancient site of Besnagar (Vidisha). It was constructed for the first time probably during the 2nd century B. C. as a huge palace wall, to serve as a fortification wall, as per the principles of ancient city planning.
C03-GAZ-039   p. 334
Coming down to the mythological and classical age we find a Puranic reference to Vidisha as a janapada. \( ^{3} \)  Another important reference is found in the pages of the Skanda Purana in which Vidisha appears as a holy place Classical History (tirtha), which should be visited after travelling to Someshwar.
As per Anguttara Nikaya Vidisha was included in the Avanti Janapada before the birth of the Buddha.
C03-GAZ-040   p. 33
The region around Vidisha was known in ancient time as Dasharna, with Vidisha or Besnagar as its capital. Kautilya the minister of Chandragupta Maurya, referred to this region as Dasharna and so also did Kalidasa. Dasharna (eastern Malwa) extended over the adjacent Sagar plateau, dissected by the deeply entrenched valleys of the river Dhasan and its headwaters.
In Brahmanical Buddhist and Jain literature Vidisha has been called by different names, viz., Vessanagar, Vaisvanagar, Visvanagar, Besnagar, Vaidisa, Vidisa, etc.
C03-GAZ-041   p. 34
According to the Puranic tradition the region was once under a branch of the Haihayas which was known as Pradyut dynasty, also called Dasharna.
C03-GAZ-042   p. 253
Vidisha is taken to be the north-eastern part of the Satavahana empire, that had to change its allegiance between these two powers quite often.
As has been stated earlier, that according to the puranas a Satavahana ruler killed the last Kanva ruler and became the master of the Magadhan empire including Malwa, in the first century B C.
C03-GAZ-043   p. 38
Inscriptions and coins of both the houses have been unearthed in Vidisha.
C03-GAZ-044   p. 38
Coins of Pulumayi and his successor were discovered from Vidisha proper. \( ^{5} \)  Probably they were the last known southern rulers of this region. Several Kshatrapa coins belonging to Viradaman, Rudrasena II, etc. found in Vidisha, suggest a prolonged Kshatrapa occupation in this tract after the Satavahanas.
C03-GAZ-045   p. 39
According to the puranas the Naga rulers flourished after the decline of the Kushanas at Vidisha. It has been surmised that the Naga house probably originated at Vidisha, from where they moved on to the north, establishing
C03-GAZ-046   p. 39
branches at Padmavati, Kantipuri and Mathura. \( ^{1} \)  Several copper coins of the Naga rulers have been brought to light in the excavations at Besnagar. One of these belongs to Ganapati and one to Bhima, the Naga rulers of Padmavati, indicating that the kings of Padmavati may have extended their influence over other Naga centres.
C03-GAZ-047   p. 40
According to the Allahabad Pillar inscription, Samudragupta vanquished two Naga rulers, Ganapatinaga and Nagasena alongwith the other rulers of Aryavartya, during his empire building conquests about the middle of the 4th century A.D. They were extirpated and crushed by him,\(^{5}\) and the region passed under the Gupta suzerainty. Samudragupta might have established friendly relations with the Naga rulers afterwards, tor the married his son Chandragupta II to a Nega princess, Kubernaga.\(^{6}\) A child of this union was Prabhavatigupta, who became the queen of Rudrasena II of the Vakataka house. Amongst the many tribes which Samudragupta subdued were the Sanakanikas and the Kakas, who are believed to have lived in the neighbourhood of Vidisha.\(^{7}\) Skandagupta seemed to have maintained cordial relations with them, for a Naga Chief, Sarva Naga was appointed a Vishyapati (provincial governor) over an Antarvedi district between the Ganges and the Yamuna and between Hardwar and Prayag in A.D.
C03-GAZ-048   p. 40
The puranas mention Vindhyasakti (c. A.D. 255-275), the founder of the dynasty, as a ruler of Vidisha. Vindhyasakti seems to have enlarged his patrimony, comprising a district or two in Vidarbha, and extended its limits across the Vindhya range, so as to include a portion of Malwa. This may perhaps explain why the puranas mention Vindhyasakti as the ruler of Vidisha.\( ^{4} \)
C03-GAZ-049   p. 40
Chandragupta II left undisputed evidence of his sovereignty in Malwa in the shape of two epigraphs at the caves of the Udayagiri hills of Vidisha. These record the gift made by the feudatory ruler Maharaja Sanakanika during the reign of Chandragupta II in 82 Gupta Era (A.D. 401); and the excavation of a cave by his minister of peace and war, Virasena, who came to this place with the king himself.
C03-GAZ-050   p. 41
The poet Kalidasa, who probably adorned the court of this monarch with 8 other jewels (raina), has immortalised the grandeur and beauty of Vidisha and its environs in his famous work Meghaduta.
C03-GAZ-051   p. 41
The early Kalachuris, who claimed their descent from the Haihaya king Kartaviryarjuna and grew powerful in northern Maharashtra, Gujarat and in parts of Malwa during the later part of the 6th century A. D. became the rulers of Vidisha. They used the Kalachuri Era. There are epigraphic records of three of these rulers, viz. Krishnaraja, his son Sankaragana and grandson Buddharaja. All of them were devotees of Siva.
C03-GAZ-052   p. 41
His Vadner grant dated K. E. 360 (A. D. 608) was issued from Vaidisa (Vidisha or Besnagar), in order to grant land in the Vatanagara bhoga (Vadner in the Chander taluk of Nasik district). It is not unlikely that East Malwa, of which Vidisha was the old capital, was conquered by one of these kings from Devagupta of the Later Gupta dynasty.
C03-GAZ-053   p. 41
But the Muslim traveller had admitted that the Hindus had always called it Bhilsa ever since its foundation Bhilsa is a corrupt form of Bhailasvamin or Bhailasvamo, that was originally the name of an idol of the Sun god, enshrined and worshipped in a great temple of this place. An inscription from Vidisha, incised during the rule of king Krishna (probably the Rashtrakuta monarch Krishna III of A. D. 939-68), refers to this temple of Bhillasvamin.
C03-GAZ-054   p. 42   also Ch 10
During the 10th century A. D., however, it was incorporated in the Chandella empire. It is said that the most powerful monarch of this family, namely, Dhanga (A. D. 954-1002) extended his kingdom upto the Jamuna (Kalandi) in the north, Gwalior (Gopa mountain) in the north-west and Bhasvat on the river Malwa (Bhilsa or Vidisha on the Betwa).
C03-GAZ-055   p. 42
\( ^{2} \)  This image is ascribed to the age of Chandragupta II (A. D. 5th century). Its size and height are such as to suit the abacus top of Kham Baba pillar. Probably this Gupta King, being a staunch devotee of Vishnu, crowned the column with this image of Garuda.
C03-GAZ-056   p. 329
It is in eight lines. It is dated in the year 106 of the Gupta Era, corresponding to A. D. 272.
C03-GAZ-057   p. 344
On the back wall of it there is an inscription of five lines,
indicating that the cave was made by a minister of Chandragupta. The roof inside is ornamented with a large lotus flower. The inscription informs that the minister accompanied the king and made this cave, dedicated to Shambhu or Siva. Unfortunately the inscription is not dated.
C03-GAZ-058   p. 345
Both these contain important historical inscriptions of the Gupta period. The cave No. 6
inscriptions bears inscription of Chandragupta II dated in the year 82 of the Gupta Era.
C03-GAZ-059   p. 345
It was a rich and prosperous trading centre in ancient period. The economic prosperity of this area was retained at least till the days of the Imperial Guptas, for in his Meghaduta, Kalidasa has referred to Vidisha as a place where everybody gets wealth to his heart's content. This economic prosperity was due to its advantageous position on the cross-roads of two important trade routes. One of these connected Mathura with Pratisthan (modern Paithan on the Godavari) via-Vidisha and another route, passing through this place, connected Pataliputra in the east with Bharukachchha (modern Broach), in the west.
C03-GAZ-060   p. 5
The economic prosperity was due to its advantageous position on the cross roads of two important trade routes. One of these ran from Pratisthan (modern Paithan in the Godavari region) to Mahishmati, Ujjayini, Gonadda, Vaidisa (Vidisha) and Kausambi, while the other connected Bharukachchha (Broach) and Surparak on the Arbian Sea, to Mathura via Ujjayini. A branch of this route ran from Vidisha to Kausambi through the valley of the Betwa, or Betravati and then to Pataliputra.\(^{1}\)
C03-GAZ-061   p. 34
Malwa, to which the present District of Vidisha belongs, had been a centre of culture and civilization for centuries. The roads that passed through Malwa were military as well as trade routes. The commercial activities of this region received an added impetus from these routes.
C03-GAZ-062   p. 154
In the ancient period, the District lay on the important road link connecting Northern India with Southern India and the Magadha empire with part of Surparaka. The earliest Buddhist Literature records the trade route of Paithana in the Deccan to Sravasthi, \( ^{1} \) passing through the District.
C03-GAZ-063   p. 170
The Skanda Purana refers to Vidisha as a tirth (holy place) which should be visited after visiting Someshwar.
C03-GAZ-064   p. 253
## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 27)
The Haihayas later separated into a number of branches one of which ruled at Vidisha (Bhilsa) which in Central India is next only to Mahishmati inthe
point of its high antiquity.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 26–27
Prince Ashoka had married a daughter of a banker of Vidisha from whom were born prince Mahendra and princess Sanghamitra so famous in the history of early Buddhism.
The services rendered by Ashoka to the cause and spread of Buddhism need hardly be mentioned. He laid the foundations of the great Stupa at Sanchi and raised a huge monolithic pillar close to it with his usual edicts inscribed thereon.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 28
We learn from Kalidasa's literary romance: Malavikagnimitra that the crown prince Agnimitra served as a Viceroy at Vidisha during the reign of his father Pushyamitra. That Vidisha was within Shunga dominion is further supported by the Besnagar Pillar inscription of Heliodoros. It is a Garuda pillar set up by him during his stay there. He is mentioned therein as having come as the ambassador of the Indo-Greek King Antialcidas of Taxila to the court of King Bhagabhadra. This Bhagabhadra is generally identified with the fifth Shunga king Bhandaka of the later Shungas who are supposed to have made Vidisha their capital.
It is interesting to find from the inscription that the Greek ambassador had embraced the Bhagavata sect and raised the pillar in honour of the god Vasudeva, an event of much significance to the history of the Bhagavata sect in India.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-003   p. 28
It seems that in the midst of the rapid spread of Buddhism in the country Vidisha was holding out as a centre of the orthodox faith as testified not only by the inscription but by a number of other antiquities found here belonging to the succeeding centuries. In any case it is quite clear that the Vidisha of the Shungas, and of the succeeding dynasties, was a centre of a great cultural and art movement now represented by the surviving monuments in the neighbourhood of Bhilsa and Sanchi which rank high amongst the most remarkable and extensive of the remains to be found in the whole of India.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-004   p. 28
*More than a dozen images of the Saptamatrikas. Durga, Kubera, etc., have been recovered from Besnagar which would seem to belong to the age of the Nagas rather than the Guptas to which they have so far been assigned.
†A chief of this tribe mentioned as Maharaja was ruling as a vassal of Chandragupta II near Udyagiri (Bhilsa) where he caused a cave to be excavated.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 31
The antiquities from Besnagar are most important for the early history of the Hindu sects of Vaishnavism and Shaivism. The colossal image of Durga, the sculptures of the Seven Mothers, Nrisimha, Vishnu and the Ekamukha lingas are the instances in point, in addition to the stone figures of Kubera and other antiquities.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-006   p. 32
It is possible that just at this time a Vaishnava temple existed at Besnagar, in front of which a Greek ambassador erected the famous Garudadhvaja still standing in situ. But nothing has survived to show how the earliest Hindu sanctuaries, if any, were in those days.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-007   p. 45   also Ch 5
There is, however, a group of antiquities, viz. the free standing colossal statues, with regard to the age of which there is a divergence of opinions assigning them from the pre-Mauryan to the Shunga times. For such statues the ancient site of Besnagar may take the place of pride in yielding, up-to-date, three exactly similar figures of Yakshini* and, what may be considered to be, the most unique and gainly figure of Kubera recovered from the river Betwa a few months back.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-008   p. 52   also Ch 2
The latter site has also yielded a little less than a dozen statues of the Seven Mothers, Durga, Nrisimha, Kubera, etc., all free-standing and carved on four sides, which artistically should be taken to form a joining link between the earlier art and the classical art of the Gupta period. In the matters of form, volume and expression they somewhat differ from their earlier counterparts, bringing them much closer to the classic art of the later age.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-009   p. 53
It will be seen from above that from the view-point of antiquity and historical importance Bhilsa is the richest district in Madhya Bharat. The monuments here are mostly centred round the ancient city site of Vidisha, i. e., at Besnagar and Udaygiri, a mile to the west.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-010   p. 69
Though Vidisha was a great centre of the orthodox religion, under the Shungas and later, the popularity of the new faith is amply reflected in the monuments of Sanchi and Besnagar, as at Ujjain and Kasrawad. There were, however, no great monarchs ruling in Madhya Bharat who had special attachment for the faith with the result that little of recorded or literary evidence is available to attest to the extent and popularity of Buddhism then in Madhya Bharat. Under the Guptas, it appears, Vidisha was a stronghold of Brahminism and at other places also Buddhism was losing ground.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-011   p. 71
in Madhya Bharat is found in the stone images of Durga and of the Seven Mothers and the Ekamukha Lingas from Besnagar now in the Gwalior Museum. They have been dated to the early Gupta period of the 4th century A.D. though as observed elsewhere their date can be pushed back to a century.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-012   p. 72   also Ch 8
Vaishnavism.—Archaeologically Vaishnavism is the earliest recorded sect in Madhya Bharat as is evident from the famous Heliodoros pillar inscription of 1st or 2nd century B.C. at Besnagar near Bhilsa. The inscription states that a Greek ambassador from Taxila at the court of the Shunga King of Vidisha (Bhilsa) had become a convert to the Bhagavata sect and raised a Garuda pillar in honour of the god Vasudeva.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-013   p. 73   also Ch 8
Bhilsa is the modern representative of the ancient Vidisha which stood on the other side of the river Betwa 2 miles to the north. The name of Bhilsa is not directly related to the ancient name of Vidisha; it seems to have been derived from a great temple of the sun god called as Bhailasvamin and an incidental reference to the place as Mahabalistan is made by Albiruni in the early 11th century A. D.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-014   p. 111   also Ch 2
Site of Besnagar.—The site of the famous ancient city, mentioned as Vessanagar in the Pali books of the Buddhists and by the name of Vidisha in the Sanskrit literature of the Hindus, is about two miles to the west of the Bhilsa Railway Station and
is situated in the fork of the two rivers, the Betwa (Vetravati) and the Bes. In the days of the Buddha Vidisha was a great centre of trade connecting the flourishing towns of the Gangetic plains with the western coast and the Deccan. It came more into prominence in the time of Ashoka, the Mauryan Emperor, who had married a daughter of a merchant from the town. Under the Shungas it was a great religious centre, for Hindus and Buddhists alike, as is amply reflected in the famous ruins of Sanchi close by and in the numerous Hindu antiquities found from its old site.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-015   p. 112–113   also Ch 2
Of them the three free-standing statues of Yakshinis, a colossal statue of Kubera, the images of Durga, Seven Mothers, Nrisimha, etc., are the most interesting, most of them being now preserved in the Museum at Gwalior. On the site itself now stands a monolithic pillar known in the locality by the name of Kham Baba, "the Pillar God". The pillar bears a Brahmi inscription stating that it was set up as Garuda pillar in honour of God Vasudeva (Vishnu) by a Greek named Heliodoros who had come to the court of King Bhagabhadra of Vidisha as an ambassador from the Greek king Antialcidas of Takshashila (Taxila) in Western Punjab and who has styled himself as a Bhagavata, having been a convert to Hinduism.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-016   p. 113
Till the age of the Guptas (5th century A. D.) it was a prosperous town but what happened to it afterwards is not known; for by the 10th century the town of Bhilsa, on the other side of the river, had already came into existence, the older town having been almost totally deserted by then.
The site of the ancient town is now marked by mounds which have from time to time yielded numerous stone or other antiquities of historical or artistic importance.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-017   p. 113
In ancient geography it was called as Dasharna known as such from the days of the Buddha to those of Kalidasa in about the 5th century. A. D. The name still lives in the modern name of a stream called Dashan which, rising in the Bhopal territory and flowing through Bundelkhand, empties into the river Betwa. In late historical times it came to be known as part of the Gondwada ruled by the semi-civilized tribe of the Gonds. The ancient Vidisha and its later representative Bhilsa had always been the focal point of this region.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-018   p. 19   also Ch 2
According to his Nasik prashasti, territories which he conquered from Nahapana included Anupa (the country on the Narbada about Nimar), Akara (east Malwa) and Avanti (west Malwa).
C03-MADHYABHARAT-019   p. 30
According to a passage in Puranas, they were a people known as Andhras led by the then ruler Simuka, generally identified with the Simuka Satavahana of the Nanaghat inscription. Simuka, in the course of his northern campaigns when he extirpated the last Shunga and Kanvas, must have conquered districts round Vidisha. For, besides the Puranic passage, discovery of certain early Satavahana coins suggests that the Malwa region was within early Satavahana influence.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-020   p. 29
While the southern parts of Madhya Bharat were thus undergoing rapid political changes the regions about Gwalior and Bhilsa were under a comparably more stable government of the Nagas ruling in small principalities from Kantipui (according to Cunningham Kotwal 20 miles north of Gwalior) Padmavati (Pawaya, 40 miles south of Gwalior) and Vidisha (Bhilsa). Numerous copper cast coins of the Nagas have been found at Kotwal and Pawaya which testify to this.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-021   p. 30
In the north about Gwalior the authority of the Nagas was supplanted by the newly rising power of the Guptas and as stated in his famous Allahabad Pillar Inscription Samudragupta finally vanquished them sometime in the middle of the 4th century A. D. The inscription also claims victory over a number of ruling tribes and dynasties in Northern India such as the Malavas mentioned above and the Sanakanikas† of the region near Bhilsa. It seems therefore fairly clear that under the Guptas the whole of Madhya Bharat was once more under one sceptre with an assured peace and a stable government in power.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-022   p. 31
The prosperity of Madhya Bharat territories under the Guptas is well reflected in the few monuments left at Pawaya, Tumain (Guna District), Besnagar and Udaygiri (Bhilsa), Mandsor and in the famous caves and paintings of Bagh. The antiquities recovered from the sites of Padmavati (Pawaya) and Vidisha (Besnagar) are unique in the whole range of the classic art of the “Golden Age” of the Guptas.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-023   p. 32
The plain of Nimar and eastern part of the plateau near Bhilsa were, however, for a time under the rule of the Rastrakutas* of the
*The Pathari (Bhilsa District) Pillar inscription refers to the erection of the pillar by a Minister of Rastrakuta king Parabala in 861 A. D.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-024   p. 33
The caves at Udaygiri.—There are in all 20 caves excavated on the eastern face of the Udaygiri hill. The caves are mostly small rock-cut chambers enshrining idols or images of divinities carved into the rock of the hill, Caves Nos. 1 and 20 are Jain caves. Architecturally cave No. 1 is a little interesting in so far as it represents the earliest phase in the development of temple architecture in India, planned as it is with a small
shrine and a simple porch in front.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-025   p. 113–114   also Ch 5
Of the other caves, the most notable are the caves Nos. 6 and 7 containing important historical inscriptions of the Gupta period, and No. 13 for the large rock-cut image of Sheshashayi Vishnu in it.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-026   p. 114
Sarais, Bridges and Dharmashalas.—Being placed in the central parts of the country the territory of Madhya Bharat must have been called upon to respond to the normal needs of trade and commerce by maintaining an effective line of communications consistent with the conditions of the times. The great trade routes connecting the northern and southern parts of the country inevitably passed through this region. In ancient times Vidisha (Bhilsa), Ujjain and Mahishmati formed the important links joining the western coast, the Gangetic plains and the Deccan.
C03-MADHYABHARAT-027   p. 80
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 9)
विदिशा का ज्ञात इतिहास ढाई हजार साल का है। एक लंबी समृद्ध सांस्कृतिक परंपरा है, लेकिन आज के समाज में उसका लेशमात्र नहीं है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The known history of Vidisha is two and a half thousand years old. It has a long, rich cultural tradition, but today's society retains not a trace of it.
C03-JAGTA-HI-001   p. 23
अब हम उदयपुर के जिला मुख्यालय पर जा पहुँचे थे, जो ऐतिहासिक रूप से उदयपुर से भी काफी पहले से समृद्ध प्रमुख नगर रहा है। विदिशा का अतीत सम्राट अशोक तक तो सीधा ही ले जाता है, जिनका विवाह यहीं की एक कन्या से हुआ था। 23 सौ साल पहले।
*Gloss [my translation]:* We had reached the district headquarters, historically a prosperous major city much earlier than Udaipur. Vidisha's past leads straight to Emperor Ashoka, who married a girl from here, 2300 years ago.
C03-JAGTA-HI-002   p. 57
विदिशा उदयपुर की तुलना में बड़ा और अधिक प्राचीन शहर था, जिसका ज्ञात इतिहास ही मौर्ष्य तक तो बड़े आराम से ले जाता है। मौर्ष्य साम्राज्य में अशोक सम्राट बनने के पहले उज्जैन में रहे थे। विदिशा के पास सांची के स्तूप तभी बनना शुरू हुए थे। कालिदास के मेघदूत विदिशा की आकाशीय यात्रा करते हुए दिखाई दिए हैं। बीच में गुप्त काल गुजरा, जिसके प्रमाण इस इलाके में जगह-जगह बिखरे हुए हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Vidisha was a bigger and more ancient city than Udaipur, whose known history goes back easily to the Mauryas. Ashoka lived in Ujjain before becoming emperor. The Sanchi stupas near Vidisha began to be built then. Kalidasa's clouds travel through Vidisha in the Meghaduta. Then came the Gupta period, whose evidence is scattered around the region.
C03-JAGTA-HI-003   p. 57
यह एक ऐसे शहर की यादगार यात्रा थी, जहां दो हज़ार साल पहले एक यूनानी राजदूत आया था। उसने विदिशा आकर यहां के समाज और रीति-रिवाजों के असर में वैष्णव धर्म स्वीकार करने का निर्णय ले लिया था। वह इतिहास में दर्ज विदिशा के लिए गर्व करने लायक एक अमर प्रसंग है, जिसकी याद बेतवा नदी के किनारे पत्थर के एक स्तंभ में दर्ज है। वह स्तंभ खामबाबा के नाम से प्रसिद्ध है, जो कभी एक मंदिर के सामने गढ़ स्तंभ की शक्ल में स्थापित किया गया होगा। आज मंदिर का नामोनिशान बाकी नहीं है। लेकिन हेलिओडोरस का वह लेख आज भी उस स्तंभ पर पढ़ा जा सकता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It was a memorable visit to a city where two thousand years ago a Greek ambassador came. Moved by the society and customs, he decided to accept Vaishnavism. It is an immortal episode recorded on a stone pillar on the Betwa, famed as Khambaba, once a garuda pillar before a temple. No trace of the temple remains, but Heliodorus's inscription can still be read on the pillar.
C03-JAGTA-HI-004   p. 58
जब वह पहली बार यहां आया था तब मगध सम्राट अशोक और पुण्यमित्र शुंग के किस्से बहुत ताजे थे। सांची में स्तूपों का निर्माण जारी था। महेंद्र और संघमित्रा श्रीलंका प्रस्थान के पहले अपनी माता से मिलने आए थे। शुंगों की यज्ञवेदियों के धुएं की सुगंध हवाओं को ताजादम बनाए हुए थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* When he first came, the stories of Magadha's Emperor Ashoka and Pushyamitra Shunga were very fresh. Stupas were still being built at Sanchi. Mahendra and Sanghamitra had come to meet their mother before leaving for Sri Lanka. The fragrance of the smoke of the Shungas' sacrificial altars freshened the air.
C03-JAGTA-HI-005   p. 59
वह भी विदिशा को दिल दे बैठा। इतना कि उसने अपना धर्म ही बदल लिया। इसकी विधिवत घोषणा एक बड़े स्तंभ पर अंकित करा दी थी ताकि इतिहास को भलीभांति पता रहे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* He gave his heart to Vidisha so much that he changed his religion. He had it formally announced inscribed on a large pillar so that history would know it well.
C03-JAGTA-HI-006   p. 59
आज का मालवा ईसा पूर्व की सदियों में दशाणर्ण और अवन्ति दो नामों से जाना जाता था। यह विशाल क्षेत्र सम्मिलित तौर पर अशोक के अधीन था। बाद में इसे मालवा कहा जाने लगा। विदिशा पूर्वी मालवा का केन्द्र था और उज्जयिनी पश्चिमी मालवा का।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Today's Malwa was known in the centuries BCE by two names, Dasharna and Avanti. This vast region was jointly under Ashoka. Later it came to be called Malwa. Vidisha was the centre of eastern Malwa and Ujjayini of western Malwa.
C03-JAGTA-HI-007   p. 163
उस विशाल चट्टान पर विष्णु को पहचानकर उसे कुछ राहत महसूस हुई। विष्णु अपनी भव्य मुद्रा में कमर पर हाथ रखकर वाराह अवतार में शान से खड़े हैं। पृथ्वी स्त्री के रूप में उनकी दाढ़ पर है। विष्णु ने प्रलय में डूबी पृथ्वी का उद्धार किया है। अपने आराध्य विष्णु का यह आशाजनक दृश्य वह अपलक देख रहा है। उदयगिरि में पत्थर पर अंकित 1500 साल पुरानी गुप्त सम्राटों की पवित्र निशानी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Recognising Vishnu on that huge rock gave him relief. Vishnu stands proudly in his boar incarnation, hand on waist, Earth as a woman on his tusk; Vishnu has rescued the earth sunk in the deluge. A 1500-year-old sacred sign of the Gupta emperors in stone at Udayagiri.
C03-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 61
एक बार मेरा जाना एक ऐसी जगह पर हुआ, जो पटारी की तरफ उदयपुर से करीब 15 किलोमीटर एक सुरम्य हरी-भरी पहाड़ियों में है। अगर आप गूगल मैप पर उदयपुर को पटारी से जोड़ेंगे तो ठीक बीच में यह जगह है। पटारी के खंडहरों से साफ पता चलता है कि यह मौय्यों और गुप्त राजाओं के समय एक विकसित नगर रहा होगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Once I went to a place about 15 km from Udaypur towards Patari, in picturesque green hills. If you join Udaypur and Patari on Google Maps, this place lies right in the middle. The ruins of Patari clearly show that it must have been a developed town in the time of the Mauryas and Gupta kings.

## Corpus Inscriptionum Indicarum, Vol. VII, Part 2: Inscriptions of the Paramāras — Trivedi, Harihar Vithal (ed.) (CII, 9)
It is interesting to note that the earliest mention of this name is in an inscription of V.S. 935 or 878 A.C. and also that the name figures as Bhillasvāmipura in the Māndhātā inscription of Jayasinha II issued in (V.) S. 1331.
C03-CII-001   p. 236
Bhāillasvāmippura (l. 9) is the ancient city of Vidishā (Bhilsā), which was formerly the capital of the Akara-janapada, roughly corresponding to east Mālava. The name Bhillasvāmin (Bhāillasāmin) was originally of an image of the Sun deity worshipped in a temple at that place which subsequently became famous under the deity's name, as we also know from Albērūnī (c. 1030 A.C.) who tells us that “the name of the town is identical with that of the idol worshipped there”.
C03-CII-004   p. 236   also Ch 8
Now, in view of the fact that the Chandēlla territory then extended up to Vidishā in the south-west, the principality of the ruler who is stated to have been placed on the throne by Vāchaspati should be looked for still further in the south or south-west of Vidishā.
C03-CII-005   p. 24
This suggestion is confirmed by Tale No. 8 of the S.M.K., which states that Pūrṇapathaka-pattana came on the way of a traveller from Vidishā to Mānyakheta (p. 62).
C03-CII-006   p. 53
Sircar, who was then the Government Epigraphist for India, happened to inspect the stone and realised the importance of the inscription which, as found by him, was devoted neither to a distinguished personage, nor to a king, but to eulogise the Sun-god: and his conclusion that the inscribed stone slab originally belonged to the temple of Bhāilla- or Bhāila-svāmin at the place appears to be quite sound.
C03-CII-007   p. 195   also Ch 6
The name Bhilsā still retains the memory of the Sun-deity, a temple dedicated to whom existed there some time back.
C03-CII-008   p. 195
The immediate object of the inscription appears to be to record the construction of a temple of Murāri, probably by king Trailōkyavarman himself or by one of his subordinates, and making some donations to it by him. The date of the record, as given in figures only in the end, is the twelfth tithi of the dark half of Chaitra of the (Vikrama) era 1226.
C03-CII-009   p. 224   also Ch 4
THIS inscription is incised on a rectangular stone slab which is now set up above the door-part of a house in front of the Jaina temple at Vidishā, the principal town of a district in Madhya Pradesh.
C03-CII-010   p. 224   also Ch 7
The following three stanzas (4-6) inform us that he (probably the king himself) constructed a temple of Murārī in the boar-incarnation, a temple which was very high and as lustrous as the moon (kumuda-bandhu), and also installed in it images of some other deities with their respective weapons. The place where the temple was constructed is not mentioned in this stanza or the name of the place may have been lost in it: but evidently it was the town of Vidishā itself, as we know from the mention of the river Vētravatī occurring in the next stanza, and more so when we take into account that such a massive stone could not have been brought from a far off place.¹ The seventh stanza again refers to the same person who was the constructor of the temple and states that he endowed it with a garden;² and the following three stanzas contain a poetic description of the temple.
C03-CII-011   p. 225   also Ch 7
## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 8)
Of Ujjayini and Vidisha, indeed, countless mentions are available in ancient literature. In the various Puranas, the Mahabharata, Buddhist literature, the vast Jain literature, various epics, many prose works, champu works, ancient inscriptions and short lyric poems,
King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 21
boundless description of these towns is available. But a systematic description is found for the first time only in Banabhatta's *Kadambari*.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 21–22
Vidisha is extremely famous and popular in Indian history and tradition, in poetry and in archaeology. Its mentions are found in abundance in the Ramayana and Mahabharata, in the Puranas and belles-lettres, and in Buddhist and Jain literature. Kalidasa displayed special interest in describing Vidisha — in his *Meghaduta* and *Malavikagnimitra*. The whole play *Malavikagnimitra* tells the story of the Shunga-era palaces of Vidisha itself.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 41
From being on the bank of the Vidisha (Bes) river, it was called Vaidisha-nagara, whose corrupted form is called Besnagar today.
There was bustle in these settlements until the close of the Gupta age. Afterwards the centre of the settlement began to shift toward the north of the Betwa, where the settlement of Vidisha is today.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-003   p. 43   also Ch 2
The land of Malava, bounded "Chambal on this side, Betwa on that," is endowed with ancient cultural glory. Even before the Malavas, this land and its towns kept attaining the glory of a capital.¹ Vidisha had already become a capital in the age of the Ramayana itself.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 51   also Ch 2
The ancient name of Bhailasvamipur was Vidisha. In the Ramayana, Mahabharata, various Puranas, the various versions of the *Brihatkatha* and the like, and in the Jataka stories and the vast Jain literature, countless mentions of Vidisha are found. The field of action of Kalidasa's play *Malavikagnimitra* is Vidisha itself, which is described as the capital of the Shungas. In the *Meghaduta* too it is called a "capital," and the pleasure-caves here are described.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 61
For instance, Patanjali's residence Gonarda has now been identified as Godarmau near Bhopal. From Buddhist literature it is clear that this site was a trading centre situated between Vidisha and Ujjayini.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 21
Bhailasvamidevapura kept the cultural tradition of ancient Vidisha unbroken from the 9th to the 12th century. It was a prosperous centre of the Jain and Vedic religions, and many beautiful and vast temples had been built there, which, according to Muslim historians, invaders such as Iltutmish and Alauddin Khilji later razed to the ground.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 43
Vidisha was in truth a town of Vaishyas (merchants), because it was situated on the trade-route (*sartha-patha*) running from Shravasti through Kaushambi to Avanti, and on the routes going thence toward Mathura and toward Vidarbha. Consequently, in ancient times it became a chief centre of trade. This is why there was a vast settlement of wealthy Vaishyas there, which was called Veshnagar.
C03-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 43
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 7)
Vidisha was a larger and more ancient city than Udaipur, whose known history comfortably takes [one] up to the Mauryas. In the Maurya empire, before becoming emperor, Ashoka had lived in Ujjain. The stupas of Sanchi, near Vidisha, had begun to be built just then. Kalidasa's Meghadutas (cloud-messengers) are seen making the aerial journey of Vidisha. In between, the Gupta period passed, the proofs of which are scattered here and there in this region.
C03-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 58 (Hindi original); also at ≈ p. 58 (Hindi original)
This was a memorable journey of such a city where, two thousand years ago, a Greek ambassador had come. He, coming to Vidisha, had decided — under the influence of the society and customs here — to accept the Vaishnava religion. That is an immortal episode, worthy of pride for the Vidisha recorded in history, the memory of which is recorded in a stone pillar on the bank of the Betwa river. That pillar is famous by the name of Khamba Baba, which must once have been erected, in the form of a flag-pillar (*garud-stambha*), in front of a temple. Today no trace of the temple remains. But that inscription of Heliodorus can still be read on that pillar today.
C03-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 59 (Hindi original)   also Ch 2
He too lost his heart to Vidisha — so much that he changed his very religion. He had the formal declaration of it inscribed upon a large pillar, so that history might know it well. Seeing that pillar on the banks of the Vetravati (Betwa), that whole era revolved in Heliodorus's eyes — when his glittering chariots had reached here via Mathura.
C03-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 60 (Hindi original)   also Ch 2
When he had come here for the first time, the tales of the Magadha emperor Ashoka and of Pushyamitra Shunga were very fresh. The construction of the stupas at Sanchi was going on.
C03-JAGTA-EN-004   ≈ p. 60 (Hindi original)
Today's Malwa, in the centuries before Christ, was known by two names — Dasharna and Avanti. This vast region was, collectively, under Ashoka. Later it came to be called Malwa. Vidisha was the centre of eastern Malwa, and Ujjayini of western Malwa.
- Udaipur, about seventy kilometres from Vidisha, cannot even be called a town (*kasba*) today. A sleeping sort of village, that awaits the moment of waking from its past… A thousand years ago from today, when it was in its prime *[the source reads "सुमावस्था," apparently for "सु-अवस्था" — good/flourishing state]*, its splendour would have been that of the temple-city of the ancient Dasharna kingdom.
C03-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 163 (Hindi original)   also Ch 12
Vishnu stands, in his majestic posture, hand on hip, splendidly, in the Varaha (boar) incarnation. The Earth, in the form of a woman, is on his tusk. Vishnu has rescued the Earth drowned in the deluge. He is gazing, unblinking, at this hope-giving scene of his adored Vishnu. In Udayagiri, the 1500-year-old sacred sign of the Gupta emperors, inscribed on stone. The episode of Chandragupta and Dhruvaswamini has come alive.
C03-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 62 (Hindi original)
Once I happened to visit a place that lies about 15 kilometres from Udaipur, toward Patari, amid scenic, verdant hills. If you join Udaipur to Patari on Google Maps, this place is exactly in the middle. From the ruins of Patari it is plainly evident that it must have been a developed town in the time of the Mauryas and the Gupta kings.
C03-JAGTA-EN-008   ≈ p. 154 (Hindi original)
## Temples of India (TEMPLES-IND, 3)
Two other temples of a comparable date existed, one dedicated to Sankarshana and Vasudeva at Nagari (ancient Madhyamika) in Udaipur District, Rajasthan and the other, also a Bhagavata (Vaishnava) shrine, at Besnagar near Sanchi in Central India. Both of them were elliptical on plan and were possibly made of timber, but their remains are too scrappy to yield an idea of their elevation and design. In front of the Besnagar shrine stands the famous stone pillar recording that this Garuda-standard was set up … by the Greek ambassador Heliodorus who proudly called himself a Bhagavata (Vaishnava).
C03-TEMPLES-IND-001   p. 17–18
Small, flat-roofed shrines comprising a tri-ratha sanctum and a portico with two pillars in front are found in Central India, continuing the tradition of the early Gupta temple form. More than a dozen of them have been recently discovered at the sites of Ramgarh, Chhapara and Badoh in District Vidisha, near Sanchi, in addition to a few from the Lalitpur area of District Jhansi. These are unpretentious temples with a simple podium, a wall of large slabs, and a flat monolithic slab-roof each for the sanctum and the portico, the latter being slightly lower. Most of them are smaller than the earliest Gupta temples at Sanchi and Tigawa and are generally simpler in construction, though they have a more ornate door-frame and pillars,
C03-TEMPLES-IND-004   p. 30
The above temples are followed by the Jarai Math at Barwasagar (District Jhansi), and Gadarimal Temple at Badoh (District Vidisha).
C03-TEMPLES-IND-006   p. 40
## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 2)
Lithic in its construction and not only in its substance is the solid stone fence unearthed in Besnagar, Bhopal. It belonged to an “Uttama Prāsāda” of Vāsudeva—only its brick foundation exists—of which also the Garudā stone pillar with the inscription of Heliodoros, son of Dion and an inhabitant of Taxila, formed part (see also ASIAR, 1913-14, Pt. I.). These relics date from the 2nd century B.C.
C03-KRAM-1-001   p. 120   also Ch 5
The earliest preserved dry masonry is the stone facing of Stūpa I, Sañcī; the earliest occurrence of lime mortar in historical times is in the brick foundation of the Viṣṇu temple at Besnagar (ASIAR, 1913-14).
C03-KRAM-1-002   p. 132
## The Skanda-Purāṇa, Part XIII (SKANDA, 2)
It was due to the service to Khaṇḍeśvara that a maidservant and her husband of Vaishya community of Vidiśā were born as Queen Kāntimati and King Bhadrāśva.
C03-SKANDA-001   p. 110
This your queen was a maidservant of a Vaisya named Haridatta in the city of Vaidisā. You were her husband. As a result of the incomplete Vrata you became a servant. That Vaisya went to Mahākāla and worshipped Lord Maheśvara duly with splendid scents, flowers, etc. After worshipping, he returned home where both of you were the supervisors.
C03-SKANDA-002   p. 111
## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 1)
Early in the 5th century, at Udayagiri near Vidisha (MP) and at Ramgarh in the same region, the Gupta rulers initiated the creation of Brahminical or Hindu cave temples, along with sculpted panels establishing long-lasting formulations for Hindu iconography.

# Chapter 4. The Paramāras and the Foundation of Udaypur: Kingship, Patronage and Urban Formation
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 55)
> In the western direction there is a great mountain named Arbund, son of Himagiri — a place of the *siddhi* of accomplished couples (*siddha-dampati*), and one that grants the desired fruit to the wise; where Vishwamitra forcibly seized the cow of Vashishtha, and then, by the power of that [Vashishtha], a hero arose out of the fire-pit (*agnikund*) who, single-handed, destroyed the enemy army.
C04-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 140 (Hindi original)
> Having slain the enemies, he brought the cow back; then the sage said — "You shall be a king by the name of Paramar." (6)
C04-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> In that dynasty there arose an Upendraraja (king) whose fame is sung by the gods along with the whole assembly of *yajnas*, who is like a jewel among the community of the twice-born (*dvija*), and who, by his valour, won the honour of exalted kingship.
C04-JAGTA-EN-004   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> From him arose Shri Siyaka, whose footstool was long tinted by the jewel-radiance of the crowns of the kings of the earth, and in the water-waves of whose sword the enemy-hosts sank — he who was the foremost among victorious kings.
C04-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> His son was Bairisimha, who was as a lion to the king-elephants, who was the mightiest among the valiant, and who set up praise-bearing victory-pillars (*jayastambha*) upon the earth girdled by the four oceans.
C04-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> From him arose Shri Vakyapati, who was as the sun to the lotus-eyes of the young women of Avanti, who was self-luminous by the flash of the sword in his hand, who was the equal of Indra, and whose war-steeds (or axles) drank the waters of the Ganga and the ocean.
C04-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> From him arose a son named Bairisimha, who was famed in the world also by the name Vajratasvami; through the slaying of the enemy-host by the edge (*dhara*) of whose sword, the illustrious city of Dhara (Dhara Nagari) was brought to renown.
C04-JAGTA-EN-008   ≈ p. 141 (Hindi original)
> His son — who was as an ornament to the entire earth, who was the sole abode of virtues, whose store [of glory] increased through the wealth won from all the enemies vanquished by valour, who was accomplished in oratory (*vaktritva*), lofty poetry, proficiency in logic, and adept in *shastra* and *agama* — is he whose fame is forever sung by the good, by the name of Shrimat Vakyapatirajadeva.
C04-JAGTA-EN-009   ≈ p. 142 (Hindi original)
> He whose lotus-feet were tinted by the crown-jewels of the (kings of the) Karnata, Lata, Kerala and Chola, and who was renowned, like the wish-tree (*kalpavriksha*), for fulfilling the desires of the beseeching supplicants.
C04-JAGTA-EN-010   ≈ p. 142 (Hindi original)
> His younger brother Shri Sindhuraja, having vanquished the Huna king, won the fortune of victory (Vijaya-shri); from whom was born Bhojraja — who made even the best of men tremble, and who was a peerless jewel.
C04-JAGTA-EN-011   ≈ p. 142 (Hindi original)
> He, desirous of victory over the city of Tripuri, having conquered the Yuvaraja in battle and slain his commanders, raised his sword on high.
C04-JAGTA-EN-012   ≈ p. 142 (Hindi original)
> From him arose Shri Harshadeva, whose kettledrum-blast was as splendid as the roar of the great elephants roaring amid the armies of the enemy-hosts, who was as mighty [as Garuda], and who in battle seized the Lakshmi (fortune) of Khodingdev.
C04-JAGTA-EN-013   ≈ p. 142 (Hindi original)
> The armies of the Karnata, the Lord of Lata, the Gurjar king, and the Turks — chief among them the Chedi king Indrath, Togal and Bhima — were conquered by his mere servants; and for this reason it is [only] the might of the arms of his hereditary forces that is reckoned, not that of his warriors — for the warriors' turn had not even come.
C04-JAGTA-EN-019   ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)
> He who, all around, by constructing temples of Kedareshvar, Rameshvar, Somnath, Sundir, Kala, Anal, Rudra and the rest — abodes of the gods — made the sacred earth one bearing a name true [to itself].
C04-JAGTA-EN-020   ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
> What he accomplished (won), what he ordained, what he gave, what he knew — no other could do. What greater praise than this can there be of the poet-king (*Kaviraja*) Shri Bhoj?
C04-JAGTA-EN-021   ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
This is the story of the great achievement of the Paramara kings, to whose glory a king named Udayaditya, King Bhoj's successor, had added lustre.
C04-JAGTA-EN-023   ≈ p. 45 (Hindi original)
After all, Udaipur too has a deep relation with Raja Bhoj. Udayaditya became Maharaja only after him. Bhoj must have been well aware of every outline of Udaipur's development. Udayaditya must have prepared himself, under his very protection, along with his rich tastes, for skilled statecraft.
C04-JAGTA-EN-024   ≈ p. 63 (Hindi original)
The king Udayaditya, after whose name this city was named, is the 12th king of the Paramara dynasty. He is a kinsman (*bandhu*) of Raja Bhoj. That is, he must have learned and understood the ways-and-manners of statecraft with Raja Bhoj himself; and when Raja Bhoj oversaw his empire from the capital Dhar, Udayaditya must have been sent here, made the feudatory.
C04-JAGTA-EN-025   ≈ p. 96 (Hindi original)
In its second shloka is — "The illustrious Pawar (Paramar) dynasty's king, a brave warrior, became a learned king who established the Malava kingdom and who punished the sinners. From him [came] a famous son, endowed with all virtues — a god-devotee named Gandal (Gondal), a giver (*dana*), an enjoyer (*bhokta*), discerning, and conqueror of all enemies, who became renowned by his fame."
In the third shloka is — "His son Pata (Jnata), by churning the enemy armies (*arivala-mathan*), obtained the kingdom, and won fame in keeping with the ancestors of the lineage. Upon the earthly sphere, going to Madhyadesh Malava, he attained renown; of a movement conforming to [his] branch, a god-worshipper, having performed religious austerity (*dharma-tapasya*), he built temples furnished with water-reservoirs."
The fifth shloka says — "A great giver of gold and grain to the Brahmins, having deemed Udayaditya worthy, he made [him] king, and amassed fame."
C04-JAGTA-EN-026   ≈ p. 131 (Hindi original)
But in the writings of Kakanadabot (Sanchi) and Udayagiri, respectively, Amrakardava *[the source reads "आत्रकादवर्द" — garbled]* and Virasena *["शाब" is unclear in source]* — who were the emperor's trusted commanders — were called the *tat-padanudhyata* of Chandragupta II Vikramaditya. From this it was concluded that the literal meaning, "meditating upon the feet," is more apt. This is a eulogy of praise, which has nothing to do with succession. Therefore, here, the claim — from the Shorgadh inscription — that Udayaditya received the succession directly from Bhoj is weakened.
C04-JAGTA-EN-027   ≈ p. 132 (Hindi original)
He says that the mention of this 'Pata' or 'Jnata' as the father of Udayaditya is a point that makes new gestures. One meaning of this is that Udayaditya was not coming down from the lineage-tradition of Sindhuraj, but was related to a junior branch (*avara-shakha*) of the Paramara dynasty. On this basis it may be supposed that Udayaditya possibly ruled in the eastern part of Malwa as a feudatory-lord (*samantadhipati*) under Maharaja Bhoj. Within this region lay Vidisha too. It must have been then that Udaipur began to develop as an important centre under it.
C04-JAGTA-EN-028   ≈ p. 132 (Hindi original)
One more such link joins in, from the Shorgadh inscription of Udayaditya. In its fifth and sixth lines it is said that: "Parama-bhattaraka Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara Shri Bhojdev's *padanudhyata* [one who meditates at his feet], Parama-bhattaraka Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara Shri Udayadityadeva, [is] well (*kushali*)."
C04-JAGTA-EN-029   ≈ p. 132 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
It may also be that when the combined armies of the Chalukyas of Kalyani and the Karnatas attacked the capital Dhara and, around 1070 CE, killed in battle Bhojdev's son and successor Jaisingh I — then, in such a situation, in the political emptiness suddenly created, Udayaditya may have come to the throne.
C04-JAGTA-EN-030   ≈ p. 132 (Hindi original)
> That is — "The Shiva-devotee (Bhojdev), whose splendour was like the sun, having departed to heaven — when the whole earth, like the city of Dhara, became pervaded by the darkness in the form of enemies, and his traditional warriors grew weak — then, like the sun, Udayadityadev, destroying the risen enemy-darkness with the rays of his sword-staff (*khadga-danda*), and gladdening the minds of all people, rose (*udit*)."
C04-JAGTA-EN-031   ≈ p. 133 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
> That is — "On that one's (Bhojdev's) attaining the kinship of Indra (i.e., passing away), and the kinsmen (*kautumbik*) of the kingdom's lord being immersed in the water of conflict, his kinsman (*bandhu*) Udayaditya became king — who, having arisen, rescued this earth (tormented jointly, like the great ocean, by kings such as the Karnata, Karna, and the rest) and thereby imitated the Varaha (Boar) avatar."
In this, Udayaditya is described as the "*bandhu*" (kinsman) of Bhojdev. This is a word worthy of note.
C04-JAGTA-EN-032   ≈ p. 133 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
The Nagpur inscription is that of King Narvarman, son of King Udayaditya, who, after Udayaditya, became the next king of the Paramara dynasty in 1094 and ruled until 1133 CE. In it is the complete information of the Paramara dynasty up to Narvarman — who came to the throne after whom. In the 56th shloka it is clear that this prashasti was composed by King Narvarman himself.
C04-JAGTA-EN-033   ≈ p. 134 (Hindi original)
> This means — "He whose father was Udayaditya, and whose *pitrivya* — that is, paternal uncle — was Dev Bhojraj: these two, flourishing, soon by their own splendour attained the sovereignty of the earth and became established (like the sun and the moon, the *pushpavantau*)."
C04-JAGTA-EN-034   ≈ p. 134 (Hindi original)
> That is — "After him (Bhoj Dev), his brother Udayadity rose to eminence — he who rescued the land of Malava, which had been [ground into the dust] / laid low by the attack of three enemies."
In the Jainad writing, Jagdev wrote King Udayadity as his father and King Bhojdev as [his] uncle (*pitrivya*), whereas in the Dongargaon inscription Jagdev has written his father Udayadity as Bhojdev's 'brother' (*bhrata*).
C04-JAGTA-EN-035   ≈ p. 134 (Hindi original)
Jagdev was the third and youngest prince of his father Udayadity (as his inscriptions tell us). Older than him were two brothers, Lakshmadeva and Narvarman.
Prince Lakshmadeva did not survive even his father's lifetime. Prince Narvarman became the Paramaras' next ruler after [his father] Udayadity's death.
C04-JAGTA-EN-036   ≈ p. 134 (Hindi original)
Now the last knot that remains is this: if King Bhoj and King Udayadity were both "*sahodar*" (uterine brothers, born of the same mother), then Udayadity's birth must have occurred before the death of their father King Sindhuraj, or within just a few months of that death. The exact date of King Sindhuraj's death is not known, but his son King Bhojdev appears as a king in Dhar in 1011 CE. If they were uterine brothers, then Udayadity ought, in the ordinary course, to have become king after King Bhojdev's death in 1055 CE. Why did he have to wait until 1070 CE?
C04-JAGTA-EN-037   ≈ p. 135 (Hindi original)
In it comes the name of Professor Franz Kielhorn, who held this very view [that they were brothers]. Then, on this issue, there is the mention of D.C. Ganguly, who brings a detail from the *Ain-i-Akbari* in favour of this same conclusion. Ganguly's opinion was opposed by H.C. Ray in his book *Dynastic History of Northern India*. He does not consider *bandhu* to mean brother.
C04-JAGTA-EN-038   ≈ p. 135 (Hindi original)
> On the departure to heaven of the Shiva-devotee (King Bhoj), whose splendour was like the sun — when the whole earth, like the city of Dhara, became pervaded by the enemy-like darkness, and his hereditary warrior-folk grew weak — then, like the sun, Udayadityadev, destroying the risen mass of enemy-darkness with the rays of his sword-staff, and gladdening the minds of all people, rose.
C04-JAGTA-EN-039   ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
In passing: the famous Udayagiri caves near Vidisha; Udaipur, seventy km from Vidisha; and, in this same town, the Udaysamudra situated about five hundred metres away, in front of the Udayeshvar Mahadev temple — these names are all named after Udyadity, who is mentioned in Paramara-era history as the grandson (*pautra*) of Bhoj. In some places he is mentioned as Bhoj-*bandhu* (Bhoj's kinsman) too. However, the fifth-century-CE caves near Sanchi were not built by Udyadity; he [merely] gave the hill his name.
C04-JAGTA-EN-040   ≈ p. 167 (Hindi original)   also Ch 3, Ch 7
The Saraswati-kanthabharana of Dhar, Ujjain and Mandu have been proof of this — as also the historical assembly-hall (*sabha mandal*) of old Bhopal in the Chowk Bazaar, which was built by this very Udaipur's creator, Maharaj Udayaditya — who came after King Bhoj in the Paramara dynasty.
C04-JAGTA-EN-041   ≈ p. 12 (Hindi original)   also Ch 1
It was a small place situated five hundred kilometres from Dhar, the capital of the Paramaras, on their border — which certainly had strategic importance in the sense that it touched the territory of the Kalachuri dynasty. Udaipur became an important centre situated on the frontier of the two dynasties because there were sandstone quarries here. Being under the Paramaras, Udaipur had seen itself developing like some mini-capital. Its importance lay in this — that a future emperor of the Paramara dynasty had, in his youth, lived in Udaipur. That crown-prince was Udayaditya, whose coronation the city of Dhara once witnessed — that same city of Dhara which was King Bhoj's beloved city; today's Dhar, the magnificent capital of the Paramaras.
C04-JAGTA-EN-042   ≈ p. 17 (Hindi original)   also Ch 2
This place's fame is from the Neelkantheshwar temple, which was built by Maharaj Udayaditya, who came in the generation after King Bhoj. The name of this town, Udaipur, too is connected with him.
C04-JAGTA-EN-043   ≈ p. 18 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5, Ch 1
It is very possible that Crown-Prince Udayaditya was sent, in his father's time, to Udaipur situated on the border of the Paramaras' kingdom. It may be that Udayaditya himself made the first major plan for the development of this frontier town and, according to the tradition of his lineage, began the finest constructions, which must have continued even after he became king. Otherwise, such temples are there village by village, but the layout of a palace encircled by a vast boundary wall and bastions, within a town protected by a square fort, is only there in this region. It must certainly have been a settlement inhabited before Udayaditya, where the Paramaras' military detachment must have been posted for the protection of the borders. Udayaditya's appointment had awakened the fortune of this settlement, where now something was going to happen that would remain alive in memory even after centuries.
C04-JAGTA-EN-044   ≈ p. 19 (Hindi original)   also Ch 1
In the Paramaras' time this was the Sabha Mandal — the city's important Brahmasthan. It was a vast temple precinct, which was also a Sanskrit school — exactly on the lines of the great Saraswati-kanthabharanas of Ujjain, Dhar and Mandu. But the construction of the Sabha Mandal took place after Bhoj, and it was Udayaditya himself who had it done.
C04-JAGTA-EN-045   ≈ p. 80 (Hindi original)
Udaipur was on the geographic border of the Paramara dynasty, which was strategically important. On the hill adjoining Udaipur — encircled on all sides by a fort — the remains of a fortification are visible even from Google Map. Besides the main temple, the presence of the vast precinct of the palace tells that the kin of the Paramara kings themselves must have lived here for the security of this border.
C04-JAGTA-EN-046   ≈ p. 96 (Hindi original)
In the first to sixth lines is a description of some important military victories of Udayadity. Among these vanquished ones, the only name [given] is that of the Chedi ruler of Dahalamandala, the "Dahaladhish." It is recorded in the inscription that the Dahaladhish was defeated and killed. Dahal was originally the region around Jabalpur, which is considered the original home (*mulasthan*) of the Kalachuris.
C04-JAGTA-EN-048   ≈ p. 137 (Hindi original)
As far as Udaipur is concerned, it is evident from its very name that it is related to King Udayadity. The fact that no inscription of the earlier Paramara kings is found here tells us that this town came into existence only in Udayadity's time.
C04-JAGTA-EN-049   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)
In Vidisha district, Jaisingh is probably the first Paramara ruler whose writing is found here. In this one too, the genealogy of the Paramara kings goes only up to Udayadityadev.
C04-JAGTA-EN-050   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)
King Udayadity's reign lay between 1070 and 1093.
C04-JAGTA-EN-051   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)
> The task of rescuing the earth that the Varaha (Boar) avatar performed — that same task of lifting up the land, this Paramar accomplished with such ease. How wondrous this is!
C04-JAGTA-EN-052   ≈ p. 143 (Hindi original)
Udaipur, Udaydit had fashioned with his whole heart. These descendants of King Bhoj were the governor of this last edge of the kingdom. He had the Udaysagar built.
C04-JAGTA-EN-053   ≈ p. 160 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
Udaipur can be part of a deep research project, in which Raja Bhoj's town-planning is an important subject. It has been a splendid town, of square shape, encircled on all sides by equally long walls, within which the presence of the king's palace indicates that Bhoj's heirs must indeed have lived here — and it is that very Udayaditya after whose name this town is.
C04-JAGTA-EN-056   ≈ p. 22 (Hindi original)   also Ch 7
The vastness of the Vijay Mandir is, in comparison with Udaipur's temple, quite different and magnificent. It must have been several times more magnificent and larger than Udaipur's. Maharaj Narvarman had, indeed, to draw his own bigger line; he had, indeed, to have a construction made vaster and more magnificent than Maharaj Udayaditya's.
C04-JAGTA-EN-057   ≈ p. 58 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
The rulers of the Paramara dynasty were worshippers of Shiva, and in their long rule of three or four hundred years, they had built countless temples, schools and hostels in their vast kingdom. On the border of Bundelkhand, Udaipur was one of the busiest commercial centres of their kingdom, where, in a city encircled on all sides by a fort, a palace, several stepwells, doorways, temples, squares-and-platforms, and a courthouse were built over centuries. The whole town-layout can even today be seen and understood from Google Map, which extends up to the fortification done all the way up the hill.
C04-JAGTA-EN-058   ≈ p. 70 (Hindi original)   also Ch 7, Ch 8
It is very possible that when Udayaditya, becoming king, departed for the capital Dhar, then this beloved character of his did not move from her beloved city Udaipur, and accepted staying right here. Staying right here, she must have always expressed good wishes for her king; and when the construction of the temple was completed, she must have awaited the king's chariots at the time of the flag-hoisting ceremony of 30 March 1080.
C04-JAGTA-EN-061   ≈ p. 111 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
From the knowers of the Nagari script it is learned that its primary ruler was possibly one Rudraditya. This inscription tells that the Nemak-line rulers were worshippers of Shiva. There was another feudatory named Shudrak, who had won a victory over some Gurjar king. This inscription describes that it was the son of this very feudatory named Shudrak — whose name is unclear — who had the temple built in Udaipur. We can only surmise that at the time the construction of the Udaipur temple must have begun, this feudatory-family of the Nemak dynasty was the local administrator here, and it was under its supervision that this temple was built.
C04-JAGTA-EN-062   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
In the old settlement of today's Vidisha, the most special of all is that very Vijay Mandir, which was built by the Paramara king Narvarman. In the genealogy of the Paramaras, king Narvarman's order comes after king Udayaditya; he was his successor. It is possible that at the time of the construction of Udaipur's Neelkantheshwar Mahadev temple, the young Narvarman lived in Udaipur itself, and that, after taking up the affairs of state in the capital Dhar, his inclination remained toward this region.
C04-JAGTA-EN-063   ≈ p. 58 (Hindi original)
Whichever king may have had it started, I feel he wanted to leave behind his predecessor king Udayaditya — exactly like king Narvarman, who, by building the Vijay Mandir in Vidisha, tried to go several steps ahead of his father's great construction work in Udaipur.
It is possible that he was going to create something four steps ahead of even Narvarman's Vijay Mandir.
C04-JAGTA-EN-065   ≈ p. 78 (Hindi original)
It is very possible that this is an episode of the later period of the Paramaras. Whatever emerged, emerged as much as it could and stopped right there. In Udaipur's fate, only this much of what the Paramaras did was written.
C04-JAGTA-EN-066   ≈ p. 79 (Hindi original)
In the form of the Vijay Mandir, Maharaj Udayaditya's son, and the 13th Maharaj of the Paramara dynasty, Narvarman, made in Vidisha a construction vaster and grander than his father's. But it could last only a hundred years. The great father's creation stands proudly even after a thousand years.
C04-JAGTA-EN-067   ≈ p. 106 (Hindi original)
Worthy of note, too, is that long before the date of the inscription, Devpaldev had already begun sitting upon the royal throne in the capital Dhar and using the full royal titles. Before becoming king, he must have held some military or administrative responsibility in Udaipur itself — and that is why, in this inscription, he is addressed only with the title of *Mahakumar*. If he was in Udaipur before becoming king, then this fact enhances the importance of Udaipur; for, about a hundred and fifty years before him, Udayadity too must have been in some such role. Now this also becomes clear — that Udaipur was such a centre from which at least two crown-princes came forth to become kings in the Paramara dynasty.
C04-JAGTA-EN-071   ≈ p. 146 (Hindi original)
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 50)
अपने पाँच शताब्दियों के लम्बे काल में महात्वाकांक्षी परमार राजाओं ने एक बड़े भूभाग पर अपनी सीमाओं का विस्तार किया। उनका राज्य मालवा के साथ भोपाल के आगे विदिशा और उदयपुर तक, पश्चिम में हंसोल, मोढासा, महुडी जैसे इलाकों तक फैला हुआ था। दक्षिण में नर्मदा के तटीय क्षेत्रों जैसे होशंगाबाद से लेकर उत्तर में कोटा तक के इलाकों पर परमार राज कर रहे थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In their long period of five centuries ambitious Paramara kings expanded their borders over a large area. Their kingdom spread with Malwa beyond Bhopal to Vidisha and Udaypur, in the west to areas like Hansol, Modasa and Mahudi. Paramaras ruled areas from Hoshangabad on the Narmada coast in the south to Kota in the north.
C04-JAGTA-HI-001   p. 139   also Ch 1
की गाय का बलात् हरण किया, तब उन (वसिष्ठ) के प्रभाव से अग्निकुण्ड में से एक
वीर उत्पन्न हुआ जिसने अकेले ही शत्रु सैन्य का नाश किया ॥५॥
मारयित्वा परान्धेनुमानिन्ये स ततो मुनिः।
उवाच परमारा [ख्यः पा]थिवेंद्रो भविष्यसि ॥६॥
शत्रुओं को मार कर वह गाय को वापस ले आया, तब मुनि ने कहा--"तुम परमार
नाम से राजा होगे ॥६॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* ... when (he) forcibly took away the cow, then through the influence of (Vasishtha) a hero arose from the fire-pit who alone destroyed the enemy army. Having killed the enemies he brought back the cow; then the sage said: 'You will be a king by the name Paramara.'
C04-JAGTA-HI-002   p. 140   also Ch 6
उस वंश में अखिल यज्ञ समूह से तूभ देवताओं के द्वारा जिसकी कीर्ति गाई जा रही
है व जो द्विज समूह में रत्न के समान हैं और जिसने अपने पराक्रम से उच्च राजत्व का
मान प्राप्त कर लिया है ऐसा उपेन्द्रराज (नरेश) हुआ ॥७॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* In that lineage was born Upendraraja, whose fame is sung by the gods and who is like a jewel among the twice-born, and who by his valour has attained the dignity of high kingship.
C04-JAGTA-HI-003   p. 140
उसका पुत्र बैरिसिंह था जो राजारूपी हाथियों के लिये सिंह के समान था, जो
पराक्रमियों में सबसे बलवान था और जिसने चारों समुद्रों से युक्त पृथ्वी पर प्रशस्तियुक्त
जयस्तम्भ स्थापित किये !!८॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* His son was Vairisimha, who was like a lion to elephant-like kings, strongest among the valiant, and who set up victory pillars with prashastis on the earth bounded by the four seas.
C04-JAGTA-HI-004   p. 140
उसके श्री सीयक (उत्पन्न) हुआ जिसका पादपीठ पृथ्वी के नरेशों के मुकुटों के
रत्नप्रभा से रंजित होता था तथा जिसकी तलवार की जल लहरियों में शत्रुसमूह डूब
जाते थे, जो विजयी राजाओं में सर्वश्रेष्ठ था ॥९॥
तस्माद्वन्ति तरूणीय नारविन्दभारवान्भूत्करकृपाणगरीचिदीपः।
श्रीवाक्यपतिः स (श) तमरवान्कृतितुरंगा गंगासमुद्र सलिलानि पि वन्ति यस्मै ॥१०॥
उससे श्री वाक्यपति हुआ जो अवन्ति की युवतियों के नयन कमलों के लिये सूर्य के समान था, अपने हाथ की तलवार की चमक से जो स्वयं प्रकाशमान था, जो इन्द्र के समान था तथा जिसके अक्ष गंगा व समुद्र के जलों का पान करते थे ॥१०॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* From him was born Shri Siyaka, whose footstool was coloured by the jewel-radiance of the crowns of the kings of the earth and in whose sword's water-waves enemy hosts drowned, the foremost of victorious kings. From him came Shri Vakpati, who was like the sun to the lotus-eyes of the young women of Avanti, self-radiant with the gleam of the sword in his hand, like Indra, and whose horses drank the waters of the Ganga and the sea.
C04-JAGTA-HI-005   p. 140–141
उसका पुत्र जो संपूर्ण पृथ्वी के लिये आभूषण रूप था, जो गुणों का एकमात्र आश्रय था, शौर्य से जीते समस्त शत्रुओं से धन प्राप्ति के द्वारा जिसके कोप में वृद्धि होती थी, जो वकृत्य, उच्च कवित्व, तर्क प्रवीणता तथा शास्त्र आगम में निष्णात था तथा सज्जनों के द्वारा श्रीमत् वाक्यपतिराजदेव के नाम से जिसकी कीर्ति गाई जाती है ॥१३॥
कर्णाटलाटकैरल चोलशिरोरन्तरागिपदकमलः।
यशः प्रणीयगणार्थितदाता कल्पवृक्षप्रख्यः ॥१४॥
जिसके पदकमल कर्णाट लाट केरल चोल (नरेशों) के मुकुट रत्नों से रंजित होते थे और जो प्रार्थना करने वाले याचकों की इच्छा पूर्ति के लिये कल्पवृक्ष के समान विख्यात था ॥१४॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* His son, an ornament for the whole earth, sole refuge of virtues, whose wrath grew by wealth taken from enemies conquered in valour, skilled in oratory, high poetry, logic and the scriptures, whose fame is sung by good men by the name of Shrimat Vakpatirajadeva. Whose lotus feet were coloured by the crown jewels of Karnata, Lata, Kerala and Chola kings, and who was known as a wish-fulfilling tree for petitioners.
C04-JAGTA-HI-006   p. 141–142
त्रिपुरी नगरी में विजय की इच्छा करने वाले उसने युद्ध में युवराज को जीतकर तथा उसके सेनापतियों को मार कर अपनी खड्ग को ऊंचा किया ॥१५॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* Desiring victory at the city of Tripuri, he defeated the Yuvaraja in battle, killed his commanders and raised his sword high.
C04-JAGTA-HI-007   p. 142
उसके छोटे भाई श्री सिंधुराज ने हूणराज को जीत कर विजयश्री प्राप्त की, जिससे भोजराज उत्पन्न हुआ, जिसने उत्तम व्यक्तियों को भी कंपा दिया व जो अद्वितीय रत्न था ॥१६॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* His younger brother Shri Sindhuraja won victory by conquering the Huna king, from whom was born Bhojaraja, who made even excellent men tremble and who was a unique jewel.
C04-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 142
जिसने कैलाश से मलय तक तथा उदयाचल से अस्ताचल तक संपूर्ण पृथ्वी का पृथुराज के समान उपभोग किया, जिसने अपने धनुष की डोरी से सहज ही में दिक्पालों को उखाड़ कर दिशाओं में फेंक दिया तथा जिसने पृथ्वी को परमप्रीति से प्रसन्न किया ॥१७॥
साधितं विहितं दत्तं ज्ञातं तथान्य केनचित्।
किमन्यत्कविराजस्य श्रीभोजस्य प्रशस्यते ॥१८॥
जो उसने साधित किया (प्राप्त किया), जो आदेश किया, जो दिया, जो ज्ञात किया वह कोई न कर सका। कविराज श्री भोज की इससे अधिक प्रशंसा क्या हो सकती है ॥१८॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* He who enjoyed the entire earth from Kailasa to Malaya and from the rising mountain to the setting mountain like Prithu, who easily uprooted the guardians of the directions with his bowstring and threw them in the directions, and who gladdened the earth with supreme love. What he achieved, ordained, gave and knew, none else could do. What more praise can there be of Kaviraja Shri Bhoja.
C04-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 142
कर्णाट, लाटपति, गुर्जर नरेश व तुर्कों, जिनमें मुख्य चेदि नरेश इन्द्रथ, तोगल तथा भीम थे, की सेनाओं को जिसके भृत्यमात्रों ने ही विजित कर लिया व इस कारण जिसके पारंपरिक सेनाओं के बाहुबल की उन्नता की गणना की जाती है, उसके योद्धाओं की नहीं की जाती (क्योंकि योद्धाओं की तो अभी बारी भी नहीं आ पाई थी) ॥१९॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* The armies of the Karnata, the lord of Lata, the Gurjara king and the Turks, chiefly the Chedi king Indratha, Togala and Bhima, were conquered by his mere servants, and so the strength of his traditional armies is counted, not that of his warriors (since the warriors had not yet had their turn).
C04-JAGTA-HI-014   p. 143
यह परमार राजाओं के महान कृतित्व की कहानी है, जिसकी शान में उदयादित्य नाम के राजा भोज के उत्तराधिकारी ने चार चांद लगा दिए थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* This is the story of the great works of the Paramara kings, to whose glory Udayaditya, a successor of King Bhoja, added lustre.
C04-JAGTA-HI-016   p. 44
आखिर उदयपुर का भी तो राजा भोज से गहरा रिश्ता है। उदयादित्य उनके ही बाद में तो महाराजा हुए थे। उदयपुर के विकास की हर रूपरेखा से भोज भलीभांति अवगत रहे होंगे। उदयादित्य ने उनके ही संरक्षण में खुद को अपनी समृद्ध अभिरुचियों के साथ कुशल राजकाज के लिए तैयार किया होगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* After all, Udaipur too has a deep relationship with Raja Bhoj. Udayaditya became maharaja after him. Bhoj must have been well aware of every outline of Udaipur's development. Udayaditya must have prepared himself for skilled governance with his rich interests under Bhoj's patronage.
C04-JAGTA-HI-017   p. 62
जिन राजा उदयदित्य के नाम पर इस नगर का नामकरण हुआ, वे परमार राजवंश के 12 वें राजा हैं। राजा भोज के बंधु हैं। यानी राजकाज के तौर-तरीके उन्होंने राजा भोज के साथ ही सीखे-समझे होंगे और जब राजा भोज राजधानी धार से अपना साम्राज्य देखते होंगे तब उदयदित्य को यहां का सामंत बनाकर भेजा गया होगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* King Udayaditya, after whom this town is named, is the 12th king of the Paramara dynasty. He is King Bhoja's brother. That is, he must have learned and understood the ways of governance along with King Bhoja, and when King Bhoja was overseeing his empire from the capital Dhar, Udayaditya must have been sent here as feudatory.
C04-JAGTA-HI-018   p. 97
यह भी परमारों के दूसरे शिलालेखों की तरह शिव की आराधना से शुरू है और आगे उदयাদित्य के बारे में बताना शुरू कर देता है। हर शिलालेख कुछ नई जानकारी देता है। कुछ अलग इशारे करता है। यह भोज और उदयাদित्य के बीच के समय की धुंध में अस्पष्ट से दिखाई देने वाले संबंधों पर नई रोशनी डालता है।
इसके दूसरे श्लोक में है-"श्रीमान पावर (परमार) वंश नरेश सूरवीर मालव राज्य स्थापित करने वाला विद्वान नरेश हुआ जो पापियों को दण्डित करता था। उससे प्रसिद्ध पुत्र सकल गुणों से युक्त गांडल (गोंडल) नामक देवभक्त, दाना, भोक्ता, विवेकी और समस्त शत्रुओं को जीत वाला, अपनी कीर्ति से विख्यात हुआ।"
*Gloss [my translation]:* Like other Paramara inscriptions it begins with worship of Shiva and goes on to tell about Udayaditya. Each inscription gives some new information. It throws new light on the relation between Bhoja and Udayaditya, seen faintly in the haze of time. Its second verse says a heroic, learned Paramara king established the Malwa kingdom and punished sinners. From him a renowned son named Gandal (Gondal), devout, generous, discerning, who conquered all enemies, became famous for his glory.
C04-JAGTA-HI-019   p. 131   also Ch 6
यह भी हो सकता है कि जब कल्याणी के चालुक्यों और कर्णटों की संयुक्त सेनाओं ने राजधानी धारा पर हमलाकर भोजदेव के पुत्र और उत्तराधिकारी जयसिंह प्रथम को 1070 ई. के आसपास युद्ध में मार दिया हो तो ऐसी स्थिति में अचानक पैदा हुए राजनीतिक खालीपन में उदयাদित्य सिंहासन पर आए हों। यह सवाल और अहम है कि उदयাদित्य का राजा भोज से रिश्ता क्या था?
*Gloss [my translation]:* It may also be that when the combined armies of the Chalukyas of Kalyani and the Karnatas attacked the capital Dhara and killed Bhoja's son and successor Jayasimha I around 1070 CE, Udayaditya came to the throne in the sudden political vacuum. The question of Udayaditya's relation to Raja Bhoja is more important.
C04-JAGTA-HI-020   p. 131
वे कहते हैं कि उदयাদित्य के पिता के रूप में इन 'पाता' अथवा 'ज्ञाता' का जिक्र नए इशारे करने वाला बिंदु है। इसका एक मतलब यह है कि उदयাদित्य सिंधुराज की वंशपरम्परा से नहीं आ रहे थे बल्कि परमार राजवंश की एक अवर-शाखा से संबंधित थे। इसके आधार पर ऐसा माना जा सकता है कि उदयাদित्य संभवतः महाराजा भोज के अधीन एक सामंतान्धिपति के रूप में मालवा के पूर्वी भाग में राज करते थे। इस इलाके में विदिशा भी था। तब उदयपुर इसके अंतर्गत एक महत्वपूर्ण केंद्र के रूप में विकसित होने लगा होगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Verma stops here to analyse. He says that the mention of 'Pata' or 'Jnata' as Udayaditya's father is a point suggesting new indications. One meaning is that Udayaditya did not descend from Sindhuraja's line but belonged to a junior branch of the Paramara dynasty. On this basis it can be assumed that Udayaditya probably ruled in the eastern part of Malwa as a chief feudatory under Maharaja Bhoja. Vidisha was also in this region. Then Udaypur must have begun to develop under it as an important centre.
C04-JAGTA-HI-021   p. 131   also Ch 6, Ch 3
तीसरे श्लोक में है-"उसके पुत्र पाता (ज्ञाता) ने शत्रुसैन्यों का मंथन कर (अरिवलमथन) राज्य प्राप्त किया व वंश के पूर्वजों के अनुसार कीर्ति प्राप्त की। भूमंडल पर मध्यदेश मालव में जाकर प्रसिद्धि प्राप्त की, शाखानुकूल गति वाला, देवपूजक, धर्म तपस्या करके, जलाशय से युक्त देवालयों का निर्माण किया।"
पांचवा श्लोक कहता है-"ब्राह्मणों को सुवर्ण धान्य का अतिदान देने वाला, उदयাদित्य को योग्य मान कर नरेश बनाया व कीर्ति का संचय किया।"
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the third verse: his son Pata (Jnata) crushed enemy armies, obtained the kingdom and gained fame like his forefathers; he built temples with reservoirs. The fifth verse says: giver of abundant gold and grain to Brahmins, he regarded Udayaditya as worthy, made him king and accumulated fame.
C04-JAGTA-HI-022   p. 131   also Ch 6
अर्थात् "शिवभक्त (भोजदेव), जिसका प्रताप सूर्य के समान था, के स्वर्ग चले जाने पर, धारा नगरी के समान जब सारी पृथ्वी शत्रुरूपी अंधकार से व्याप्त हो गई तथा उसके पारंपरिक योद्धाजन दुर्बल हो गये, तब सूर्य के समान उदयादित्यदेव,
उन्नत शत्रूपी अंधकार को खड्गदण्ड की किरणों से नष्ट कर समस्तजनों के मनो को आनन्दित करता हुआ, उदित हुआ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* After the Shiva-devotee (Bhojadeva), whose glory was like the sun, went to heaven, when the whole earth like the city of Dhara was covered by the darkness of enemies and his traditional warriors had grown weak, then Udayadityadeva, like the sun, rose, destroying the towering darkness of enemies with the rays of his sword and gladdening all people.
C04-JAGTA-HI-023   p. 132–133   also Ch 6
ऐसी ही एक और कड़ी जुड़ती है उदयादित्य के शोरगढ़ अभिलेख से। इसकी पांचवीं और छठवीं पंक्ति में कहा गया है कि "परमभट्टारक महाराजाधिराज परमेश्वर श्री भोजदेव पादानुध्येत परमभट्टारक महाराजाधिराज परमेश्वर श्री उदयादित्यदेव : कुशली।"
*Gloss [my translation]:* Another link is added by Udayaditya's Shorgarh inscription. Its fifth and sixth lines say: 'Paramabhattaraka Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara Shri Bhojadeva-padanudhyata Paramabhattaraka Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara Shri Udayadityadeva: well.'
C04-JAGTA-HI-024   p. 132   also Ch 6
उदयादित्य के सबसे छोटे पुत्र जगदेव के जैनड अभिलेख के श्लोक-6 में है-
यस्योदयादित्य नृपः पितासी, देवः पितृव्यः स च भोजराजः ।
विरेजतुयौ वसुधाधिपत्य, प्रास प्रतिष्ठानिव पुष्पवन्तो ॥6॥
इसका मतलब है- "जिसके पिता उदयादित्य थे और देव भोजराज पितृव्य अर्थात चाचा थे, विकसित होने वाले ये दोनों शिग्र ही अपने तेज से वसुधा का आधिपत्य प्राप्त कर प्रतिष्ठित हुए।"
*Gloss [my translation]:* In verse 6 of the Jainad inscription of Jagadeva, Udayaditya's youngest son: 'He whose father was Udayaditya and whose uncle (pitrivya) was the divine Bhojaraja.' It means: 'Whose father was Udayaditya, and the god Bhojaraja was the paternal uncle; these two, growing brilliant, attained sovereignty over the earth by their own splendour and were established.'
C04-JAGTA-HI-026   p. 133
नागपुर अभिलेख राजा उदयादित्य के पुत्र राजा नरवर्मन का है जो उदयादित्य के बाद 1094 में परमार वंश के अगले राजा हुए और 1133 ई० तक शासन किया। इसमें परमार राजवंश की नरवर्मन तक की पूरी जानकारी है कि कौन किसके बाद सिंहासन पर आया। 56 वें श्लोक में साफ है कि यह प्रशस्ति स्वयं राजा नरवर्मन के द्वारा रची गई थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The Nagpur inscription is of King Naravarman, son of King Udayaditya, who became the next king of the Paramara dynasty in 1094 after Udayaditya and ruled until 1133 CE. It has full information on the Paramara dynasty up to Naravarman, who succeeded whom. Verse 56 makes clear that this prashasti was composed by King Naravarman himself.
C04-JAGTA-HI-027   p. 133   also Ch 6, Ch 1
