अर्थात् "उस (भोजदेव) के इन्द्र की बंधुता को प्राप्त (दिवंगत) होने पर और राज्यस्वामी के कौटुम्बिकों के संघर्षरूपी जल में मग्न होने पर, उसका बंधु उदयादित्य नरेश हुआ, जिसने महासागर के समान संयुक्त रूप से कर्णाट कर्ण आदि नृपतियों के द्वारा पीड़ित इस पृथ्वी का उद्धार कर के वराह अवतार का अनुकरण किया। इसमें उदयादित्य को भोजदेव का "बंधु" बताया गया है। यह ध्यान देने योग्य शब्द है। धर्मशास्त्र में तीन प्रकार के बंधु हैं-आत्मबन्धु, पितृबन्धु और मातृबन्धु। इसके अलावा नातेदार, भाई-बिरादरी, सम्बन्धी, पारिवारिक नातेदार भी बंधु कहलाते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* After he (Bhojadeva) attained the kinship of Indra and the lord of the kingdom's kinsmen were sunk in the water of conflict, his bandhu Udayaditya became king, who, like the Varaha incarnation, rescued the earth troubled by the Karnata Karna and others joined like an ocean. Here Udayaditya is called Bhojadeva's 'bandhu'. This is a noteworthy word. In dharmashastra there are three kinds of bandhu: atmabandhu, pitribandhu and matribandhu. Besides, relatives, brotherhood and family kin are also called bandhu.
C04-JAGTA-HI-028   p. 133   also Ch 6
अर्थात् "उन (भोज देव) के पश्चात् उनके भाई उदयदित्य वृद्धि को प्राप्त हुए, जिनने
मालव भूमि का उद्धार किया, जो तीन शत्रुओं के आक्रमण से (धूली में) मन हो गई
थी।"
जैनड लेख में जगदेव ने राजा उदयदित्य को अपना पिता व राजा भोजदेव
को चाचा (पितृव्य:) लिखा जबकि डोंगरगांव अभिलेख में जगदेव ने अपने पिता
उदयदित्य को भोजदेव का 'भ्राता' लिखा है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Meaning: 'After him (Bhojadeva), his brother Udayaditya grew in power, who rescued the Malwa land, which had been (reduced to dust) by the attack of three enemies.' In the Jainad inscription Jagadeva wrote King Udayaditya as his father and King Bhojadeva as uncle (pitrivya), whereas in the Dongargaon inscription Jagadeva writes his father Udayaditya as Bhojadeva's 'brother'.
C04-JAGTA-HI-029   p. 134
तीसरे एवं सबसे छोटे राजकुमार थे (जो उनके अभिलेख बताते हैं)। उनसे बड़े दो भाई
लक्ष्मदेव व नरवर्मन थे।
राजकुमार लक्ष्मदेव अपने पिता के समय ही नहीं रहे। राजकुमार नरवर्मन अपने
उदयदित्य की मृत्यु के बाद परमारों के अगले शासक बने।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Jagadeva was the third and youngest prince of his father Udayaditya (as his inscriptions say). Two elder brothers were Lakshmadeva and Naravarman. Prince Lakshmadeva did not survive in his father's time. Prince Naravarman became the next ruler of the Paramaras after Udayaditya's death.
C04-JAGTA-HI-030   p. 134
तारीख ज्ञात नहीं है परन्तु उनके पुत्र राजा भोजदेव 1011 ईस्वी में धार में एक राजा के
रूप में दिखाई देते हैं। अगर वे सहोदर थे तो उदयदित्य को राजा भोजदेव की 1055
ईस्वी में मृत्यु के बाद सामान्य रूप से राजा बन जाना चाहिए था। उन्हें 1070 ईस्वी
तक का इन्तजार क्यों करना पड़ा?
*Gloss [my translation]:* The exact date of King Sindhuraja's death is not known, but his son King Bhojadeva appears as a king at Dhar in 1011 CE. If they were brothers, Udayaditya should normally have become king after Bhojadeva's death in 1055 CE. Why did he have to wait until 1070 CE?
C04-JAGTA-HI-031   p. 134
एक तर्क यह भी है कि अगर वे भोज के बंधु ही
होते तो भोज के समय के किसी भी शिलालेख या किताबों में कहीं तो उनका जिक्र
होना चाहिए था!
*Gloss [my translation]:* One argument is also that if he had been Bhoja's kinsman, he should have been mentioned somewhere in some inscription or book of Bhoja's time.
C04-JAGTA-HI-032   p. 134
शिव भक्त (राजा भोज), जिसका प्रताप सूर्य के समान था, के स्वर्ग चले जाने पर धारा नगरी के समान सारी पृथ्वी शत्रु रूपी अंधकार से व्याप्त हो गई तथा उसके पारंपरिक योद्धाजन दुर्बल हो गये, तब सूर्य के समान उदयादित्यदेव उन्नत शत्रुरूप अंधकार को खड्गदण्ड की किरणों से नष्ट करके समस्त जनो के मनो को आनन्दित करता हुआ उदित हुआ।॥२१॥
येन धरणीवराहः परमारेशो[दृधुतो] निरायासा (तु) ।
तस्यतस्य भूमेरुद्दरो वत कियन्मात्रः ॥२२॥
जो (पृथ्वी उद्धार का) कार्य वराह अवतार ने किया, वह भूमि उद्धार का कार्य इस परमार ने कितनी सुगमता से किया, यह कितना आश्चर्य है ॥२२॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* After the Shiva-devotee (King Bhoja), whose glory was like the sun, went to heaven, the whole earth, like the city of Dhara, was covered with the darkness of enemies and his traditional warriors grew weak; then Udayadityadeva rose like the sun, destroying the towering darkness of enemies with the rays of his sword and delighting all people. The work of rescuing the earth that the Varaha avatar did, how easily this Paramara did it, how wonderful.
C04-JAGTA-HI-035   p. 143   also Ch 6
चलते-चलते विदिशा के पास प्रसिद्ध उदयगिरि गुफाएँ, विदिशा से सतर किमी दूर उदयपुर और इसी नगर में उदयेश्वर महादेव देवालय के सामने करीब पाँच सौ मीटर दूर स्थित उदयसमुद्र आदि संज्ञाएँ परमारकालीन इतिहास में भोज के पौत्र के तौर पर
उल्लेखित उद्यादित्य के नाम पर ही हैं। कहीं कहीं उसका उल्लेख भोजबन्धु के रूप में भी है। हालाँकि सांची के समीप पाँचवीं सदी ईस्वी की गुफाओं का निर्माण उद्यादित्य ने नहीं कराया था। पहाड़ी को उसने अपना नाम दिया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Names such as the famous Udayagiri caves near Vidisha, Udaypur seventy km from Vidisha, and in this same town the Udayasamudra located about five hundred metres in front of the Udayeshvara Mahadeva temple are all after Udayaditya, who is mentioned in Paramara history as Bhoja's grandson. In some places he is also mentioned as Bhojabandhu. However, the fifth-century caves near Sanchi were not built by Udayaditya. He gave the hill his name.
C04-JAGTA-HI-036   p. 166–167   also Ch 7
महाराष्ट्र, गुजरात और राजस्थान के भीतर से लेकर परमारों के राज्य की उत्तर दिशा में सीमा यहां उदयपुर तक विस्तृत थी। यहां से कलचुरियों का इलाका शुरू होता था। उदयपुर सीमा पर होने की वजह से परमारों के लिए सामरिक दृष्टि से बहुत अहम रहा होगा, जो कि इसके एक हज़ार साल पुराने विकास और विस्तार से जाहिर भी है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* From within Maharashtra, Gujarat and Rajasthan, the northern limit of the Paramara kingdom extended up to Udaipur here. From here the Kalachuri territory began. Being on the border, Udaipur must have been strategically very important for the Paramaras, as its thousand-year-old development and expansion also show.
C04-JAGTA-HI-037   p. 11   also Ch 3
यह तो किसी राज्य की राजधानी भी नहीं था। वह परमारों की राजधानी धार से पांच सौ किलोमीटर दूर उनकी सीमा पर स्थित एक छोटा सा स्थान था, जिसका सामरिक महत्व इस नाते जरूर था कि वह कलचुरि राजवंश के क्षेत्र को छूता था। उदयपुर दोनों राजवंशों की सरहद पर स्थित एक महत्वपूर्ण केंद्र इसलिए बन गया कि यहां बलुआ पत्थर की खदानें थीं। परमारों के मातहत होने से उदयपुर ने किसी मिनी केपिटल की तरह खुद को विकसित होते हुए देखा था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* This was not even the capital of any state. It was a small place on the Paramāra border, five hundred kilometres from their capital Dhar, whose strategic importance was that it touched the Kalachuri dynasty's territory. Udaypur became an important centre on the border of both dynasties because there were sandstone quarries here.
C04-JAGTA-HI-038   p. 16   also Ch 2
परमारों के मातहत होने से उदयपुर ने किसी मिनी केपिटल की तरह खुद को विकसित होते हुए देखा था। इसका महत्व इसी में था कि परमार राजवंश का एक भविष्य सम्राट अपनी युवावस्था में उदयपुर में रहा था। वह युवराज उदयादित्य थे, जिनका राज्याभिषेक कभी धारा नगरी ने देखा। वही धारा नगरी जो राजा भोज की प्रिय नगरी थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Under the Paramāras, Udaypur saw itself developing like a mini capital. Its importance lay in this: a future emperor of the Paramāra dynasty lived in Udaypur in his youth. That prince was Udayāditya, whose coronation Dhara city once witnessed. The same Dhara city that was King Bhoja's favourite city.
C04-JAGTA-HI-039   p. 16
बहुत संभव है युवराज उदयादित्य अपने पिता के समय परमारों के राज्य की सीमा पर स्थित उदयपुर भेजे गए हों। हो सकता है कि उदयादित्य ने ही इस सीमावर्ती नगर के विकास की पहली बड़ी योजना बनाई हो और अपनी वंश परंपरा के अनुसार बेहतरीन निर्माण शुरू किए हों, जो उनके राजा बनने के बाद भी जारी रहे होंगे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is quite possible that prince Udayāditya was sent to Udaypur, on the border of the Paramāra kingdom, in his father's time. It may be that Udayāditya himself made the first major plan for developing this border town and began excellent constructions as per his dynastic tradition, which continued after he became king.
C04-JAGTA-HI-040   p. 18
इस जगह की प्रसिद्धि नीलकंठेश्वर मंदिर से है, जो राजा भोज के बाद की पीढ़ी में हुए महाराज उदयादित्य ने बनवाया था। इस नगर का नाम उदयपुर भी उनसे ही जुड़ा है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* This place is famous for the Nilakantheshwar temple, which Maharaja Udayaditya, of the generation after King Bhoja, had built. The name of this town, Udaypur, is also connected with him.
C04-JAGTA-HI-041   p. 18   also Ch 5
उदयपुर एक गहरी शोध परियोजना का हिस्सा हो सकता है, जिसमें राजा भोज की नगर रचना एक महत्वपूर्ण विषय है। यह चौकोर आकार में चारों तरफ से बराबर लंबी दीवारों से घिरा एक शानदार नगर रहा है, जिसके भीतर राजा के महल का होना बताता है कि यहां भोज के वारिस रहे ही होंगे और वो उद्यादित्य ही हैं, जिनके नाम से यह नगर है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udaypur could be part of a deep research project in which King Bhoja's town planning is an important subject. It has been a splendid town in a square shape enclosed by walls of equal length on all sides, the presence of a king's palace inside which shows that Bhoja's heirs must have been here, and that is Udayaditya, after whom the town is named.
C04-JAGTA-HI-042   p. 22   also Ch 7
उदयदित्य ने उदयपुर में रहते हुए अपनी गौरवशाली राज परंपरा को ही बढ़ाया था। यही वजह है कि सदियां बीतने के बावजूद वे अब तक इस पूरे क्षेत्र की स्मृतियों मैं जगमगा रहे हैं...
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udayaditya, while living in Udaypur, carried forward his glorious royal tradition. That is why, even after centuries, he still glows in the memories of this entire region.
C04-JAGTA-HI-043   p. 97
उदयपुर परमार राजवंश की भौगोलिक सीमा पर था, जो सामरिक दृष्टि से महत्वपूर्ण था। चारों तरफ दुर्ग से घिरे उदयपुर से सटे पहाड़ पर भी एक किलेबंदी के अवशेष गूगल मैप से भी दिखाई देते हैं। मुख्य मंदिर के अलावा राजमहल के विशाल परिसर की मौजूदगी बताती है कि परमार राजाओं के परिजन अपनी इस सीमा की सुरक्षा के लिए स्वयं रहे होंगे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udaypur was on the geographic border of the Paramara dynasty, which was strategically important. Remains of a fortification on the hill adjoining Udaypur, surrounded on all sides by a fort, are visible even on Google Map. Besides the main temple, the presence of the large palace complex shows that family members of the Paramara kings themselves must have been here for the security of this border.
C04-JAGTA-HI-044   p. 97   also Ch 7
वर्मा अगला सवाल लेकर आते हैं कि उदयपुर का निर्माण वास्तव में किसने किया? उदयदित्य के समय का एक शिलालेख (उदयपुर प्रशस्ति का परवर्ती परंतु तिथि रहित) इस बारे में कुछ कीमती सुराग देता है। लेकिन इसका आधे से ज्यादा हिस्सा अत्यधिक क्षतिग्रस्त हो जाने के कारण पढ़ने लायक नहीं बचा। इस पर कोई तिथि भी नहीं है। हो सकता है वह उस हिस्से में रही हो जो बरबाद हो गया। इसकी अहमियत यही है कि यह उदयपुर नगर और मंदिर के बारे में हमें रोककर कुछ सुराग देता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Verma brings the next question: who actually built Udaypur? An inscription of Udayaditya's time (later than the Udaypur prashasti but undated) gives some valuable clues about this. But more than half of it has become unreadable because of heavy damage. It has no date either. Perhaps it was in the part that was destroyed. Its importance is that it gives clues about the town of Udaypur and the temple.
C04-JAGTA-HI-045   p. 136   also Ch 6
जहां तक उदयपुर की बात है तो इसके नाम से ही जाहिर है कि यह राजा उदयादित्य से संबंधित है। पूर्ववर्ती परमार राजाओं के किसी भी अभिलेख का यहां न मिलना यह बताता है कि यह नगर उदयादित्य के समय में ही अस्तित्व में आया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* As far as Udaypur is concerned, its very name shows it is related to King Udayaditya. That no inscription of earlier Paramara kings is found here shows that this town came into existence in Udayaditya's time.
C04-JAGTA-HI-046   p. 137   also Ch 1
पहली से छठवी पंक्ति में उदयादित्य की कुछ महत्वपूर्ण सैन्य विजयों का वर्णन है। इन विजितों में नाम केवल ढाहलमण्डल के चेदि शासक "ढाहलाधीश" का ही है। शिलालेख में दर्ज है कि ढहलाधीश को हराकर मार डाला गया। ढाहल मूलतः जबलपुर के आसपास का इलाका था जो कलचुरियों का मूलस्थान माना जाता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Lines one to six describe some important military victories of Udayaditya. Among these conquered the name only of the Chedi ruler of Dhahala-mandala, the 'Dhahaladhisha', is given. It is recorded in the inscription that the Dhahaladhisha was defeated and killed. Dhahala was originally the area around Jabalpur, which is considered the original home of the Kalachuris.
C04-JAGTA-HI-047   p. 137
इस पर कोई तिथि अंकित नहीं है परन्तु इस अभिलेख से प्राप्त जानकारी के अनुसार यह राजा भोज के उत्तराधिकारी राजा उद्यादित्य के काल का प्रतीत होता है। राजा उद्यादित्य का शासनकाल 1070 से 1093 के बीच रहा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* No date is marked on it but according to information from this inscription it appears to be of the time of King Udayaditya, successor of King Bhoja. King Udayaditya's reign was between 1070 and 1093.
C04-JAGTA-HI-048   p. 138   also Ch 6
उदयपुर को उदयदित्य ने अपने पूरे मनोयोग से रचा था। राजा भोज के ये वंशज राज्य के इस आखिरी छोर का राज्यपाल थे। उसने उदयसागर बनवाया। नीलकंठेश्वर मंदिर खड़ा किया। देश के सुंदरतम देवालयों में से एक। स्थापत्य का बेजोड़ नमूना।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udayaditya had created Udaypur with his full heart. This descendant of Raja Bhoja was the governor at this last edge of the state. He had Udayasagar built. He raised the Nilakantheshwar temple. One of the most beautiful temples of the country. A matchless specimen of architecture.
C04-JAGTA-HI-049   p. 160   also Ch 5
भूमिपूजन, शिलान्यास और गर्भगृह में अभिषेक का वह उत्सव, जब महाराज उदयादित्य स्वयं अपने मंत्रियों सहित सपरिवार यहां उपस्थित थे। धारा नगरी, उज्जयिनी, मंडपदुर्ग और भोपाल से भी अनेक प्रमुख सामंत और शिल्पी अपने शिष्यों के साथ यहां आए थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The festival of bhumipujan, foundation-laying and abhisheka in the sanctum, when Maharaj Udayaditya himself was present with ministers and family. Many chief feudatories and craftsmen with their pupils came from Dhara city, Ujjayini, Mandapadurga and Bhopal.
C04-JAGTA-HI-052   p. 27   also Ch 8
यहीं रहकर अपने राजा के लिए सदैव शुभकामनाएं व्यक्त की हों और जब मंदिर का निर्माण पूर्ण हुआ होगा तब उसने 30 मार्च 1080 के ध्वजारोहण समारोह के समय राजा के रथों का इंतजार किया हो। उदयपुर के राजमहलों की वह रौनक भी आखिरी नहीं थी। ढाई सौ साल बाद तक उदयपुर ऐसा ही चमकता रहा था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* When the temple's construction was complete she may have waited for the king's chariots at the flag-hoisting ceremony of 30 March 1080. That splendour of Udaipur's palaces was not the last. Udaipur kept shining like this for another two and a half hundred years.
C04-JAGTA-HI-058   p. 111
इस शिलालेख की बाद की पंक्तियों में परमारों के अधीन किन्हीं नेमक वंश के स्थानीय नरेशों का विवरण है। चूंकि उदयपुर सीधा परमार शासन के अंतर्गत था तो ऐसा कैसे मुमकिन है कि किसी और वंश का जिक्र आए। मुमकिन है कि राजा उदयादित्य ने अपने मातहत नेमक वंशीय किसी सामंत को यह इलाका सौंप रखा हो। इस अधूरे शिलालेख में हो सकता है कि नेमकों के बारे में और कुछ ब्यौरे भी रहे हों, लेकिन वे पढ़े जाने लायक नहीं बचे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the later lines of this inscription there is an account of local rulers of a Nemaka dynasty under the Paramaras. Since Udaypur was under direct Paramara rule, how could another dynasty be mentioned? It is possible that King Udayaditya had assigned this area to a feudatory of the Nemaka lineage. Perhaps this incomplete inscription had more details about the Nemakas, but they have not survived to be read.
C04-JAGTA-HI-059   p. 137
यह अभिलेख बताता है कि नेमक वंशीय शासक शिव के उपासक थे। शूद्रक नामक एक अन्य सामंत हुआ, जिसने किसी गुर्जर नरेश पर विजय प्राप्त की थी। इस अभिलेख में वर्णन है कि इसी शूद्रक नामक सामंत के पुत्र जिसका नाम अस्पष्ट है, ने उदयपुर में मंदिर का निर्माण करवाया था। हम यह अंदाजा ही लगा सकते हैं कि जिस समय उदयपुर मंदिर का निर्माण शुरू हुआ होगा, नेमक वंश का यह सामन्तकुल यहां का स्थानीय प्रशासक रहा हो और उसी की देखरेख में इस मंदिर का निर्माण हुआ हो।
*Gloss [my translation]:* This inscription tells that the Nemaka-line rulers were worshippers of Shiva. Another feudatory named Shudraka defeated some Gurjara king. The inscription describes that the son of this Shudraka, whose name is unclear, had a temple built at Udaypur. We can only guess that when construction of the Udaypur temple began, this feudatory family of the Nemaka line may have been the local administrator here and the temple was built under its supervision.
C04-JAGTA-HI-060   p. 137   also Ch 5, Ch 6
हम अच्छी तरह से जानते हैं कि महात्वाकांक्षी परमार राजाओं की प्रसिद्धि उनकी वीरता और युद्ध विजयों के कारण ही नहीं है। उनके समय ज्ञान की सभी विधाओं को आसरा मिला। स्थापत्य के बेजोड़ स्मारक सदियों तक बनते ही रहे। इन स्मारकों के आसपास उज्जैन, धारा, मांडू, भोजपाल, विदिशा और उदयपुर तक बड़े नॉलेज सेंटर बने, जिन्हें इन राजाओं ने ऊंचाइयों तक पहुंचाया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* We know well that the fame of ambitious Paramara kings is not only for valour and conquests. In their time all branches of knowledge found shelter. Unmatched monuments of architecture were built for centuries. Around these monuments big knowledge centres arose up to Ujjain, Dhara, Mandu, Bhojpal, Vidisha and Udaypur, which these kings raised to heights.
C04-JAGTA-HI-061   p. 137   also Ch 6
जिसने चारों ओर केदारेश्वर, रामेश्वर, सोमनाथ, सुंदीर, काल, अनल व रुद्र आदि मंदिरों का निर्माण कर पवित्र पृथ्वी को यथार्थ नाम वाली बनाया ॥२०॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* He who built on all sides temples such as Kedareshvara, Rameshvara, Somanatha, Sundira, Kala, Anala and Rudra, and made the sacred earth true to its name.
C04-JAGTA-HI-062   p. 143
बहुत मुमकिन है कि यह परमारों के उत्तराध्य का प्रसंग हो। जो जितना उभर पाया था वह वहीं ठहर गया। उदयपुर के भाग्य में परमारों का किया हुआ इतना ही लिखा था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is quite possible this is an episode of the late Paramara period. Whatever had risen stopped there. In Udaipur's fate the Paramaras' work was written only this much.
C04-JAGTA-HI-064   p. 79   also Ch 7
विजय मंदिर के रूप में महाराज उदयदित्य के बेटे और परमार राजवंश के 13 वें महाराज नरवर्मन ने विदिशा में अपने पिता से विशाल और भव्य निर्माण किया था। मगर वह सिर्फ सौ साल ही रह पाया। महान पिता की रचना एक हज़ार साल बाद भी शान से खड़ी है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* As Vijay Mandir, Naravarman, son of Maharaj Udayaditya and the 13th king of the Paramara dynasty, made a bigger and grander construction than his father at Vidisha. But it survived only a hundred years. The great father's creation still stands proudly after a thousand years.
C04-JAGTA-HI-065   p. 106
ध्यान देने वाली यह भी है कि अभिलेख की तिथि से बहुत पहले देवपालदेव राजधानी धार में राजसिंहासन पर बैठकर पूर्ण राजकीय उपाधियों का उपयोग करने लगे थे। वे राजा बनने के पहले उदयपुर में ही किसी सैन्य या प्रशासनिक जिम्मेदारी में रहे होंगे और इसीलिए इस अभिलेख में उन्हें महाकुमार की उपाधि से ही संबोधित किया गया है। अगर वे राजा बनने के पहले उदयपुर में रहे हैं तो यह तथ्य उदयपुर की अहमियत को बढ़ाने वाला है क्योंकि उनसे करीब ढेढ़ सौ साल पहले उदयादित्य भी ऐसी किसी भूमिका में रहे होंगे। अब यह भी स्पष्ट हो जाता है कि उदयपुर एक ऐसा केंद्र था जहां से कम से कम दो युवराज तो परमार राजवंश में राजा बनकर निकले थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is worth noting that long before the date of the inscription Devapaladeva had sat on the throne in the capital Dhar and begun using full royal titles. Before becoming king he must have held some military or administrative responsibility at Udaypur, and hence in this inscription he is addressed only by the title Mahakumara. If he lived at Udaypur before becoming king, this fact raises Udaypur's importance, because about a hundred and fifty years earlier Udayaditya too must have been in such a role. Now it also becomes clear that Udaypur was a centre from which at least two princes of the Paramara dynasty emerged as kings.
C04-JAGTA-HI-068   p. 146   also Ch 6
## Corpus Inscriptionum Indicarum, Vol. VII, Part 2: Inscriptions of the Paramāras — Trivedi, Harihar Vithal (ed.) (CII, 40)
The inscription then proceeds to trace the genealogy of the Paramāra house of Mālwā. It tells us in vv. 5-6 that a hero who had sprung from the fire-altar of Vasishtha on the Mount Arbuda (Ābū) slew the enemies and brought back the cow of the sage which Viśvāmitra had taken away, and was, in reward of this deed, given the name of Paramāra (slayer of the foes) by Vasishtha who also blessed him with kingship. \( ^{2} \)  This account is purely legendary, a later creation, and can mean nothing more than to invent a high origin for the house, calling the Paramāras as belonging to agni-kula, exactly similar to that of the races born from the Sun and the moon. \( ^{3} \)  The present inscription, as we may note here, is the first to mention this legend, which is echoed in the later epigraphical records of the house, e.g., in the Nagpur Museum inscription and the Mändhātā copper-plate grant of Jayasimha II, as we shall see below.
C04-CII-020   p. 131
Verses 8-11 mention the names of Vairisinha, Siyaka, Vākpati and another (a second) Vairisinha, each of whom was the son of his predecessor. The description here is purely conventional, except that we learn that the last of these rulers, viz., Vairisinha, was also known as Vajraṭa. Indirectly it also suggests that Vākpati fixed his capital at Dhārā. Here it is worth noting that of all these five rulers from Upēndrāraja to Vairisinha, the Nagpur prasasti gives only the last name, the Tilakamaṇāyari only that of Vairisinha, and the Navasāhasānika-charita which gives the name of Upēndrāraja, omits those of Vairisinha (I) and Siyaka (I). Thus the present inscription is the only record which gives a complete genealogy of the earlier Paramāra rulers of Mālwā, as already stated before.
C04-CII-021   p. 132
The next three verses (13-15) speak of Siyaka's son Vākpatirāja, who is stated to have been well-versed in the Sāstras and a poet too. The N.S. Charita calls him Utpalarāja (XI, 92). In the Nagpur prasasti we have the name Muñja instead of Vākpati; and Hall recognises first the identity of the two names. The land-grants call him Vākpati and add that he was also known as Amōghavarsha and bore the titles of Prithvī-vallabha and Śrī-vallabha. The praise given by the present prasasti to Vākpati is not undeserved, as we know from the various quotations of his verses in anthologies, works on alaṅkāvas, the Prabandhacintāmaṇi and the Bhōjaprabandha, etc., though some of these may be of a doubtful nature.
C04-CII-022   p. 132   also Ch 6
In verse 8, which begins the genealogy, the inscription mentions the name Upēndrarāja, adding that he was born in the lineage of Paramāra and that “he acquired the high honour of kinghood by his prowess”. This indirectly goes to indicate that he was the first of the Paramāra kings of Mālwā. The land grants, on the other hand, mention the originator of the house to be Krishnarāja; and Dr. Hall and Cunningham have therefore taken both these names to be identical. Bühler also drew our attention to the fact that both these names are identical, and he further suggested that Upēndrarāja was probably the poetical form of the name and Krishnarāja was its Prakrit equivalent to be used in every day life.
C04-CII-023   p. 132
Bhōjadēva, is eulogised in the prāśasti for his strength, charities, victories and temple-building activities, in vv. 16-20. The assertion of the prāśastikāra that Bhōja resembled Prithu' and ruled the earth from Kailāsa (in the north) to Malaya-gri (in the south), and from the mountain where the Sun rises to that where it sets (east and west respectively) is merely a poetic way of expression which is absolutely worthless for historical purposes. Verse 18 alludes to his knowledge and endows him with the title of kavurāja. The praise is not undeserved, since, as we know, a number of works were composed by Bhōja himself and his protégés. Verse 19 enumerates Bhōja's military exploits and victories won by him over the lord of Chēdis, Indraratha, Toggala(?) Bhūma, the king of the Gurjaras, the lord of Lāṭa, the Karnāṭas and the Turushkas.
C04-CII-069   p. 133
Verse 20 of the prāsasti states that Bhōja ‘covered the world all around with temples dedicated to Kēdāra, Rāmēśvara, Sōmanātha, Sunḍīra (?), Kāla, Anala and Rudra.’ The first three of these names appear to be intended to refer, respectively, to the well-known Śiva temples in the north, south and west; and following the same, the fourth, which is a strange and so-far-unknown name, may possibly have been one in the east. Kāla, the fifth of the names enumerated here, may possibly be taken to be an abbreviated form of Mahākāla, at Ujjain; and the last two of the names, i.e., Anala and Rudra, which also are synonymous with Śiva are very common and cannot be specified. Commenting on this statement of the prāśasti, Bühler has rightly remarked that it cannot be corroborated, \( ^{5} \)  and agreeing with him, we are also inclined to hold that the choice of these names may have been made with a view to impress what the prāśasti has already stated in v.
C04-CII-070   p. 134
His last date cannot be accurately ascertained: but his latest known date is furnished by the Tilakawādā grant which was issued in V.S. 1103 or 1047 A.C.\(^{6}\)
C04-CII-071   p. 134
Bhōjadēva's earliest epigraphic date is supplied by the Mōdāsā grant of V.S. 1067 or 1011 A.C.
C04-CII-072   p. 134
The name of Jayasiniha is not known from any other inscription of the house—not even from the Udaipur and Nagpur prasats which give an almost complete list of kings ruling at Dhara up to the time when they were engraved. On the other hand, Bhojadêva is generally taken to have been succeeded by Udayadilva who is now definitely known to be his brother. But the present record, which mentions Jayasimha as the immediate successor of Bhojadêva and the evidence of which cannot be doubted for any reason, definitely shows that Jayasiniha must be placed between Bhojadêva and Udayadilva and thus between 1047 and 1080 A.C. which are respectively the latest and the earliest known years of these two rulers.
C04-CII-076   p. 108
The inscription thus reveals for the first time a new historical fact, viz. that Lakshmadēva predeceased both Udayāditya and Naravarman, though the circumstances leading to his death, along with the approximate time when it took place, are not known. Here we have also to note that the Nagpur Museum stone inscription, which enumerates the names of all the rulers from Vairisinha to Lakshmadēva, while introducing each one of them, uses such a word as signifies 'a king', e.g., kshutipati, bhūpati or ūripa, but such a word is not used in it in the case of the last of them only, i.e. Lakshmadēva, though it states that he was devoted to govern the subjects. \( ^{4} \)  The omission of a word denoting a king only with the name of Lakshmadēva in the record is significant as it perhaps implies to show that he never ascended the throne of Dhāra. That he worked as governor in some of the eastern part of the Paramāra dominions under his father Udayāditya is also suggested by the donation made by him as recorded in the same inscription.
C04-CII-077   p. 117
The inscription refers itself to Udayāditya who is endowed in it with the titles of Paramabhattāraka, Mahāvājādhiraja and Paramēśvara, and is stated to have then encamped at the village Dhavalī near Ujjain. \( ^{1} \)  The purpose of the inscription is to record the donation made by Naravarman, who was evidently his son, of 12 halas (?) of land in the village known as Rādhaghatikā, for perpetually burning a lamp (akshaya-dīpikā) in honour of Lakshmadēva who was another son of Udayāditya and thus a brother of Naravarman. The year, mentioned in numerical figures in the last line of the inscription, is 1140, without any further details. As the current Vikrama year, it corresponds to 1082 A.C. The name of the engraver is not recorded, nor is any epithet attached either to the name of Naravarman or to that of Lakshmadēva.
C04-CII-078   p. 117
Lakshmadēva and Naravarman are the well-known sons of Udayāditya: but the expression denoting the purpose of the donation made here calls our special attention. It is akshaya-dīpi-kāthē, which appears to have been used for its longer and complete form, viz., akshaya-lōka-dīpi-kāthē, in the sense of “a lamp giving perpetual happiness in heaven”. And taken in this sense, which appears to be the only possibility, the inscription contains a very important historical reference, leading us to conclude that Lakshmadēva had died a short time before the record was executed. Naravarman had a great affection for his brother Lakshmadēva, as we know from the Nagpur Museum stone inscription which was composed by the former ruler who devoted as many as 19 stanzas giving fulsome praise to the description of Lakshmadēva.
C04-CII-079   p. 117
So far as the genealogy is concerned, the present record adds only the name of Udayāditya, who is mentioned here to have been a successor of Bhōjadēva. And it is now well known that he was the brother of Bhōjadēva.\(^{2}\) It may also be observed here that excepting the formal portion, the present record is almost a copy of the Māndhātā grant of Jayasimha, whom we know to have occupied the throne after Bhōjadēva and before Udayādityadēva.
C04-CII-080   p. 126
The following verse (21) introduces Udayāditya, who is spoken of as 'another Sun dispelling the dense darkness in the form of his magnanimous foes with the rays issuing from his strong sword and thereby gladdening the hearts of his people by his splendour'. The enemies referred to here are not specified, but the description goes to indicate that they were very powerful and had deprived the Paramāras of their kingdom in the last days of Bhōja, or immediately after his death. From the Prabandhachintāmaṇi we learn that in the last days of Bhōjadēva the Kalachuri Karṇa and the Chaulukya Bhīma attacked Mālwā simultaneously and took possession of his kingdom. This statement is corroborated by epigraphical records of the Paramāras. The Nagpur prasasti, which is more explicit on the point, states that Udayāditya resembled the Great Boar in delivering the earth (i.e., the Paramāra kingdom) which had submerged in the mighty oceans in the form of the Karṇāta, Karṇa and others (prabhriti). This description agrees very closely with that of the Mándhātā grant of Jayasinīha II, though the enemy mentioned there was only the Gurjara king.
C04-CII-081   p. 134
Kalachuri Karṇa, (2) the Chaulukya Bhīma and (3) the Hoysala Ereyānga, \( ^{1} \)  and it need not be repeated here. And the credit of vanquishing them all goes to Udayāditya, whose relations with Bhōjadēva are not stated in the inscription but who was his brother, as the Dōngargāon inscription informs us.
C04-CII-082   p. 135
The next two verses (5-6) introduce Bhōja's brother Udayāditya, who uplifted the kingdom of Mālava which had sunk under the attacks of three enemies. This information is historically important in two ways, viz., in showing Udayāditya's definite relationship with Bhōja and also in giving the exact number of the
C04-CII-083   p. 151
As for the relationship of Udayāditya with Bhōja, we find that some of the Paramāra records describe the former of these rulers as meditating on the feet of the latter; and the Udaypur prasasti, which is explicit in stating the relationship of each of the princes mentioned in it with his predecessor, simply says that Bhōja was followed on the throne by Udayāditya. \( ^{1} \)  The Nagpur Museum stone inscription of Naravarman introduces Udayāditya as a bandhu of Bhōjadēva; \( ^{2} \)  and as the word bandhu signifies ‘a relative in general’, Kielhorn was right in taking the word as such, \( ^{3} \)  and this view has been adopted by scholars since his time.
C04-CII-084   p. 152
7) introduces Jagaddēva who is stated to have born to Udayāditya in consequence of the latter’s adoration to Hara whom he requested to give him a son after his heart, though he had some (more than two). \( ^{6} \)  The next verse states that the Royal Fortune offered herself to Jagaddēva, but he renounced this favour, apprehending that to surpass his elder brother would be incurring a sin.
C04-CII-085   p. 152
His father was king Udayāditya, and his paternal uncle the king Bhōjadēva, both of whom shone like the sun and the moon. \( ^{1} \)  In the preceding inscription we have already discussed how Jagaddēva was related to Udayāditya and the latter's brother was the illustrious Bhōjadēva who ruled in Mālwā in the first half of the eleventh century A.C.
C04-CII-086   p. 157
From the next inscription we know that this ruler succeeded his father Udayādiya in V.S. 1151 current; and in the light of this information, we are justified in believing that the present record must have been incised in V.S. 1151 expired; and this is the only way in which we can reconcile the date of the present record with that of the next inscription. \( ^{1} \)  Accordingly, Friday, the 23rd June, 1094 A.C. seems to be the true equivalent of the date, and following this, we may reject both the other solutions.
C04-CII-087   p. 164
The following four verses are devoted to describe the Paramāra family and three of the rulers belonging to it viz., Vākpati, Siyaka and Udayāditya. The description is wholly conventional. It is not known if some other names, with that of Bhōjadeva, who was the most illustrious ruler belonging to that family, are lost in the missing portion. However, as all the rulers mentioned here are known from the other records of the house, the present inscription does not add to our knowledge so far as the genealogy of the house is concerned.
C04-CII-088   p. 165   also Ch 6
From the Pānāhedā inscription dated V. 1116, we know that Manḍalika, the ruler of Vāgaḍa was greatly devoted to Jayasinha, the immediate successor of Bhōja. Presuming that the same policy may have been followed by his son Chāmunḍarāja, he too may have helped Jayasinha by destroying some of the forces of the latter's adversary Udayāditya or some other person who was then struggling against Jayasinha, and also possibly some others, for the throne.
C04-CII-106   p. 466
In view of the information furnished by the present inscription, we may also hold that it was Jayasiniha who put an end to the troubles befelling on the Paramāra kingdom on account of the simultaneous attack of the Kalahcuri Karna (c. 1041-1072 A.C.) and the Chaulukya Bhūma I (1022-1064 A.C.), when Bhōjadeva is known to have died. \( ^{2} \)  Jayasiniha, for whom it was difficult to resist the combined attack of two mighty foes, solicited the aid of the powerful Chālukva king Sōmēśvara I (1013-1068 A.C.) on this occasion. Sōmēśvara sent his son Vikramādiitya VI to help Jayasiniha and the former turned back the invaders and secured the throne for the latter, breaking the confederacy. Karna's invasion of Mālava which is graphically described in the Nagpur museum stone inscription and which was reverted by Udayādiiva, was thus, in fact, his second attack some fifteen years later. That Karna undertook two invasions—the first when Bhōja died and the second, about fifteen years later when Udavādiiva saved the country, is well shown by Dr.
C04-CII-107   p. 108
As stated above, the importance of the inscription lies in the fact that it supplies the earliest known date for the king Udayāditya, who, according to the prasasti found at the same place, rescued the earth (i.e., the Paramāra kingdom), as the primeval boar, the significance of which will be seen in its proper place.
C04-CII-108   p. 113   also Ch 6
The object of the inscription is to record paying adoration to the image, i.e., its consecration, by one Jasahara of the Lāra caste. The year which is given at the beginning in numerical figures only, is 1138. As an expired year of the northern Vikrama era, it would correspond to 1081 A.C. No other details of the year are mentioned in the inscription.
The name of the king during whose reign the image was consecrated is also missing; but from the find-spot we take him to be the Paramāra Udayāditya who is known from the preceding inscription which is dated only one year before. Thus the image is a specimen of the statutary art of the time of this king.
C04-CII-109   p. 116
The important part of the inscription is the statement that the temple was built and the record was put up when Udayāditya was ruling in the region around. We find that neither any epithet or title is attached to his name nor is given the name of the house to which he belonged. But that he was the well-known member of the Paramāra house ruling at Dhāra is obvious from the mention of his name in some other records of the house engraved about the same time and coming from the region around, for example, from the Udaipur inscription of V.S. 1137, put up only six years before the present record.
C04-CII-110   p. 121
of Udaipur in the District of Vidishā ; and in view of all this, it is obvious that the kingdom of Udayāditya, who had recovered Mālwā from the enemies, as to be seen below, included the whole of the region at least up to Jhālrapātān and Kōtā in the north and a part of the modern District of Vidishā in the north-east.
C04-CII-111   p. 124
To notice the contents of the inscription, we find that the first few letters, which can be read with certainty, speak of Udayāditya's bravery and his glorious success against the lord of the Chēdīsa (Dāhalādhiśa) whom he routed completely. This is evidently a reference to his success against the Kalachuri Karṇa who had attacked the Paramāra dominions with the sole aim of obliterating it altogether, as we have often been seeing. The genealogy of the Paramāras, which we find in the earlier part of the prasasti, stops with the mention of this king.
C04-CII-112   p. 139
The only geographical name mentioned in the record is Udayapura, which is said to be the capital town of the Nēmaka dynasty (1. 6), which was subordinate to Udayāditya. This place still bears this name, as we have already seen.
C04-CII-113   p. 139
The other important information furnished by verse 5 of the present inscription concerns the number of enemies to be three who attacked Mālava, which was rescued by Udayāditya by defeating them all. As we shall see while editing the Nagpur Museum stone inscription, \( ^{5} \) these three enemies were the Kalachuri Karna (1041-1072 A.C.) and the Chālukya Sōmēśvara II (1069-1076 A.C.), the son of Āhavamalla-Sōmēśvara I, who had despatched his general, the Ganga Udayāditya, and his feudatory the Hoysala Ereyanga. This was the second Kalachuri invasion of Mālwā, the first being the one which occurred some fifteen years previously when Bhōja died and his successor Jayasinha I succeeded in driving out the allied armies of the Kala-churi Karna and the Chaulukya Bhūma with the help of Vikramāditya VI.
C04-CII-114   p. 152
The record is silent as to the house to which this king belonged, as we also find in the Atru and Bhilsa inscriptions, edited above; but it is evident that he was a member of the royal Paramāra house of Mālava which held this region under its sway from the time of Udavāditya, whose records were found quite near by, at Udaipur, which is about 15 kms. distant from the find-spot of the present inscription.
C04-CII-117   p. 317
The last line of the record expresses the purpose, in which the temple is mentioned by the name Udayésvaradeva, evidently after the name of its constructor. And with the expressions ‘auspiciousness, great fortune’, the record comes to an end.
C04-CII-119   p. 113   also Ch 5, Ch 6
The inscription, after the words “Om. adoration to Siva,” has the date Samvat 1143, the tenth of the bright half of Vaisākha. Taking the year as Chaturādī, the date corresponds to 26th April 1086 A.C. and for Kārtṭikādī, to 7th April, to 1087. It cannot be verified. The record then mentions the name of Udayādītyadāva, who is stated to have been then prosperous in the region: and, following this, it records the proper object which was the construction of a temple of Sambhu by the pattakla (patēl) Jannaka, who was a son of pattakila Chāhila and of tailikānvaya, i.e. of oilman's lineage (ll. 3-4).
C04-CII-120   p. 121
The object of the inscription is to record the royal endowment of a village situated in the Chachchurōni maṇḍala, by Udayāditya, who was then encamped at the village Karpāsiṅā, in favour of the God Sōmanāthadēva on the fort of Kōśavardhana.
C04-CII-121   p. 126
Following this, we have the description of the members of the Nēmaka dynasty.\(^{1}\)
Owing to the imperfect nature of the record, the names of the ruling princes of this dynasty cannot be made out completely; and the object of the inscription appears to be that Udaipur was placed in charge of a king who belonged to this dynasty, and to record that (he, or during his reign was) constructed a temple, evidently the one referred to in it. The father of this king was Sūdraka, who is said to have vanquished the king of Gujarāt, possibly to be identified with the Chaulukya king Karna, whose dates range from 1064 to 1094 A.C., or, his successor Bhima.
C04-CII-122   p. 139
Verses 13-15 introduce an illustrious personage named Lōlārka who was born in the renowned family of the Dāhimas and was a “repository of fame and bravery incarnate”. He was a minister of Udayāditya (the Paramāra king). His grandfather was Mahēndu and father Gunarāja, who resembled Arjuna and was dear to Udayāditya.
C04-CII-123   p. 158
Verses 14-15 of the present record state that “his lotus-feet were coloured by the jewels on the heads of the Karnnāṭas, Lāṭas, Kéralas and Chōlas and he vanquished Yuvarāja, and slaying his general, as a victor, raised on high his sword in Tripuri.” The Kalachuri Yuvarāja of Tripuri (c. 980-990) who was Vākpati’s contemporary was a weak ruler, quite unlike his father Lakshmaṇa; and Vākpati may have come into a clash with Yuvarāja whose kingdom lay immediately to the east of Mālwā and scored a victory over him, though of a fleeting nature. It has to be admitted here, however, that we have no evidence in support of this assertion, nor is it possible to fix the date of this clash. With reference to Vākpati’s victory over the Karnātaṣ (evidently the Chālukyas), we know that in his long struggle with his adversary Trailapa II, he had to lose his throne and life in the end; and the claim of the prasasti that Vākpati subdued the Karnāṭas is evidently inadmissible. It is however possible that he may have gained some success in one of his earlier expeditions against the region and that success may have been referred to here.
C04-CII-129   p. 132
Bühler contends that the story of Bhōja's reverse narrated by the Prabandhachintāmaṇi and also by the epigraphical records of the house cannot be implicitly accepted because it does not find place in the inscriptions of the Kalachuris; nor does Hémachandra, who extols Bhima 'in the best possible light' and who wrote his Dvyāśraya-kāvya about 150 years before Mērutunga, say anything about it. But here we are to remember that it was a long-drawn war and the story does not end with the reverse sustained by Bhōja but with the 'total destruction (saṅhāra) of the Kalachuri king (Dāhal-ādhiśa) at the hands of Udayāditya,' as we know from 1.2 of the following inscription, which is in continuation of the inscription in hand and was discovered 37 years after Bühler worked out his article.
The next verse (22), which is the last complete verse of the prasasti, again glorifies Udayā-ditya, stating that 'for him who restored the earth (from the enemies) it was easy to restore the Premial Boar.' This statement appears to be a hint to the restoration of a Boar-temple by Udayāditya, as we shall see in the inscription that follows (Part B).
C04-CII-130   p. 135
134. This inscription is from Udaipur in the Vidishā District and is dated V. S. 1562. The find-spot of this record made Dr. Ganguly inclined to hold that Udayāditya who was a scion of the junior branch of the Paramāra house was a feudatory of the Imperial house of Dhārā and was ruling at Udaipur.
C04-CII-131   p. 152
Thus the information conveyed by the present inscription in clear words, viz., that Udayāditya was a brother of Bhōjadēva is indeed valuable; and in view of this, the altogether different genealogy of the former of these kings recorded in a sixteenth century inscription mentioning him to have been the son of Gyātā, grandson of Gōndala and great-grandson of Śūravīra of the Paramāra family, \( ^{4} \)  has to be rejected. The relationship between the two rulers is stated in very clear words also in the following inscription, as we shall see presently.
C04-CII-132   p. 152
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 35)
There we have noticed that Upendra was the first king of this dynasty, and was a subordinate chief of the Râstrakûta Govinda III. Both the Udayapur Prasasti \( ^{1} \) and the Navasâhasânka-carita \( ^{2} \) state that Upendra was a king of great valour, and was particularly famous for performing a ‘multitude of sacrifices.” The first record further tells us that the king reduced the burden of taxes borne by his subjects.
C04-GANGULY-023   p. 42   also Ch 6
Siyaka was also known as Harsa. \( ^{2} \)  It is now beyond all dispute that Siyaka and Harsa were names of one and the same king, as both are described in the epigraphic records as the father of Sindhurâja. \( ^{3} \)  The Udayapur Prasasti and the Arthuna inscription mention him as Harsa, \( ^{4} \)  and Merutunga calls him by the name Simhadantabhaṭa. \( ^{5} \)  But in the rest of the ancient records the king is introduced to us as Siyaka.
C04-GANGULY-030   p. 51   also Ch 6
Dhanapāla, in the concluding verses of his Pāiyalacchī, states that he completed this work "when one thousand years of the Vikrama era and twenty-nine besides had passed, and at the time when Mānyakheda (or Mānyakheta) was plundered in consequence of an attack by the lord of Mālava."
The date expressed here in the Vikrama era corresponds to 971-2 A. D. It has been noticed that at this time Khottīga was on the throne of Mānyakheta. The Malwa king referred to above was, in all probability, Sīyaka, as the Udayapur Prasasti makes mention of his victory over Khottīga.
C04-GANGULY-032   p. 56   also Ch 6
Vakpati next directed his victorious troops towards Tripuri, the capital of the Kalacuris, and within a short time captured that city. The Udayapur prasasti tells us that Vakpati defeated Yuvarâja in battle, slaughtered his generals, and established his supremacy in Tripuri. \( ^{2} \)  This is corroborated in general by the Kauthem grant \( ^{3} \)  of the Câlukya Vikramâditya V, in which it is stated that Utpala destroyed the power of the Caidyas, i.
C04-GANGULY-039   p. 64   also Ch 6
But in these dark days there was one ray of light on the eastern horizon, which gradually expanded, and dispersed all the black clouds from the sky of Malwa. Udayāditya, a scion of a junior branch of the Paramāras, stood gallantly for the liberation of his ancestral dominion. In those perilous hours he appealed to the Câhamâna Durlabha III, the king of Sâkambhari, for assistance. After the discomfiture of the Câhamâna Viryarâma by Bhoja, a friendly relation seems to have been established between the houses of Sâkambhari and Dhârâ. Hence Durlabha lost no time in sending a cavalry force to the assistance of the Paramâras. Udayāditya thus being sufficiently re-inforced fell upon the combined forces of the Caulukyas and the Karnâtas, and within a short time succeeded in regaining the country.
C04-GANGULY-076   p. 145
The Nagpur inscription \( ^{2} \) makes matters clearer by stating that “when he (Bhoja) had become Indra’s companion, and when the realm was overrun by floods in which its sovereign was submerged, his relation, Udayâditya, became king. Uplifting the earth, which was troubled by kings and taken possession of by Karna, who, joined by the Karmâtas, was like the mighty ocean, he acted like the holy Boar.”
C04-GANGULY-077   p. 146
was the cause of the prosperity of Udayāditya of Mâlava. Udayāditya, having received cavalry from him defeated the Gurjara Kārṇa, and obtained Mâlava.
C04-GANGULY-078   p. 146
An inscription from Udayapur, in Bhilsa, furnishes us with more detailed information regarding his pedigree. It relates that there was a prince named Sauravira, (Śūravīra) a member of the Paramāra (Pāvara) family, who carved out a principality of his own in Mālava. He was learned, pious, liberal, valiant, and possessed of both his own and his enemies' armies. His son was Gondala, who was a receptacle of noble qualities, devoted to the Gods, a conqueror of his enemies, and famous for his own deeds. His son was Gyātā (Jñātā), whose son again was Udayāditya, the great destroyer of the enemy (ari-bala-mathana), who, having obtained the eastern territory, proceeded to the interior of Mālava,\(^{2}\) which was formerly governed by his ancestors. The country was made glorious by him.\(^{3}\)
C04-GANGULY-079   p. 147   also Ch 6
It is evident from the above verses that the authority of the Paramāras in Malwa, though it received a severe blow at the hand of its foreign enemies, was soon revived by a hero known as Udayāditya. He is described in the Udayapur prasasti as a relation of Bhoja.
C04-GANGULY-080   p. 147
It follows from the above record that Udayâditya and his predecessors belonged to a junior branch of the Paramâra family. They seem to have been ruling as feudatories of the Imperial house of Dhârâ, in the east of Malwa, at Udayapur, in Bhilsa.
C04-GANGULY-081   p. 148
The Ràs Mâlâ² relates that ‘Oodayâdit’ (Udayâditya) had two wives, one belonging to the Solunkhee (Solânki) dynasty and the other to the Wâghelâ clan. By the Solânki queenen he had a son named Jug Dev (Jagaddeva), and by the Vâghelâ queen another son called Rindhuwul (Ranadhavala).
C04-GANGULY-083   p. 153
Apart from its mythological character, it is chronologically inaccurate. Jug Dev could not have ruled for fifty-two years, since Udayāditya's second son, Naravarman, began his reign some time before 1094 A. D.
C04-GANGULY-084   p. 154
But notwithstanding these discrepancies, it cannot be denied that Udayāditya had a son named Jagaddeva, who, for some time, became the king of Malwa.
C04-GANGULY-085   p. 154
The Belava inscription of Bhojavarman, \( ^{3} \) the king of Eastern Bengal, states that Jagadvijayamalla was the son of Udayi (Udayâditya), who was a great conqueror in this world. Dr. Haraprasad Śāstrī rightly suggests that Jagadvijayamalla of the above inscription is the same as Jagaddeva of Malwa.
C04-GANGULY-086   p. 155
Udayāditya had connections with many of the neighbouring rulers. His daughter, Śyamala Devī, was married to Vijayasimha, son of Vairisimha and grandson of Hamsapāla, of the Gobhila (Guhila) family.
C04-GANGULY-087   p. 155
\( ^{3} \)  The tradition runs that Jug Deb was his immediate successor to the throne, but a contemporary Paramāra record states that Laksmadeva became king of Malwa after Udayāditya's death. \( ^{4} \)  Jagaddeva's name is not mentioned in any Paramāra inscription. But that he ruled in Malwa for some years, about this time, is borne out by the two Hoysala inscriptions referred to above. In these circumstances it may therefore be coojectured that the names Jagaddeva and Laksmadeva were those of one and the same person.
C04-GANGULY-088   p. 156
was married to Guhila king. Jagaddeva gave his daughter in marriage to a Varman king of East Bengal.
C04-GANGULY-089   p. 252
It is stated that Jagaddeva's father was Udayā-ditya, and his paternal uncle (pitrīya) was Bhoja.
C04-GANGULY-091   p. 379
If Udayâditya is supposed to have ascended the throne of Mâlava, having defeated the Caulukya Kārṇa, he cannot be taken to have ruled in 1059 A. D. as a sovereign lord, because Kārṇa had not yet assumed the kingly power. This difficulty can be surmounted by assuming that Udayâditya built the Udayapur temple in 1059 A. D., when he was ruling his paternal territory apparently from Udayapur.
C04-GANGULY-092   p. 144
VII, p. 35), states that Udayâditya built the temple of Udayapur in V. S. 1116=1059 A. D. The record is very perfunctorily executed, and abounds in gross mistakes.
C04-GANGULY-093   p. 144   also Ch 5
Udayâditya is represented as the son of the last; and he is distinctly stated to have been ruling in Sam 1116, or Šaka 981.
C04-GANGULY-094   p. 148
From the evidence of these inscriptions, it may fairly be inferred that the kingdom over which Udayâditya held sway extended at least up to Jhalrapatan on the north, Bhilsa on the east, and the Nimar District on the south. This proves that he succeeded in regaining almost the whole of the territory over which his predecessor Bhoja ruled in the latter part of his reign.
C04-GANGULY-095   p. 150
After the restoration of peace in Malwa, Udayāditya had to encounter another invasion of the Cālukyas, Sōmesvara II was overthrown by his younger brother, Vikramāditya VI, in 1076 A. D. Vikramāditya, who was an ally of Jayasimha, turned an enemy of Udayāditya. He seems to have led a successful campaign against Malwa, shortly after his accession to the throne. An inscription, dated 1077 A. D., from Shikarpur Taluq, records that "he was the source of a great fever of terror to the king of Dhārā."
C04-GANGULY-096   p. 151
It will be admitted by everyone that Udayāditya's career as a king was one of unique success. He came as a boon to the Paramāras in the day of their worst calamity. The most difficult and responsible task with which he was faced, was, amidst highly adverse circumstances, conducted by him to a successful issue. He restored peace and order, and an atmosphere suitable to the peaceful pursuit of the daily avocations of life. As a true successor to the throne of Muñja and Bhoja, he paid equal attention to the healthy advancement of the literary movement. The judicious care which he took for the furtherance of education is unique in its character.
C04-GANGULY-097   p. 152
Udayāditya closed his reign shortly after 1086 A. D.
C04-GANGULY-098   p. 156
Some gold coins, apparently of the reign of Udayāditya, have been discovered in the Central Provinces. They are similar in type to those of the Kalacuri Gāngeyadeva. The legend on the obverse is \( ^{4} \) —
C04-GANGULY-099   p. 253   also Ch 8
The king Udayâditya founded the city of Udayapur, thirty miles to the north of Bhilsa.
C04-GANGULY-100   p. 266
The above story is no doubt an absolute myth, and there is evidence, in the words of a contemporary inscription,' that the city was built by Udayāditya. Udayapur is now once again only a small village, but there remain numerous traces of its ancient glory.
C04-GANGULY-101   p. 267
The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{4} \) credits him with the building of a large number of temples. To-day very few remains of his extensive work can be traced, owing to the fact that, since the final conquest of Malwa by the Moslems, a large number of ancient Hindu temples have been converted into mosques by the new rulers.
C04-GANGULY-104   p. 135   also Ch 7
Udayaditya showed unflagging zeal also in the building of architectural monuments. He laid the foundation of the city of Udayapur in Bhilsa, and erected there the great temple of Nilakanthesvara.
C04-GANGULY-105   p. 151
Another inscription, \( ^{1} \) discovered in the same temple, credits him with the foundation of Udayapur, Udayesvara, and Udayasamudra. Udayesvara is perhaps the same as Nilakanthesvara.
C04-GANGULY-106   p. 152   also Ch 7
Naravarman was himself a poet and a patron of those who devoted their lives to literary activities, giving them both sympathy and material support. He composed many hymns and verses. \( ^{2} \)  He built many temples, though few remains of them can be traced now. According to the evidence of epigraphic records, he carried on his architectural activities in Nagpur \( ^{3} \)  and Bhilsa.
C04-GANGULY-107   p. 177
Brahmanism: The Paramâra kings were devout worshippers of Śiva. The sovereigns who supported this worship by donations include Śiyaka-Harsa, Vâkpati, Bhoja, Jayasimha, Arjunavarman, Devapâla, and Jayavarman II.7 Udayâditya built a temple to Śiva at Udayapur.
C04-GANGULY-109   p. 256   also Ch 8
province of the Caulukyas, who this time established their authority over it on a firmer basis. During this period the imperial Paramâra dynasty became lost in obscurity. There was no longer a “Udayâditya” in Malwa who could offer a strong resistance to the forces of the Caulukyas. Hence the inevitable consequences followed. The Paramâras remained submerged in the depth of the deluge that flooded Malwa for a long period of years.
C04-GANGULY-132   p. 188   also Ch 10
All these inscriptions of Devapâla, together with those of Arjunavarman, go to prove that, in the early part of the thirteenth century A. D., the Paramâra kingdom extended at least as far as Udayapur on the east, Hoshangabad and Nimar Districts on the south, and the Broach District on the west.

## Some Paramāra Temples From Madhya Pradesh: A Case Study of Village Un — Swati Mondal Adhikari (PARTEMPLES, 5)
Udayāditya (c.A.D.1070-c.A.D.1093), who became the Paramāra king after Jayasimha's tragic end in the war against Somesvara II of the Western Čalukya dynasty, was an able general. He restored the lost fortune of the family by recovering the kingdom from the grip of hostile enemies. He defeated Kārṇa, king of Gujarat and subdued the revolting feudatories. The resultant political stability witnessed the efflorescence of culture of Mālava particularly in the domain of art and literature.
C04-PARTEMPLES-005   p. 9
Udayāditya's signal achievement was the construction of the Nilakanthésvara, also known as Udayeśvara, Śiva temple at Udaypura (modern Udaypur), the city named after him.
C04-PARTEMPLES-006   p. 9   also Ch 5
The in situ inscription on the temple records that the hoisting of flag by Udyāditya on the temple took place in 1080 A.D.
C04-PARTEMPLES-007   p. 9   also Ch 5
The Nilakan theśvara, representing the Bhūmija style in its fully developed form, was completed in A.D. 1080.
C04-PARTEMPLES-009   p. 45   also Ch 5
On the other hand, royal temples were continued to be erected in Udaypur, Vidisha, Mandsaur, Nemawar etc.
C04-PARTEMPLES-010   p. 47
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 5)
Bhoja's cousin or brother, Udayaditya came to the throne probably before the year A.D. 1080 and reigned till 1086. The eastern boundary of his kingdom extended upto Vidisha where he built the famous Nilkanthsavar temple at Udaypur.\(^{2}\) The next king Lakshman seems to have retained Vidisha, since he was a great king and a conqueror.

was erected in Vikrama Samvat 1137 corresponding to A.D. 1080. Thus these records make it abundantly clear that the Udayeshwara temple was constructed by the order of king Udayaditya of the Paramara dynasty, between the years A.D. 1059 and 1080.

## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 3)
It is a Śaiva temple built in the second half of the 11th century CE by Udayāditya Paramāra who was a scion of the illustrious Paramāra dynasty that ruled over Malwa.
Udayāditya ascended the throne in 1070 CE and had to face many challenges in consolidating his empire.
C04-PANDE-005   p. 8
Pratipala Bhatia, The Paramāras (c. 800-1305 A.D.) (New Delhi: Munshiram Manoharlal, 1970), 109. Even though Karṇa succeeded in invading Malwa in 1079 CE, he was ultimately defeated by the combined forces of Udayāditya Paramāra and his allies.
C04-PANDE-006   p. 95
During Udayāditya’s reign, Malwa witnessed political stability and flourished as a leading centre of religious and cultural activities. Udayāditya extended patronage to art and architecture. He founded the city of Udayapur which is located in modern day Vidisha. In this city, he constructed several temples, the most famous being the temple of Udayeśvara.

## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 2)

# Chapter 5. The Udayeśvara Temple: Architecture, Sculpture and Religious Iconography
## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 124)
The temple is built of red sandstone. It faces east and consists of a *garbhagṛha* (sanctum), an *antarāla* (vestibule), a *gūḍhamandapa* (covered hall for devotees) with three *mukhamandapas* (porches) and a low flat-roofed hall called *vedī*, separate, but on the same axis (fig. 1, pl. 2). It stood on an extensive, lofty *jagatī* (platform) and was surrounded by eight subsidiary shrines, quite a few now broken (pls. 2, 3).
C05-PANDE-001   p. 9   also Ch 7
The original entrance to the temple was by a stairway flanked by over life size figures of *Śaiva pratihāras* or door keepers (pl. 4). The entrance now is from the western side (pl. 2). The sanctum of the temple is stellate-cum-circular (pl. 5), is *saptaratha* on plan and *saptabhūmi* in elevation.
C05-PANDE-002   p. 9
At the bottom of the *latās* on the three sides (north, south and west) are three prominent sculpted medallions within a large *caitya* arch known as *śūrsenaka* (pl. 1). The *latā* on the east side terminates in the *śukanāsa*. The temples of this class are invariably *nirandhāra* (i.e. without ambulatory).
C05-PANDE-003   p. 9
The gūḍhamaṇḍapa has a samvarṇā or bell-roof (pl. 9). The gūḍhamaṇḍapa or covered hall for the devotees has three projecting balconies (mukhamaṇḍapa) on the east, north and south sides. The main entrance is from the east, where a flight of steps leads to the east mukhamaṇḍapa (pl. 10).
C05-PANDE-004   p. 12
There are three *mukhamaṇḍapas*, one each on the east, north and south which lead to the *gūḍhamaṇḍapa* (pls. 10, 25, 67). A flight of steps precedes the east *mukhamaṇḍapa* (pl. 10).
C05-PANDE-005   p. 72
The gūḍhamandapa has three entrance doors, one from the front or the east mukhamandapa, the other two doors from the north and south mukhamandapas. The main entrance is
