C05-PANDE-007   p. 85–86
Alexander Cunningham, in his Archaeological Survey of India report (compiled between 1874-77) thinks that the *vedī* was used for the preaching of the *Vedas*: “The *bedi*, or the hall for reading the *beds*, or *vedas*, is a low square building with a projection in the middle of each face. It is 23 feet square inside, with a flat roof supported on four massive pillars, each 8½ feet high and 1 foot 10½ inches square, with wide-spreading brackets.
C05-PANDE-008   p. 86   also Ch 11
"The temple stands in a spacious square courtyard again enclosed with a dwarf compound wall, the outer face of which was decorated with carvings. Inner face of the enclosure wall had line of seats furnished with backrests.
C05-PANDE-009   p. 87   also Ch 7
The other two balconies are closed with massive trellises, and each contains a large flat slab, raised 3 feet above the ground, as seats for the readers of the vedas. The side walls are closed with the same massive trellises carved out of slabs 8 inches thick, so that the building is therefore very well lighted inside. The ceiling is formed in the usual manner, by overlapping stones, the corners of the square being cut off by slabs meeting in the middle of each side, so as to form a smaller square. This being repeated, the original square of 13 feet side is reduced to a small square of only half size, which is covered by a single slab, ornamented with a lotus flower. The whole building is massive and substantial and is still in excellent preservation after the lapse of eight centuries.¹²⁷
C05-PANDE-010   p. 87
four entrances, the principal of which was one facing to the east, all having flight of steps guarded on either side by a figure of a doorkeeper.
Of the eight attendant shrines which surrounded the main temple only six are standing in a ruinous condition and sheltered subsidiary gods."129
C05-PANDE-011   p. 88   also Ch 7
Amongst the various styles of medieval Indian temple architecture, the Bhūmija is distinctive as regards both its origin and style. Etymologically, Bhūmija means that which is produced or born from the earth, which seems to connote regional affiliations. In this literal sense, it may be understood and interpreted as the native style of Malwa, which has the largest concentration of Bhūmija temples. However, it may be pointed out that Bhūmija temples have also been found in Rajasthan and northern Maharashtra. One may perceive another meaning of ‘earthly origin’. Bhūmija or of ‘earthly origin’ may be seen as a terrestrially evolved form as opposed to other forms which were believed to have a divine or supernatural origin. The origin, thus, is secular, unlike others which owe their origin to various gods and demi-gods.
C05-PANDE-012   p. 8
This temple is in the Bhūmija style of architecture.
The *Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra* of Bhoja Paramāra is one of the earliest *Śilpasāstras* that refers to the Bhūmija style of temple. The style, however, appears so well established that one is led to infer that it had earlier antecedents. It seems that the Paramāras of Malwa developed this style to its maturity. This inference is supported by the *Śilpasāstras* as also the large numbers of Bhūmija temples found in Malwa.² Bhoja Paramāra is known to be well versed in architecture.
C05-PANDE-013   p. 8
The Bhūmija temple has a unique plan, where the square of the temple is not subdivided like a grid into a number of parts. The square is made to rotate round its centre and made to stop at regular intervals. The points of the rotating square perform a circle and the original square is inscribed within it. Thus, the buttresses between the main wall projections are to be built by *parivartanā* within the circle. The projections on the main walls facing the cardinal points, however, do not result from rotation of the square, but are planned in the usual way, parallel to the square of the *garbhagṛha*.
C05-PANDE-014   p. 9
The distinctive feature of the Bhūmija style is the *śikhara* with four *latās*$^{3}$ or spines adorned with *caitya*-arch motifs on the central vertical bands of the *śikhara*. This style is identifiable by a unique feature, viz. miniature *śikharas* of diminishing heights on the four quadrants between the *latās*. These miniature *śikharas* are called *kūṭastambhas* and are arranged in rows of three to five vertically and five to nine horizontally.
C05-PANDE-015   p. 9
Yet another meaning of Bhūmija may be seen as born from *bhūmis* or storeys. Thus, the word Bhūmija may also connote that the *kūtas* which are arranged vertically one above the other, right from the ground to the *śikhara*, give the appearance of rising directly from the ground upwards.
C05-PANDE-016   p. 9
Thus, an orderly arrangement of pillar-forms and deep recesses form the exterior walls of the temple (pl. 6). These
extend to the śikhara in the same vertical rows but bear the form of kūṭastambhas or miniature śikharas now (pl. 5). The buttresses, thus, seem to rise from the ground itself (Bhūmija), have the appearance of pillars in the janghā portion and continue in the same vertical alignment forming the kūṭastambhas in the śikhara.
C05-PANDE-017   p. 12
extend to the śikhara in the same vertical rows but bear the form of kūṭastambhas or miniature śikharas now (pl. 5). The buttresses, thus, seem to rise from the ground itself (Bhūmija), have the appearance of pillars in the janghā portion and continue in the same vertical alignment forming the kūṭastambhas in the śikhara. The kūṭastambhas,
one below the other, give the appearance of long pearl strings suspended from the śikhara, interrupted only by the flat latās with their gavākṣa lace work in the four cardinal directions (pl. 5).
C05-PANDE-018   p. 12
The śikhara of the garbhagṛha is distinctive of the Bhūmija (pls. 1,
2, 5). On the four cardinal directions are the four flat bands or spines (latās) which are decorated throughout with an intricate lace-work of the gavākṣa design.
C05-PANDE-019   p. 13
In between each latā are seven bhūmis of five kūṭastambhas in each sub-quarter (pl. 1). While the ubiquitous motif of the latā is the gavākṣa, the basic motif of the sub-quarters of the śikhara is the kūṭastambha or a miniature pyramidal śikhara placed over a pillaret. There are five rows of these vertically and seven horizontally. The śikhara thus rises, a majestic arrangement of kūṭastambhas, gradually decreasing in size, towards the skandha (shoulder of the spire), the edges of which articulate the star-shaped plan of the temple.
C05-PANDE-020   p. 14
Early examples of this design component are seen on the makaras on the toraṇa and the devakoṣṭhas of the 7th century Draupadi Ratha at Mamallapuram. A variant of this design component which depicts riders on
THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE
makara figures with outstretched arms is seen adorning the window lintel of the 7th – 8th century Durga Temple at Aihole and the Daśāvatāra cave at Ellora. It is quite probable that this design component may have travelled from India to Southeast Asia as it is depicted on lintels of Pre-Angkorian Cambodian temples.
C05-PANDE-021   p. 15–16
It may be mentioned that the earliest representation of male-female figures standing erect on crocodiles can beseen on the railing pillars of the Amaravati stupa (presently in the collection of the British Museum). They are shown holding either stalks of lotus flowers, offering dishes or even a fragmented pot. The next representation of such standing figures is seen in the Ajanta caves, as for instance, on the garbhagṛha doorframe of cave number 5. These figures may have been the prototypes for the evolved figures seen at Udayeśvara.
C05-PANDE-022   p. 16
in a peculiar pose, the hips are twisted in front into a pose which would not be credible even to acrobats (perhaps only to gods!). A similar pose is seen in the south-west side of the *gūḍhamandāpa* (pl. 43). In fact, apart from the Udayeśvara temple, such a pose is seen in another Paramāra image of Śiva from the Ambarnath temple (Maharashtra) of the 11th century CE (pl. 64). The body has the same peculiar twist; torso and hips are both frontal.
C05-PANDE-023   p. 44
The Paramāras of Vāgaḍa seem to have depicted Cāmuṇḍā as the deity in the northern quarters. She is depicted in the northern *khattakas* in the Maṇḍaleśvara temple, Panaheda as also the Śiva temple, nos. 1, 2, 3 at Arthuna and shrine no. 9 of Nīlakaṇṭha Mahādeva at Arthuna.⁶⁸ It is interesting that at the Udayeśvara temple, too, the prominent depictions of Cāmuṇḍā may be seen in the northern quarter, for eg., the above mentioned sculpture and another major sculpture depicted on the northern *latā*. In many of
these Paramāra images, whether at Vāgaḍa or Udayapur, Cāmuṇḍā is depicted with a scorpion on the stomach.
C05-PANDE-024   p. 54
The *jādyakumbha* is an inverted cyma-recta moulding, a distinctive, lotus-shaped moulding of the *pīṭha*. This is ornamented by a *manibandha* (band of diamond shapes alternating with rosettes) on the side and *ṭhakāra* (*caitya*-window motif) on the top. The *karṇaka* is an arris moulding, decorated by a *gagārapaṭṭī* or a decorative motif comprising of an arrow between two spirals. The fifth is *grāsapaṭṭī*. This is a band of gorgon heads or *grāsa* or *kīrtimukha*.
C05-PANDE-025   p. 9
The *pīṭha* or the base has five mouldings, viz. *bhiṭṭa* 1, *bhiṭṭa* 2, *jādyakumbha*, *karṇaka* and *grāsapaṭṭī* (pl. 7). The first two are basically two courses of the plinth of the substructure. *Bhiṭṭa* 1 is plain, *bhiṭṭa* 2 is ornamented by leaf (*patra*) and garland (*mālā*) design. It is capped by a shoulder (*skandha*) decorated by a *padmadala* or lotus petal design.
C05-PANDE-026   p. 9
The khura is decorated by a ratnapaṭṭī (band of diamond pattern). Above a karṇikā (knife-edge moulding) rests the kumbha. This is ornamented by niches looking like miniature shrines (khattikā) which house gods and goddesses. It is also decorated by a manibandha in the middle and its shoulder or skandha is decorated by lotus petals or padmadala. Thereafter, hemmed in between two karṇikās is the kalaśa or a torus moulding. This is ornamented by a gagārapaṭṭī.
C05-PANDE-027   p. 12
The mighty caitya of the śukanāsa comprises of two parts. In the lower part, the inner trefoil arches sum up a rectangular, pillared shrine (pl. 13) and in the semi-circular wings to the sides are two dancing goddesses (pls. 14, 15). The upper part is a caitya gavākṣa containing dancing Śiva and goddesses (pl. 11).
C05-PANDE-028   p. 14
This portion of the *garbhagṛha* and the *gūḍhamandapa* (pls. 24, 25) comprises buttresses which have the appearance of faceted pillars on which are carved *khattakas* or niches housing deities. The *salilāntara* or recessed part between these buttresses are carved with lotus scrolls and the figures of celestial maidens or *apsarās*. This pattern for the exterior walls of the temple is the same for the *garbhagṛha* as well as the *gūḍhamandapa*.
C05-PANDE-029   p. 30
This is followed by an ornate fillet decorated with diamonds with double volute pattern, viz. the *rājasemaka*. Thereafter is a balustrade or *vedikā*. This consists of short pillars (*stambhikās*) of the *ghaṭapallava* class (pot and foliage decoration). In the interspaces of these pillars are the figures of celestial maidens (*apsarās*) and demi-goddesses in a variety of stances. This is followed by the *chajjikā* (roof-like moulding), *mañcikā* (dias-like moulding) with *ṭhakāra* pattern and *ghaṇṭikā* (bell-like) with *jāla* or diaper work.
C05-PANDE-030   p. 73
The celestial maidens mentioned above are modelled as slim yet with ample curves, tall, graceful and standing on stylized lotuses. They stand mostly in *abhaṅga* stances with slight hip-shot poses.
C05-PANDE-031   p. 73
The doorways of the mukhamaṇḍapa (pl. 112) which lead to the gūḍhamaṇḍapa inside are elaborately decorated, with their doorjambs picturesquely carved with designs of lotus scrolls (patraśākhā), flying or leaping figures (gāndharva śākhā), pillarets (stambha śākhā) decorated with garland design, pot foliage and triangular leaves (ardha ratna), figural representation (rūpa stambha) etc. The doorway ornamentation of the three mukhamaṇḍapa doors is similar, though they do have differences, as for eg., on the peḍya or the lower block of the door jamb. These are invariably carved with the figures of river goddesses and dikpālas as door guardians. The innermost śākhās or jambs of the south mukhamaṇḍapa door are carved with figures of Cāmuṇḍā and Gaṇeśa on either sides. Though most of these figures are either broken or defaced, yet there are some interesting door guardians like Varuṇa, who is seen standing on his crocodile mount holding a pot.
C05-PANDE-032   p. 74
The lintel of the door of the south mukhamandapa (pl. 113) consists of foliage scrolls and garland bearing figures (mālādhāras) and the lalāṭabimbas (central images of the door lintel). The lalāṭabimbas house figures of Gaṇeśa below and Lakṣmī above.
C05-PANDE-033   p. 75
These four main pillars are heavily sculpted (pl. 117). These pillars are square almost upto midway, then polygonal and circular, while the bracket capitals on the top are four sided, giving the semblances of squareness again.
These pillars being a combination of the rucaka (square pillar type) and the vṛtta (circular), may be classified as miśraka, i.e. those pillar types which combine forms from square to polygonal to circular.
C05-PANDE-034   p. 80
The ceiling of the *gūḍhamandapa* consists of concentric circles contained within an octagonal frame (pl. 123). The four corners are decorated with *kīrtimukhas*. This is the *nābhichanda* class of ceiling. This is the type of ornate ceiling with deep concentric cusped and coffered courses or bands. The outermost course of the ceiling is the *karnadardarikā*.
C05-PANDE-035   p. 83
On the northwest face of śikhara of the garbhagṛha is an unusual sculpture of a human figure which seems to ascend the śikhara (pl. 12). The sculpture exhibits an unusual balance. Its role may be to depict the flag bearer who hoists the flag of victory atop the temple śikhara.⁴
C05-PANDE-036   p. 14
In the centre of the upper medallion of the śukanāsa (pl. 11) is a multi-armed figure of Naṭeśa Śiva. He is depicted as performing the tāṇḍava⁷ in the ūrdhvajānu karaṇa.⁸ Most of the hands are broken but one of the right hand forms a khaṭakāmukha near the chest. It is possible that one of the broken left hands may have been an arāla above the knee.⁹ While performing the tāṇḍava, Śiva performed several karaṇas and aṅgahāras. Śiva has a simple conical jaṭā-kalāpa or matted locks, which balance well his squarish face-cut and emphatic chin. There are two devīs dancing on either side and fragmentary figures of gaṇas.
C05-PANDE-037   p. 16
It imitates long lotus-buds and such a type of a necklace is ubiquitous in the sculptures of North India in the 11th and 12th centuries CE.
King Bhoja Paramāra was a great patron of learning and the arts and hence Sarasvatī, the goddess of learning, was often depicted in the Paramāra sculptures. She was the presiding deity of the Bhojaśālā of Dhara attributed to Bhoja. She is invoked in a number of Paramāra epigraphs.
C05-PANDE-038   p. 18   also Ch 4
The Sarasvatī of the Udayeśvara śukanāsa (pl. 15), though fragmentary, exhibits precision and command in chiselling and superior workmanship. It is also interesting that while Sarasvatī, being the goddess of music is invariably associated with the viṇā, this is an unusual image where she is also shown as a dancer. The images of dancing Sarasvatī are quite rare.
C05-PANDE-039   p. 18
The Nāṭyaśāstra describes Śiva as the progenitor of the tāṇḍava dance. It says that after destroying the yajña of Dakṣa, he danced at evening time, the aṅgahāras (of tāṇḍava) in accordance with rhythm and drum beats. Seeing her Lord Śiva thus dancing, his consort Pārvatī performed the feminine or the graceful aspect of the dance.¹² The dance of Śiva and Pārvatī was watched by Nandi,
C05-PANDE-040   p. 19
Bhagvatpāda Śankarācārya describes the twilight time of a hot day when Śiva, blue-throated like a peacock, dances in the company of devī as the peahen, the sound of thunder being the drum played by Hari and the rain being the tears of ecstasy of devotees as spectators.¹³ The Nāṭyaśāstra mentions that varieties of drums and different types of instruments were played during this dance.¹⁴ The scene of the dance of Śiva and Pārvatī seems to have been sculpted in the śukanāsa at Udayeśvara.
C05-PANDE-041   p. 20
commentator on the Nātyaśāstra, explains that during the tāṇḍava dance of Śiva, Nandikeśvara and the other gaṇas of Śiva played the mṛdaṅgas or drums.¹⁵ Thus, in most of the Śiva tāṇḍava sculptures,
the gaṇas are shown playing the drums. According to Abhinavagupta, the tāṇḍava is accompanied not only by the drums but also the viṇā. The viṇā was to be played as an accompaniment for the vardhamānaka¹⁶ which was a complicated beat-structure of the music. Since, Sarasvatī is clearly associated with the viṇā, this seems to explain her presence in the performance of the tāṇḍava. However, the artist has been innovative and shown Sarasvatī not playing the viṇā, but dancing.
C05-PANDE-042   p. 21
The shrine houses a syncretic image of Hariharārkapitāmaha, i.e. Viṣṇu, Śiva, Sūrya and Brahmā, though the faces of only the first three are shown here. The figure stands erect in sambhaṅga and has three faces and eight hands. The central figure, wearing boots, is that of Sūrya. The kirīṭa mukuṭa that he wears is worn by Viṣṇu too. The figure is bejewelled, wears a short dhofi and the long vanamālā of Viṣṇu.
C05-PANDE-043   p. 22
These composite images of Hariharahiranyagarbha follow closely the injunctions laid in the Śilpasāstra texts. It is interesting that the central deity is Sūrya or sometimes Viṣṇu, but not Śiva or Brahmā. These images have been predominantly found in Rajasthan, Gujarat and Central India in the medieval period. Such images do not form the main image in the garbhagṛha. It is noteworthy that these composite images of Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Śiva and Sūrya have a special place in some of the Śaiva temples of the medieval period.
C05-PANDE-044   p. 22
on the north and the south, depict a five-storied celestial mansion (pls. 18, 19). Each storey of this mansion has a miniature pyramidal roof ornamented with the gavākṣa motif, a kūṭachādya and pillarettes. In each storey are housed seated images of Śiva and Śakti or devī, flanked by tall and voluptuous female attendant figures. There are, in all, five images of Śiva and five images of devī. The northern side enshrines four devī and one Śiva image while the southern side enshrines four Śiva and one devī image.
C05-PANDE-045   p. 23
and skull-bowl in her right hands. The attributes discernable in her left hands are the snake, shield, bow and a broken varada hasta. In the image of the Navadurgā Sarvamangalā, the attributes that can be seen include arrows, lotus, abhaya hasta and varadākṣa in the right hands and noose, trident, bell and water-pot in the left hands. She might have held a vajra which is now unfortunately broken.²¹ In the Śiva image, most of the hands are broken, but of the attributes, the skull-topped mace and the skull-bowl are clear. He wears a jaṭāmukuṭa and a munḍamālā (garland of skulls) around his forehead. Nandi is seated to his bottom right. The head of Nandi is turned back to look at Śiva.
C05-PANDE-046   p. 24
Gaurī. This is a very interesting image as the vāhana of Śriyā, the iguana (godhā), is depicted in the place of an attribute in her lower right hand. Also, instead of being seated on a preta (pretāsanā), she holds a preta in her top two hands and seems to be almost hurling it. It is possible that it symbolizes the enemy who is to be destroyed. The attributes discernable in her right hands are the abhaṣa hasta, arrows and the iguana.
C05-PANDE-047   p. 25
These are similar to the śukanāsa, but have much smaller dimensions. Here, too, the sides of the gavākṣa partly cover the kūṭastambhas at the back. Here, however, unlike the śukanāsa, the śūrasenaka closely adheres to the
curved body of the śikhara and hence there are no sculptures to the sides. The image in the centre of all three śūrasenakas is that of Śiva Naṭeśa.
C05-PANDE-048   p. 25
The pyramidal roof of a miniature rectangular shrine abuts the open end of the gavākṣa at the bottom. This enshrines a beautiful image of Śiva carved with precision and command (pl. 21). The three-tiered jaṭāmukuṭa, the modelling of the body, the ornaments, the beatific expression of the face, exhibit a rare finesse. This was a seated six-armed image.
C05-PANDE-049   p. 25
by two miniature standing attendant figures or Śaiva pratihāras. They have one hand in varada and the other holds a khaṭvāṅga. The parikara
Pl. 23. View of the śūrasenaka of the northern latā.
or the frame of this shrine is decorated by composite fantastic animals (vyāla figures) and elephants (gaja figures). In the even smaller miniature shrines next to this are figures of four-armed Śiva holding the trident and the serpent in the upper right and left hands respectively and varadākṣa and kamaṇḍalu in the lower right and left hands respectively.
C05-PANDE-050   p. 28
The northern śūrasenaka, too, has Śiva dancing the tāṇḍava in the ūrdhvajānu (pl. 23). The attributes are somewhat different. The upper arms hold the serpent. In the right hands can be discerned the quiver of arrows and a trident. In the left hands can be seen the bell, the bow and possibly a khaṭvāṅga (club with skull at top). The main arm is not above the uplifted knee as in the west and south śūrasenakas, but is poised obliquely across the torso like the trunk of an elephant.
C05-PANDE-051   p. 28
The south śūrasenaka has a very similar image of Śiva dancing in the ūrdhvajānu with similar weapons (pl. 22). In the pyramidal shrine below is a figure of seated Śiva, but the arms are too mutilated for identification. The figure of Nandi can be seen seated on the left below.
C05-PANDE-052   p. 28
There are two sets of framed niches sculpted on the buttresses. On the *kumbha* of the *vedibandha* are the *khattikās*, two on each buttress (one on each side). The *khattikās* have enshrined within them the figures primarily of Umā-Maheśvara, but also the *dikpālas* (guardians of the eight directions), other gods, goddesses and Gaṇeśa (pl. 7).
The larger of the niches or *khattakas* are on the *jaṅghā* of each buttress, one on either side. These project on a console and are comprised of various mouldings. The *khattakas* consist of two pillars which contain the image within, as also the triangular pediment of the shrine, the apex of which touches a serrated moulding, above which is a canopy.
C05-PANDE-053   p. 30
In the south-east side of the gūdhamaṇḍapa, the first image from the east (near the east mukhamaṇḍapa) is that of standing mulit-armed Mahābhairava (pl. 26). Most of the arms are broken, but the top left holds a shield, and the top right a damaru, the bottom right is at the thigh and holds, perhaps, the hilt of a sword. He stands in an abhaitya posture, is naked, and wears a long garland of skulls. He wears a uṅgaphaṇī (snake-hood) head-dress. His face, though now defaced, shows distinct traces of a stylized beard. His cdhana, the dog, stands near his left leg.
C05-PANDE-054   p. 31
On the buttress next to Bhairava, the image in the khattaka on the east side is that of Śūrya who stands straight in sambhaitya posture (pl. 28). He wears the kiriṇa mukata, bejewelled earrings (ratna kuṇḍala), necklaces, garlands, armlets and boots. He has four hands, the upper two hold lotuses, the lower two are broken. He is flanked by his attendants, Daṇḍī and Piṅgala. Daṇḍī is, according to injunctions, shown to the left of Śūrya and carries a daṇḍā (rod) in his hands.
C05-PANDE-055   p. 31
The description of Bhairava in the Vīṇindharmolāya Mahāpurāṇa is as follows: "Thus I will speak of the making of the form of Bhairava. He should be made having a big belly; round, tawny eyes, terrible face with big teeth, two wide nostrils, a garland of skulls and ornaments of snakes on all sides." Since the Paramāras were strong patrons of Saivism, several Bhairava images have been found in Malwa.
C05-PANDE-056   p. 31   also Ch 4
It may be noted that the position of the dikpālas, in this temple, is always on the khattakas of the central or the second buttress of the gūdhamaṇḍapa. On the central buttress, facing east proper is a bejewelled, four-armed image of Indra (pl. 30).
C05-PANDE-057   p. 31
In this image, he stands in an abhaṅga posture. He holds a thunderbolt (vajra) in his upper right hand and possibly an aṅkuśa (goad), though defaced, in his left hand. The lower right hand is varadākṣa. The lower left is broken but may have held the kamaṇḍalu. His vāhana, the elephant, is to his left below. The Kāmikāgama describes Indra as "mounted on an elephant, pale complexioned, with vajra, he should be placed in the east, (in such a way) should Indra, the thousand-eyed, be worshipped."³⁵
C05-PANDE-058   p. 32
This buttress on the south side, houses a four-armed image of Harihara (pl. 33), a syncretic image of Viṣṇu and Śiva. The proper right side constitutes Śiva and his upper hand holds the trident while the lower is in varadākṣa. The proper left constitutes Viṣṇu. His upper hand holds a cakra (discus),
the upper part of which is fashioned as a stylized lotus. The lower hand holds a śaṅkha (conch-shell). Their tiaras differ in their respective halves; Śiva wears the jaṭāmukuṭa and Viṣṇu, the kirīṭa mukuṭa. Their vāhanas are placed below; Nandi with head raised to the bottom right and Garuḍa personified with folded hands to the bottom left.
C05-PANDE-059   p. 34
On the east face is an image of Ardhanārīśvara, i.e. the right half of the body is constituted by Śiva and the left half by his consort Pārvatī (pl. 32).⁴³ The left half of the body is feminine, having a single breast, rounded thighs and the hip thrusts seductively to the side. The upper left hand holds a lotus and the lower is broken. The right half is constituted by a masculine figure. The upper hand holds a trident and the lower is varadākṣa. Near the right leg of Śiva is the bull Nandi, Śiva's mount. To the left, near Pārvatī's leg, is a lion, Pārvatī's mount.
C05-PANDE-060   p. 34
Near the south *mukhamandapa* (east side) is a multi-armed image of Śiva Nateśa (pl. 34). Though the legs of Śiva are broken, yet the lifted left knee shows that it was an *ūrdhvatāṇḍava* image. Most of the hands are broken, but some of the attributes which can be discerned are the sword, arrows, shield, the bell, the bow, *gajahasta*, etc. An attendant can be seen playing the drum below.
C05-PANDE-061   p. 35; also at p. 35
This seems to be a syncretic image of Gāyatrī, Sāvitrī and Sarasvatī. The three devīs are also called Vedamātās. The manuscript probably denotes the Vedas. Sarasvatī was much venerated by the Paramāra royal family as the goddess of learning. Gāyatrī and Sāvitrī are closely associated with the worship of Sūrya shown below in the niche of the same buttress.
C05-PANDE-062   p. 36
There are six images on the tilaka of the south-east gūḍhamandapa, two above both facets of each of the three buttresses. The images on the first and the last tilaka are Śakti images. Hemmed in between these two Śakti images are four images of Śiva.
C05-PANDE-063   p. 36
Above the *khattakas* which house the images of Śiva, Indra, Agni and Ardhanariśvara, from the east to the south respectively, are the niches of the *tilaka*, all of which house the sculptures of seated Śiva (pl. 38). Distinctive features of these images include the *jaṭāmukuṭa* and ten arms. The attributes held in the hands include the snake, hour-glass drum, trident, skull-bowl, bow, skull-topped mace, *abhaya hasta*. Sometimes, the lower hands vary with the *varada hasta* and the water-pot.
C05-PANDE-064   p. 37
With regard to the khattikās on the vedibandha of the south-east gūḍhamandapa (pl. 27), there are two types of Umā-Maheśvara images that can be seen. The first or standard type, which occurs most commonly (on other sides of the gūḍhamandapa as well), depicts Umā-Maheśvara seated on a pedestal, without their respective mounts (pl. 40). The second type shows Umā-Maheśvara with their respective mounts, the lion and Nandi (pl. 41). Both these types depict Umā seated on the lap of Śiva, her right arm around his shoulder and face towards him.
C05-PANDE-065   p. 38
This is Hamsavatī, who is the chief Sakti who presides over the *ājñā cakra*. She is described in the *Lalitāsahasranāma Stotra* of the *Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa*.$^{60}$ She is *ṣaḍānanā* or one with six faces. The three frontal faces can be seen here in this image. Her very name suggests that ‘she is the one with the swan’. The large bird seated below her is the swan. The *ājñā cakra* is located on the forehead corresponding to the auspicious *tilaka* put there.
C05-PANDE-066   p. 39
The khattikās on the vedībandha house a seated image of Gaṇeśa below Naṭeśa and seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type (Umā-Maheśvara seated on a pedestal) below both the buttresses and the south kapilī. Gaṇeśa (pl. 56) is seated in lalitāsana and is four-armed. The upper right and left hold the axe and the lotus respectively and the lower left holds the modakas (sweet-meat). The lower right may have held the broken tusk as desired by canonical injunctions, but it is indiscernible now.
C05-PANDE-067   p. 42
Bhairava worship had become more tantric in character. This is attested by the practice of śavasādhanā of Bhairavācārya described in the third ucchvāsa of the Harṣacarita. Here, Bhairavācārya is seated on the breast of a corpse adorned with red sandalwood, garlands and ornaments.⁶¹ In Paramāra sculptures, the image of Bhairava is seen both with the dog and the corpse.
C05-PANDE-068   p. 42   also Ch 8
The Aparājitapṛcchā describes him thus; "With hands as boons-giving, holding the noose, lotus and water vessel, Varuṇa should be made riding a crocodile and in the west".⁶⁶ The other sculpture, facing north, enshrines a standing male deity (pl. 60) with the attributes broken. However, besides the right foot of the deity can be seen the bull mount, with the front legs folded behind and with a bell hanging from its neck. This is Īśāna, the regent of the north-east quarter.
C05-PANDE-069   p. 43
The one facing west is Varuṇa (pl. 59), the regent of the western quarter.⁶² He stands in abhaṅga and wears a karaṇḍa mukuṭa. He holds the pāśa in his top right hand and the lower is varadākṣa. The left hands are broken. He stands on a crocodile.
C05-PANDE-070   p. 43
A seated image of Vāyu with his consort Śivā (pl. 66) is seen in a khattikā below the image of Brahmā. Vāyu is one of the eight dikpālas and is the regent of the north-western quarter. The Aparājitaprcchā describes him thus: “With hands having boons-giving (gesture) and flag, bearing a banner and water-pot, seated on a deer, green in colour is the wind (God) the dikpāla.72 The Kāmikāgama describes him as “variegated, flag in hand and seated on a deer.”73 In this image, he is two-armed (dvibhujo), in accordance with his description given in the Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa.74 He is seated in lalitāsana. He has a strange hairstyle which is probably the vyākulamūrdhajo, i.e. agitated hair, as if standing-up, blowing in the wind.75 His left arm is around Śivā, his consort.76 The attribute in his right arm is
C05-PANDE-071   p. 45
She is the most popular manifestation of Śakti. The Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa, gives a detailed description of the myth connected with her.⁷⁷ She is said to be the personified form of the wrath of all the gods. She was made manifest by the effulgence that the gods had emitted in their anger.
C05-PANDE-072   p. 48
In the above mentioned khattaka (pls. 68, 69) near the north mukhamandapa (east side), the goddess is seen with multiple arms. One of the left hands holds a shield but, unfortunately, the majority are broken. As described in the Aparājitapṛcchā, one of her left hands here seizes the forelocks of Mahiṣāsura and the right foot is placed firmly on him.
C05-PANDE-073   p. 50
Interestingly enough, following the canonical injunctions, from the severed neck arises the demon Mahiṣāsura to continue his fight with the goddess. He is now shown in human form and not that of a buffalo.
C05-PANDE-074   p. 51
It may be said that this image must have been that of Kubera, as he is the only dikpāla that remains. The positions of Kubera and Īśāna seem to have been reversed as Īśāna is the regent of the north-east quarter and Kubera is the regent of the north direction. Even their headgears seem to have been reversed. The purpose may have been
logic of symmetry of arrangement and thus a harmonious design.
C05-PANDE-075   p. 52
The dikpālas with elephant mounts, viz. Indra and Kubera have been placed on south-east and north-east, on the second buttress. The
dikpālas with bovine mounts, viz. Yama and Īśāna have been placed corresponding to each other on the buttresses facing south and north proper.
C05-PANDE-076   p. 52
Next to the image of Sarasvatī, near the east mukhamaṇḍapa, the large khattaka (facing north) houses a much mutilated image of a multi-armed Cāmuṇḍā (pls. 68, 73). The legs are completely destroyed and so are the attributes of the hands. However, her emaciated body can still be clearly discerned. She is standing on a corpse.
C05-PANDE-077   p. 53
There are six images on the *tilaka* of the north-east *gūḍhamandapa*, two above both facets of the three buttresses (pl. 68). Here too, similar to the sculptural arrangement of the south-east *tilaka*, there are four Śiva images hemmed in between two Śakti images. The images of seated Cāmuṇḍā and Śiva can be seen enshrined in the *tilaka*, above the images of dancing Śiva and Hara-Gaurī respectively.
C05-PANDE-079   p. 54
Adjacent to the image of Cāmunḍā on the tilaka (facing north), below the saṃvarṇā roof and above the Mahiṣāsuramardinī image, is a splendid three-headed and eight-armed image of Hariharārkapitāmaha facing east (pl. 78). It is fairly well preserved and in the early morning, the rays of the sun illuminate it in entirety. The central image is that of Sūrya. The deity stands erect in sambhaṅga and has a finely modelled body; well muscled torso, slim waist and rather long, slim legs. The face is squarish in the Paramāra idiom with the typical roundish but emphatic chin.
C05-PANDE-080   p. 55
The devī image (pl. 77) above the khattaka enshrining the image of Sarasvatī (facing east) is quite intriguing as one of the attributes held in her upper right hand is an elephant. All
her other hands are mutilated, but an hour-glass drum and arrows can be discerned in her right hands and a small cup is held in her lower left hand. A water-pot is placed on her right below. She wears a jaṭāmukuṭa adorned with a crescent moon and is, hence, a Śaivite goddess.
C05-PANDE-081   p. 55
The image of Hariharārkapitāmaha has been rendered here to impart a degree of symmetry by matching it with the syncretic image of Ardhanarīśvara on the corresponding side (south-east of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa). Further, this symmetry is visible in the rendition of both the images, as both stand straight and erect in sambhaṅga. It may again be noted that this symmetry is also noticeable in the position of the dikpālas on the second buttresses of the four sides of the gūḍhamaṇḍapa as well as in their arrangement on the basis of similar vāhanas.
C05-PANDE-082   p. 56
Ardhanarīśvara and Hara-Gaurī on corresponding *khattakas* on the south-east and north-east side. Apotropaic images of Śiva and Śakti as Bhairava and Cāmuṇḍā near the south and north *mukhamaṇḍapas* also exhibit a similar balance in design.
C05-PANDE-083   p. 57
The image of Vīrabhadra (pl. 81) on the south latā is mutilated; the head and tusks cannot be seen, as also the attributes held in the hands. The kapālamālā, however, is visible.⁹⁶ According to legend, the gods and Prajāpati were attacked by Vīrabhadra⁹⁷, so the prostrate figure of Dakṣa Prajāpati can be seen, as also one of the other gods who is now headless.
C05-PANDE-084   p. 58
The third or central buttress houses the images of the dikpālas, Agni (south-east face) and Nirṛti (south-west face). Here, it may be mentioned that of the five buttresses of the garbhagrha, the central buttress has the dikpālas. This is similar to the pattern of the gūḍhamandapa.
C05-PANDE-085   p. 60
Vīrabhadra is shown strangling a headless figure, probably that of Dakṣa Prajāpati, with his right leg and a fleeing god can be seen on the side of the pillared niche. Surrounding Vīrabhadra are six mātṛkās, and two more being on adjacent buttresses to the left and right. On the left buttress is seen Nandikeśvarī (pl. 89) while on the right is seen Kaumārī (pl. 91). These collectively form the aṣṭamātṛkā group.
C05-PANDE-086   p. 61
Of the six faces, the three of the front are seen. Following canonical
injunctions, the image had twelve hands, all (except one which holds a shield) are broken now. The facile modelling of the body depicts a youthful form. He wears a karaṇḍa mukuṭa. The girdle with its beautiful carving and pendant lotus-shaped beads can still be seen. His mount, the peacock, may be seen to his bottom right and a male attendant figure to the left.
C05-PANDE-087   p. 62
The first buttress, adjacent to the Vīrabhadra image (on the west latā), houses the sculptures of Kaumārī and Kumāra on either facet. The khattaka facing west enshrines an image of Kaumārī (pl. 91), the consort of Kumāra, She has three faces and four arms which are broken. She stands in abhaṅga, wears a profusion of jewellery and is chiselled with great precision and command.
C05-PANDE-088   p. 65
On the khattikās of the first buttress, below the figures of Cāmuṇḍā, are seen images of standing Umā-Maheśvara and seated Umā-Maheśvara with Nandi below. On the khattikās of the second buttress, below the images of a headless deity and Bhairava, are seen seated Umā-Maheśvara images of the standard type. On the khattikās of the third buttress, below Agni and Nirṛti, are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated
Gaṇeśa-Gaṇeśāṇī respectively. On the khattikās of the fourth buttress, below Viṣṇu and Bhairava, are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi respectively. The khattikās of the fifth buttress, below the images of Nandikeśvara and Nandikeśvarī, house images of seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi and seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type.
C05-PANDE-089   p. 65
The third or central buttress enshrines two male deities. The hands and legs of both are broken, as also the attributes. However, according to the iconographic programme of the temple, the central buttress is that of the *dikpālas*.
C05-PANDE-090   p. 66
Kubera is traditionally regarded as the lord of all treasures and wealth, as the *yakṣarāja* (lord of all the demi-gods) and the regent of the northern quarter. He is sometimes shown seated on an elephant (*gajāruḍha*) and his mount is a human (*naravāhana*).$^{107}$ The *Aparājitapr̥cchā* says: “Bearing in his hands the mace, treasure, citron and water-pot, seated on an elephant, should be made Dhanada who should be in the north.”$^{108}$ This image (pl. 98) shows him with four hands. The upper two hands hold a mongoose-shaped bag full of treasure. He holds a citron and a cup (presumably wine-cup) in his lower left and right hands respectively.
C05-PANDE-091   p. 66
Since the Kuṣāṇa times, Kubera has also been associated with the wine cup. On his two sides are two female attendants, holding fly-whisks. These are actually the two *nidhis* or treasures (personified as females) Padma *nidhi* and Śaṁkha *nidhi*, standing on stylized lotuses and attending to their master.$^{109}$ The *khattikās* of the third buttress (the *khattakas* enshrining images of Varuṇa and Vāyu) enshrine images of seated Umā-Maheśvara with both the lion and Nandi and seated Brahmā-Brahmāṇi respectively.
C05-PANDE-092   p. 66
Housed in the *khattikās* of the fifth buttress (below the *khattakas* enshrining images of a devī and an unidentified headless deity) are images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type and seated Sarasvatī respectively. The image of Sarasvatī (pl. 102), is four-armed and though greatly defaced, it is clear that she holds the book and lotus in her upper right and left hands, the *kamaṇḍalu* in her lower left, while the lower right hand may have been in *varadākṣa*.$^{110}$
C05-PANDE-093   p. 67
Cāmuṇḍā stands on a corpse (now headless), is naked, emaciated and has a sunken belly with a scorpion on it. Cāmuṇḍā is seen wearing a *muṇḍamālā*. Cāmuṇḍā, like Virabhadra on the southern and western *latās*, is also encircled by six *mātṛkās*, the seventh being Cāmuṇḍā herself and the eighth being Māheśvarī (pls. 103, 104). Māheśvarī is located on the buttress towards the north *kapilī* and is identified by the presence of Nandi below.
C05-PANDE-094   p. 67
enshrined in the upper and lower khattakas of the northern latā. Similarly, on the north-east side of the gūdhamaṇḍapa, the image of Mahiṣāsuramardinī is seen on the northern side and the image of Cāmuṇḍā is seen on the eastern side. Further, as regards the gorbhagṛha, all the three latās are encircled by aṣṭamātṛkā figures. In fact, the temple itself is surrounded by eight devakulikās (which are now destroyed) which in all probability may have housed the aṣṭamātṛkās. This is corroborated by the Kāmikāgama which mentions
that in Śiva temples in villages, the image of Gaurī should be established in eight directions.¹¹¹
C05-PANDE-095   p. 68
The khattikās on the buttresses between the north latā and north kapilī (pl. 104) house three images of Umā-Maheśvara and one image of Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa. The khattikās on the first buttress house images of seated Umā-Maheśvara of the standard type. The khattikās on the second buttress house images of seated Umā-Maheśvara and seated Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa.
C05-PANDE-096   p. 69
On the large *khattaka* below the north *kapilī* is a three-faced (the fourth, it is assumed, is at the back) image of Brahmā seated on a lotus pedestal (pls. 104, 107). His front face is bearded, but not so the profile
ones. The hands of Brahmā are broken. On his left side is seated his consort Sāvitrī, on his lap.
C05-PANDE-097   p. 71
Lakṣmī is seated on the lap of Nārāyaṇa to his left. Her right arm encircles Nārāyaṇa’s shoulder. Her left hand holds a lotus. This is the only Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa image depicted in this temple, tucked away in a shadowed *khattikā*.
C05-PANDE-098   p. 71
The last, is an interesting and unusual image, which is an aberration when compared to the others. It is seen on the *vedikā* of the east *mukhamaṇḍapa* (south side). A female stands with her body bent slightly to the right. Her left leg is on the ground, while the right is placed on an animal below, which is distinctly a camel (pl. 111). She has a long staff which rests against her left shoulder.
C05-PANDE-099   p. 73
shows nine goddesses, most of them having the lower right hand in *varada* gesture and the left hand holding a water pot. All are seated in the *ardhaparyaṅkāsana* in pedimented niches. The attributes of the upper hands differ. Starting from the proper right is Brahmāṇi, the consort of Brahmā. She holds the ladle and book in her upper right and left hands respectively. The lower right hand is in boons-giving and the left holds the water-pot.
C05-PANDE-100   p. 76
Above the figures of the *mātṛkās* are depicted the male gods and the *navagrahas* or nine planets (pl. 113). In the five projecting pedimented niches are seated the male gods. In between are four recessed niches housing the nine planets.
C05-PANDE-101   p. 77
In between these five projecting niches, are four recessed niches bearing
the images of the nine planets in personified forms, viz. Sūrya, Candra, Mangala, Budha, Śani, Bṛhaspati, Śukra, Rāhu and Ketu.¹¹⁵ Each niche has a pair of planets, as for eg., Sūrya and Candra (first recessed niche from proper right). The first recessed niche from left proper has three planets Rāhu, Ketu and possibly Saturn (because of the peculiar way in which the left leg seems to be placed). In Indian astrology, these three are known to be malefic planets and hence are placed together.
C05-PANDE-102   p. 79
The Aparājitapr̥cchā describes the image of Pārvatī thus: "Pārvatī, born of the (Himālaya) mountain, has a rosary, water pot, Śiva(linga) and the chief of the gaṇas (Gaṇeśa) and has on both the sides agnikuṇḍas or pits of fire."¹²² In the pillar niches of the interior of the gūḍhamandapa, Pārvatī is shown with four hands (pl. 118). The upper right and left hands hold the Śivalinga and Gaṇeśa respectively. The lower hands are invariably broken, but the lower left may have held the kamandalu and the lower right must have been varadākṣa. It seems that there were two agnikuṇḍas, one on each side at the bottom, as one may be seen to the bottom left.
C05-PANDE-103   p. 82
The square portions of the pillars, as mentioned above, have the sculpted images of Śiva and Tapasvini Pārvatī. In the two niche shrines of the pillars which house Śiva, he stands in a slightly abhaṅga stance. He wears a high, conical jaṭāmukuṭa, symmetrically arranged and adorned with a crescent moon. In the upper right and left hands he holds the trident and snake respectively, the lower left holds the water-pot and the lower right must have been boons-giving with rosary. Two niches house the images of Tapasvini Pārvatī (pl. 118).
C05-PANDE-104   p. 82
There is a seated Umā-Maheśvara image inside the temple (pl. 130). The finely chiselled hair-styles, face-cuts, proportionately modelled bodies are now unfortunately coated with the grime and soot of the lamps burning in front of the image. Umā is seated to the left, on the lap of Śiva. Their respective mounts, the lion and Nandi, are placed below.
C05-PANDE-105   p. 92
It has taken me more than a decade to study the Udayeśvara temple in such a holistic manner from primary sources. The religious imagery has been decoded in

Saivāg
a literal and authentic manner from the Āgamas and Śilpaśāstras of the period. Translations of these, done by me, have been included in the appendix.
C05-PANDE-109   p. 2–3
Visou and Śiva are indicative of the sun, moon and fire of which elements these gods are the adhiṣṭhātri devatās. Such an image greatly assists in the meditation of Sadāśiva.¹⁸ The composition of the śukanāsa, thus, may be related to the elements of Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy. It is seen to reflect the śuddha addhvā of the Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy. We may see the components of Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva, Īśvara and Sadvidyā in the śukanāsa.¹⁹ In the upper medallion of the śukanāsa, the components of Śiva and Śakti are seen. Śiva performing the dance of creation is also the creator of the universe or Īśvara, the controller of the destiny of the jīvas. Sadvidyā is made manifest in the form of Sarasvatī and the syncretic image of Hariharārkapitāmaha aids in the meditation of Sadāśiva.
C05-PANDE-110   p. 23
be the six-armed Mṛtyuñjaya.²⁴ In the lowest pair of hands, he holds the rosary and water-pot; the other four are unfortunately broken.
The two in the middle, which seem to turn inwards, may have been in the yogamuññā of meditative gesture. This proposition is strengthened by the fact that his face is also in a meditative pose. Furthermore, as desired by the texts, he wears snake ornaments—snake earrings (sarpa kuṇḍala), snake sacred thread (sarpa yajñopavita) and is fair.²⁵ Since this temple was built for victory, it seems fitting that Śiva-Mṛtuñjaya would help overcome death which is the imminent danger on the battlefield.
C05-PANDE-111   p. 27
He is both the sacrificial fire as well as an exalted deity.³⁸ He is called a knowledgeable Brahmin.³⁹ As such, it is not surprising that here at Udayeśvara temple, the book is shown in the hand of Agni. It symbolizes both the Vedic sacrifices and Vedic scriptures. Sacrificial rituals and oblations formed an integral part of Śaiva Siddhānta religious practices as well. Out of the three principal activities of yoga, kriyā and caryā in Śaiva Siddhānta, caryā consisted of daily fire rituals along with Śiva mantras. These were performed in accordance with the Śaiva Āgamic texts.
C05-PANDE-112   p. 32
combination of knowledge and martial aspects. The *Veda*, *vitarka mudrā*, *akṣasūtra* all are referents of knowledge. The *pāśa* is an important component in Śaiva philosophy. It is the snare or the bondage of ignorance. Weapons such as *daṇḍa*, *ghaṇṭā* and as also the *pāśa* connote the martial aspects.
C05-PANDE-113   p. 37
86), as otherwise he is relegated only to the lintel panels above the door-frames and on a single khattikā (seated with his consort Lakṣmī) between the north latā and north kapilī. The Viṣṇu image enshrined in the aforementioned khattaka is identified by the attributes of his upper two hands which hold the discus and the remains of a lotus-topped mace. the bovine-headed Śaivite mother-goddess, Nandikeśvarī, has supplanted the swine-headed mother-goddess Vārāhī, who is basically Vaiṣṇava in character. This appears as a deliberate attempt to give an exclusive Śaivite character.
C05-PANDE-115   p. 62
It is interesting to note that the sculptural programme of the Udayeśvara temple reflects the philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta. Śaiva Siddhānta is essentially a dualistic system where the *upāsanā* of both Śiva and Śakti is important. Of the five components of the *śuddha addhvā*, the two main components are Śiva and Śakti. Śakti dominates the left half of the temple because the female principle is that of *vāmā* or that on the left. Therefore, in the Udayeśvara temple, Śakti images occur both on the left side of the *garbhagṛha* (northern and north-eastern directions) and *gūḍhamaṇḍapa* as well as on the *tilaka* of the *gūḍhamaṇḍapa*.
C05-PANDE-116   p. 67
I have narrated to you the form of Virūpākṣa who is the unrivalled lord of the demons”.¹¹⁴ She is probably Ninṛti, the wife of Virūpākṣa. The long, majestic staff against her left shoulder
signifies death (death to the enemies, perhaps!) and the camel signifies delusion. This is because the camel carries people across the desert who keep seeing the mirage of greenery and water as they move. Both the hands of Ninṛti are broken, but may have held the noose and the reins of the camel. The reins, which signify pāśa are important in the context of Śaiva Siddhānta.
C05-PANDE-117   p. 74
The Udayeśvara temple is based on a Śaiva sect which derives from Śaiva Siddhānta. This temple was built by Udayāditya Paramāra. The Paramāra rulers were followers of this Śaiva sect. Bhoja Paramāra, one of the greatest rulers of this dynasty was a well known Śaiva philosopher himself. His *Tattvaprakāśa* is one of the most authentic texts of Śaiva Siddhānta.
C05-PANDE-121   p. 103   also Ch 4, Ch 8
From being a common mortal, man may aspire to the company of the lord (*śivasāyujya*). This is the primary aim of life to be pursued in the midst of its myriad purposes. The temple is a standing reminder of this possibility.

It may be noted here that the figures of Gaṇeśa and Gaṇeśāṇī are seen mainly on the khattikās of the vedibandha. Their placement
on this singular khattaka is strategic with regard to the Vīrabhadra image on the south latā. According to the Devatāmūrtiprakaraṇa, Gaṇeśa and Vīreśvara (Vīrabhadra) should be made amidst the mātrkās.⁹⁴ It significantly says that “the form of Vīreśvara should be made projected in front amongst the mātrkās.”⁹⁵ This is apparent in the south latā as six goddesses encircle the Vīrabhadra image. Two face the east, two flank the Vīrabhadra image and thus face south and two face the west. These, taken together with Gaṇeśāṇī and Cāmuṇḍā on the two buttresses flanking the latā are the aṣṭamātrkās.

Above this is the circular section of the pillar which is adorned by flying vidyādharā figures. Their bodies, closely packed and symmetrically arranged in neat, oblique positions convey the sense of movement, forward yet circular. They carry in their hands swords and shields.¹¹⁷ This modelling seems to follow the description of the Samrāṅgaṇasūtradhāra which says: “Vidyādharas are considered lower than yakṣas in the scale of bodies. They have splendid garlands and clothes and splendid swords and shields in their hands.”¹¹⁸ It is interesting that one of the earliest depictions of vidyādharas with swords goes as far back as the 5th century CE. Cave 17 of Ajanta shows two flying vidyādharas with swords tucked in their girdles.¹¹⁹ The circular section above the vidyādharas is adorned by a design of eight triangular leaves or half diamonds (ardharatna).
C05-PANDE-138   p. 80
It seems that the artisan here was well-versed with canonical injunctions. "...Inferior to the daityas should be made the yakṣas who should (appear) exuberant with intoxication. Inferior to the, yakṣas are the gandharvas and inferior to these are the nāgas. Inferior to the nāgas are the rākṣasas who are cruel and deformed. Vidyādharas are considered lower than yakṣas in the scale of bodies. They have splendid garlands and clothes and splendid swords and shields in their hands."¹²⁰

256), the Prāsāda has in each of the main directions—the west or back of the temple being shown on Pl. C05-KRAM-2-012   p. 108–109
It is therefore said that the projection (nirgama) of these buttresses either lies within the circle or else is measured by aliquot parts of the square of the Prāsāda (S.S. LXV. 1-2). Prāsādas having this plan are classified as “Bhūmi-ja”, “country-born” and are said to be “both square and circular” in plan.
C05-KRAM-2-019   p. 102
A conclave of pillar shapes assembled so as to round off the temple, forming its wall, is extended in the Sīkhara in the shape of concurrent beaded chains massed together and subordinated to the flat band of Latās in each of the four directions in which the Sālā of the wall has its extension. It is sealed by the Šukanāsā in the East (Pls. XLVII-IX) and by the Šukanāsikās, each having but half the height of the Šukanasā, in the three remaining quarters.
C05-KRAM-2-020   p. 103
The Śikhara thus ascends, an array of Śrīgas, an array of pillars, progressively diminishing in size, towards the Skandha whose edge repeats the star shaped plan of the temple, 5 pointed in each of the sub-quarters. The Lātās ‘transcend’ the shoulder-course of the Śikhara; their arches attaining to the height of the circular ‘neck’, the shaft of the temple, which is clasped by an Āmalaka whose corrugated rim responds in its cusps to the angles of the star-shaped temple.
The Nilakanthésvara temple shows the themes of its perpendicular walls continued on the curvilinear Sikhara.
C05-KRAM-2-021   p. 104
The Sīkhara rises thence with a slight ingress, a double zone of mouldings—its motives being those of capital shapes and fillets—forms its base. The arch of the Sīkhara ascends from this base, unbroken in the four quarters, where flat Latās, with their Gavāksa lattices are flung up to the Āmalaka, exceeding bow-shaped (dhanus), the truncated body of the Sīkhara. These arches, each comprised of 3 (or 5) Latās, spring from and are partly covered by, the Šukanāsā at the bottom. Between them are seven Bhūmis of five Śrīgas each, in every sub-quarter.
C05-KRAM-2-022   p. 104
Part of the central offset of the Sīkhara, facing west (details of Pls. XLIII and XLIV); Nilakanthesvara Temple, Udayapur.
Firm in contour and design, the prolongation of the central buttress in the Western quarter (as also in the South and North) is set against a background of deep darkness. The purity of its vertical edges and the precision of its detailed pattern are thrown into relief by the surrounding shadows, themselves replete with meticulous carvings, whose presence is more felt than seen.
C05-KRAM-2-023   p. 105
Nilakaṃtchevara T., Udayapur, 200, 218, 246, 255, 256, 260, 389-396
C05-KRAM-2-024   p. 170   also Ch 7
Offering their two slanting surfaces to the light at a right angle, the 'bud'-like buttresses of this kind of Prāsāda are rich with many gradations of light and shade in which the images and carvings are embedded (Pls. XLIII-IV). The Pallavikās, the faceted or 'indented' apices, further dissolve the mass of the walls into sheets of light and darkness.
The buttresses, in their Janghā portion, which is the wall proper of the temple, have the appearance of pillars.
C05-KRAM-2-040   p. 102
Higher up, a broad Vājana, or fillet—having for its enrichment (alamkāra) a Krittimukha in the centre—clasps the pillar-buttress before it is to be contracted into its neck on which rest the mouldings forming its capital. From the Krittimukha, extending on the main facet downwards, a heavy chain is carved terminating with a small bell in relief (prāsakinkinikā; S.S. I,VII, 788).
These adornments belong to the wall as pillar (janghā) proper; they are not extended at a further depth of the buttress.

are the same as for instance on the Adhisthāna and Vedikā of the temples in Khaju- raho (Figs. on pp. 259-60) by the modifications of proportion and position their effect is altogether different.
C05-KRAM-2-042   p. 103
The broad band of the Latā is carved on three levels; the lowermost protrudes its fringe made of tiers or Bhūmis; on the next higher level the fringe of tiers emerges from below a carved lace pattern cast over, while yet revealing, however so little, the stratification. The all-over pattern is bounded on the inside by a narrow and plain vertical edge whence protrudes, accompanied by a line of deepest shadow, the central face of the Sīkhara. Over it too, is cast the Gavāksa-net, veiling and linking its strata; at the edges they emerge clean cut.
C05-KRAM-2-043   p. 105
Nilakantheśvara Temple, Udayapur.
The balcony which affords the entrance is the large projection (bhadra; śālā) in the middle of the Southern part of the Mandapa. A similar pillared hall provides an entrance from the North while the main entrance in the East is of the same kind.
Steps lead up across the mouldings of the projecting base (Padma, etc.) with their enrichments. The Vedi proper, the main portion of the vertical walls is underlined by a dwarf railing between boldly projecting fillets (pattikā; giri patrikā of the Rājasenā or Senaka), above and below, which add their dark lines of shadows to those of the base. The Vedi, a railing of square pillars, is enlivened by the images of attendant goddesses carved in high relief from the intervening siabs.
C05-KRAM-2-044   p. 108
Nilakanthésvara Temple, Udayapur.
One of the main pillars of the Mandapa, a pillar and wall-pillar of the Antarāla and the vertical surround of the entrance to the Garbhagrha are seen in this Plate.
The hall pillars are square at the base (2' 9" square) and up to more than half the height of the shaft (5' 6"); the octagonal part is 3' 8" high, the circular section only 1' 3". The transition from the square at the root to the circle on top, with an intervening octagonal section is the rule in mediaeval pillars.
C05-KRAM-2-045   p. 109
The Pallavikās, the faceted apices of the triangular buttresses, are summed up at the bottom of each of the two sides by a pillared shrine projecting on a console formed of various mouldings. The shrines are replete each with an image of a Parivāra-devatā, such as Gancśa—on the left of the Plate—and with his female counterpart in the adjacent shrine at a right angle, on the same buttress. The triangular pediments of the shrines touch a moulding which is a canopy, shrunk in depth, of the image in its niche, and a clasp of the pillar shape of the “buttresses”.
C05-KRAM-2-081   p. 103
There the recessed planes are filled by one continuous panel having an image of Sakti as Vana-devatā, below a burly creeper which carries celestials and the figures of various animals.
C05-KRAM-2-082   p. 103
Against this background of carved lace are set the images of the Šukanāsā; sculptures in the round along its edge, they play their short drama from Kirttimukha to Kirttimukha (only the group on the right is preserved); between them is extended the Gavāksa-part of the antefix. Pointed extrados and circular intrados circumscribe its frame—as they did when the arch of manifestation, the arch of Prakrti, conveyed by its form only the meaning which since was summed up in the Face of Glory and given exposition in the creamy plenitude of waves of carved figures and shapes.
C05-KRAM-2-083   p. 105
Nilakan thesvara Temple, Udayapur.
The main Šukanāsā (cf. Pl. XLVIII), seen from the front, is a mighty blind, whose inner trefoil arch sums up the rectangular shrine and its accompanying images, in the lower part, and the image of Šiva dancing and his accompanying goddesses in the circle above.
C05-KRAM-2-084   p. 106
The close correspondence of the structure and superstructure of the temple brought about by the theme of the pillar is however not particular only to the Nilakanthésvara temple at Udayapur, Gwalior; nor to 'star shaped' Prāsādas in general. Temples with orthogonal buttresses are similarly ordered,* the composition moreover follows the same vision whether the temples belong to the Nāgara or the Vārāta 'style'.** Thus they are prescribed to be built in the 'Samarāṅganasūtradhāra', chapters LXVIII and LXIV, treating of Nāgara and Vārāta Prāsādas respectively.

## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 17)
At the end of the lane a very old, huge stone doorway appears, on the other side of which is the Neelkantheshwar Mahadev temple, one of the finest monuments of this region from the era of the Paramara kings. That narrow, suffocating lane is the only way that leads to the doorway in the rear part of the temple. The temple's main east-facing doorway has been shut for who knows how long. It must once have been shut for some reason, and it is shut to this day.
C05-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 9 (Hindi original)
Whatever the case, fortunately, in the deadly storms of the religious ignoramuses, Udaipur's most priceless construction, the Neelkantheshwar temple, survived. Every pillar, every doorframe and every door of the ruined palace precinct, present a little distance behind the temple, gives the priceless testimony of the Paramaras' clear architecture.
C05-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 53 (Hindi original)   also Ch 10
Go up the hill and see, and you will find that, like Ellora's rock-temple, in Udaipur a rock-temple was going to be made by cutting the hill. But this work was stopped at the very initial stage. A few proofs exist even today.
C05-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 78 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
Narayan Vyas possibly called Udaipur the land of heroes because Udaipur's ancestors saved the temple, in its full height, from being annihilated at the hands of Tughlaq's similarly greedy plunderers who came a hundred years after Iltutmish. If one sees the demolished temples in this region, it is a big miracle that this temple built by king Udayaditya survived to a large extent.
C05-JAGTA-EN-004   ≈ p. 106 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
Closing the temple's western door, a small place of worship was stretched up, and this was work done in six or eight months. The western door leading inside the temple is erected — during that very time — from the rubble that came out of the pavilions (*mandapa*) broken in the temple precinct.
C05-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 108 (Hindi original)
Udaipur is a poem written on stones, which the Paramara kings sang in praise of their adored Lord Shiva. In composing this praise, three years' time was taken, and thousands of artisans, having come from who-knows-where, had displayed the very best of their art.
C05-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 121 (Hindi original)
This temple, which took more than twenty years to be built and made ready, must, even while under construction, have been a subject of discussion far and wide; and at the time of the flag-hoisting, art-loving guests from far and near must have seen with their own eyes what the might and creativity of the Paramaras is called.
C05-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 126 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
He raised the Nilkantheshvar temple — one of the most beautiful shrines in the country, a matchless specimen of architecture. From base to spire (*shikhar*), several replicas of the temple itself have been arranged, one atop another.
C05-JAGTA-EN-008   ≈ p. 160 (Hindi original)
- Udayeshvar Mahadev is considered the finest specimen of Paramara-era construction. In the classical terminology of architecture (*vastu*), it is called "Bhumija" architecture — a distinctive style of constructing upon level, sloping ground. In it, the walls were made of bricks and stones.
- Scholars say that the birth, elevation and development of the Bhumija style of architectural construction took place in Malwa — particularly in the Paramara period of the ninth–tenth centuries. From here, this style spread to Rajasthan, Malwa, Gujarat, Maharashtra, Chhattisgarh, Odisha and Uttar Pradesh as well.
C05-JAGTA-EN-009   ≈ p. 164 (Hindi original)
When I came near him, my eye fell on an image of a goddess. About six-and-a-half to seven feet above the ground, on the outer wall of the temple, it was — among countless images — one such broken and extremely beautiful image whose head had been struck off, whose hands had been badly cut away. The beautiful foot-sole of one leg too was cut. Blows had been dealt to its bosom, and
on the whole body the marks of wounds from the strikes of chisels could clearly be seen.
C05-JAGTA-EN-010   ≈ p. 16 (Hindi original)
So that it could not speak or tell anything, the invaders had severed its head from the trunk. So that it could not even make gestures, its ten arms had been cut off. So that it might not come out of the image, the soles of its feet too had been blown off. The invaders, attacking the temple in some century in between, had done heavy damage to the images inside and out.
C05-JAGTA-EN-011   ≈ p. 24 (Hindi original)
Even today you can see the breasts — adorned with the ornaments of the goddess-images — cut off. The nose and ears will be seen broken. The faces you will find only disfigured.
C05-JAGTA-EN-012   ≈ p. 27 (Hindi original)
The images had been disfigured. Their hands and feet had been broken. The marks of the attacks were absolutely clear.
C05-JAGTA-EN-013   ≈ p. 106 (Hindi original)
But Dr. Narayan Vyas, after looking at that image carefully, on the way back told [us] that it is the image of a yaksha.
C05-JAGTA-EN-014   ≈ p. 111 (Hindi original)
Govind tells us that most of the idols are of the avatars of Lord Vishnu. Several idols have been placed on a verandah, and some are present in the premises of the District Archaeology Museum, Vidisha. Among these is a masterpiece of the Varaha avatar too, which by luck survived whole. It was in a pair-form (*yugal*), the second part of which went to the museum. A Vishnu idol named Vishwa Mohan was rescued from smugglers about 15 years ago; this too is in the Vidisha museum.
C05-JAGTA-EN-015   ≈ p. 153 (Hindi original)
About five kilometres from here, on the bank of a river named Sonari Kevtan, is a very old village. When the water dries up, the river spews out idols, broken and whole. In the village houses, the idols found here have been kept safe.
C05-JAGTA-EN-016   ≈ p. 153 (Hindi original)   also Ch 2
Look at some of the idols a little carefully, and one has a broken hand, another a missing head. On many, the marks of blows are clear.
C05-JAGTA-EN-017   ≈ p. 160 (Hindi original)
## Geo-Heritage of Udaypur, Vidisha District, Madhya Pradesh: The Third Eye (GEOHER, 16)
The famous Udayeshwar temple, also called the Neelkantheshwar Mahadev temple, is the largest temple of Bhumija's style of architecture. A style that was propagated by the Paramars.
C05-GEOHER-001   p. 11
1) Pillars: A heavily decorative pillar with two projections made of red sandstone is noticed here.
Image 33 Decorative pillars found on the lower hill
2) Decorative Stone Fragments: Such objects are found here in multiple numbers. There are floral, geometric, and many unknown motifs.
C05-GEOHER-002   p. 52   also Ch 7
5) The Amlaka: A grounded Amlaka has been found to have eleven petals. Its current dimension says that it was large and heavy in nature.
Image 37 Part of the large amalaka … 6) The Capitals: A massive pillar capital is placed in the middle of the present path, made of red sandstone bearing thirty-one inverted lotus-like carvings.
C05-GEOHER-003   p. 54–55
Moving further up, the 1000-year-old Natraj sculpture can be seen, which is the largest such man-made sculpture of dancing anywhere in the world. This sculpture is lying on its back.
The common belief is that the sudden decline of the Parmar dynasty led to the abandonment of this project on the hilltop. Had this been completed, this would have been one of the tallest temples of Shiva anywhere else in the world.
C05-GEOHER-004   p. 19   also Ch 4
The local history, the largest native sculpture, which in local languages is called Ravan Tol, the water bodies of the area, and other historical aspects, and the landscape of the village, are evidence of the diverse cultural exchanges that have taken place in the last 1000 years.
C05-GEOHER-005   p. 46
This site has the big stepwell, the Hindu temples remain, the big 4 pillars of a temple structure, and a large number of other antiquities of Hinduism.
The significant among them are the Saptmatrika, the Navgraha, and other Hindu gods and goddesses, very prominently carved on the rock faces. The paleographic features and iconography indicate that it could be from a 9th-century CE site or maybe even earlier.
C05-GEOHER-006   p. 61   also Ch 6
The petroglyph of Lajja Gowri- The other most significant finding is the petroglyphs of relatively Lajja Gori, probably the largest one anywhere in India.
C05-GEOHER-007   p. 65
The left-hand side panel has a group of 4 images beginning with a Ganesh sculpture, which can be distinctly identified by the long snout. And the remaining three are Matrika images. The adjacent panel has four images of Matrikas, followed by the last one being Ganesh. Therefore, this panel of Saptmatrika has Ganesh flanking on both sides. Dimensions of the panel engravings present in the two parts are 1200x800mm and 1500x800mm, respectively. Due to exposure to sunlight, wind, and rain, the panels are showing signs of erosion, and the fine elements of the panels are diminishing.
C05-GEOHER-008   p. 66
Saptmatrika Panel- The Saptmatrika is adjacent to the Navagraha panel on the same face of the rock hill, facing east. This panel is divided into two parts, the left side showing 4, and the adjacent part showing the remaining three. All the matrikas at the site are in sitting posture, unlike the standing images found at other locations in the country.
C05-GEOHER-009   p. 66
This site at Udaypur Hillis is not dated, but based on the comparative study of Saptmatrika found in Pathari and Udayagiri locations of Vidisha district, it can be said that the Navagraha and Saptmatrika panels of Shiv Pindi hill belong to the same period of the 6th century CE.
C05-GEOHER-011   p. 67   also Ch 3
The middle hill rock has a Navagraha panel carved out of sandstone on the eastern side. The panel is somewhat eroded, but the Navagraha can be clearly identified. The dimension of engraving is 1800 mm x 700 mm. It is situated at a height of 3000 mm above from the base level of the platform. The style, the location, and the antiquity of the place indicate that this is the pre-Parmara period panel.
C05-GEOHER-012   p. 68
During the course of travel and investigating the open area of the hill fort sculptures were found near the fortification wall. These have now been relocated to the site museum at the village of Udaypur. A brief description of these is as follows:
Shesh Saiya- This is a Vishnu culture measuring 900 mm in length and 400 mm in width. The head of the sculpture is damaged similarly; the lower portion of the Limbs is also damaged.
C05-GEOHER-013   p. 121
Lakulisa- The third Sculpture appears to be the door panel of a temple, the center of which is the Lakulisa deity. On both sides, there are minor deities as seen on the panel.
C05-GEOHER-014   p. 122
Next on the pathway is the Natraj sculpture, just close to the hillfort. This Natraj is the largest man-made Natraj statue of the medieval era, lying on its back. It appears that the Parmar building of these grand temples was attacked by rulers of some adjoining states, and they had to abandon this temple project.
C05-GEOHER-015   p. 151
A very significant petroglyph found on the Udaypur Pindi hill is the Lajja Gauri petroglyph located on the eastern side of the middle hill. It is the engraved image of a woman in a sitting posture commonly understood as Lajja Gauri.
600mm
Lajja Gauri
The dimension of this petroglyph is 60x55 cm carved on the red sandstone on the eastern side. This assumes tremendous significance because it is the first time that such a petroglyph image of Lajja Gauri has been discovered in Central India.
C05-GEOHER-016   p. 170
This figure of Lajja Gauri is one of its kind and the figure having a head hasn't been found anywhere else. This figure is engraved on the stone surface, on the other hand, the sculpture of the fertility goddess is present in forms of sculpture in very small sizes approx. 4-5 inch. As we studied, we found out that Lajja Gauri is usually found without a head part, however, the fertility goddess found in Udaypur Pindi is carved in much more detail.
C05-GEOHER-017   p. 171
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 15)
by him in 1059 A. D., and was considered the most superb of its kind in the whole of India.
C05-GANGULY-001   p. 152   also Ch 4
achievements were marvellous. The great Nila-kantheśvara temple still stands to testify to his greatness. Thus the work set on foot by Muñja and Bhoja for erecting Malwa into an ideal state was in full operation during the reign of Udayāditya, and did not flag for want of royal patronage.*
C05-GANGULY-002   p. 153   also Ch 4
The great Nilakanthēsvara temple at Udayapur was built by Udayādiya in Sam 1116=1059 A. D. \( ^{4} \)  An
C05-GANGULY-003   p. 268   also Ch 4
The temple is still perfect and complete, \( ^{3} \) and is one of the best specimens of North-Indian architecture. It is built of hard, fine-grained red sandstone, and stands in the centre of a large paved courtyard. It consists of a shrine and a hall. The hall has three porches projecting from its side, access to them being given by flights of steps, the principal one being on the east.
C05-GANGULY-004   p. 269
In the centre of the hall or sabhâmanâpa there is a bull (Nandi), above which is a domed ceiling. Eight pillars and four pilasters support the roof of the hall. The pillars are massive but well-proportioned and beautiful. Each of them is square from the bottom to a certain height, and then rises octagonal to the top, where it is crowned by a circular cap. Over each of these caps there is a bracket capital, rendering the whole pillar from top to bottom symmetrical. The external roof of the hall is pyramidal, and is made of overlapping stones forming circular steps, diminishing in size as they ascend.
C05-GANGULY-005   p. 270
In front of the temple is a “vedi”, which is said to have been built by the king for offering a sacrifice just after the completion of the fane. It is a square chamber enclosed with stone screens.
C05-GANGULY-006   p. 271
Each hall for reading Vedas is a square building, the roof of which is supported by four massive pillars. The ceiling is, as usual, built of overlapping stones. There are four balconies, one on each side of the hall, two of which are closed with massive trellises. Each of these balconies is provided with a raised slab of stone which served as a seat for the reader.
C05-GANGULY-007   p. 272
There were four subordinate temples, one in each corner of the courtyard, and four square 'vedis' for reading 'Vedas' in, one in the middle of each side of the courtyard.
C05-GANGULY-008   p. 272
The tower of the shrine is similar in form to that of Gondesvara at Sinnar, in the Nasik District, and that of Ambaranātha, in the Thana District, and differs in detail and plan from the Khajuraho groups. Its external surface is divided into four sections by an equal number of decorated horizontal bands. Each of these divisions is covered with thirty-five miniature 'sikharas' or spires, divided equally into five rows. The bands are remarkable for their elegance of design and refined carving. The whole of the tower is surmounted by an 'āmalakasilā' and a vase.
C05-GANGULY-009   p. 270
The main tower is surrounded by miniature … 'sikharas' like that of the Pārsvanātha temple at Khajuraha. They are not so conventional as those on the temple of Nilakantheśvara at Udayapur.
C05-GANGULY-010   p. 275–276
Architecture reached a high pitch of excellence, and the temples of Udayapur and of Un rank high among the best specimens of ancient Indian architectural remains.
C05-GANGULY-011   p. 307   also Ch 4
Mr Beglar, who has made a special study of it, remarks that “It is in excellence of execution of the floral sculptured ornamentations that this temple surpasses all others. They are executed with remarkable fineness and freedom, and are not overcrowded so as to destroy effect, but by a judicious interposition of broad plain surfaces worked with exquisite care, the ornamental value both of the flowered bands and of the plain, are mutually enhanced.”
Mr. Fergusson, the well-known authority upon Eastern architecture, says that, "As every part of this temple is carved with great precision and delicacy, and as the whole is quite perfect at the present day, there are few temples of its class which give a better idea of the style than this one."
C05-GANGULY-012   p. 271   also Ch 11
The entrance-door leading to the interior of the shrine, where the deity is, is profusely decorated with sculptures. There are five jambs on each side of the door, some of which are enriched with elegant floral designs. The outermost jambs are sculptured with a series of horsemen, and thus differ in detail from the rest. On the lintel are figures of Ganesa and Śiva, and on the frieze above are the figures of five goddesses in a row.
C05-GANGULY-013   p. 271
In the north-western corner, immediately below the 'āmalakasilā,' there is a human figure elaborately dressed and ornamented, holding the lower end of a flag-staff. It is probably a statue of Udayāditya, the founder of the temple. An inscription records that Udayāditya repaired this flag-staff in 1080 A. D.' In the
C05-GANGULY-014   p. 270   also Ch 4, Ch 6
sanctum there is a big stone 'linga,' covered with a brass sheet, which was only recently added.
C05-GANGULY-015   p. 271
