## Bhoja Paramara and His Times — Mahesh Singh (BHOJA-TIMES, 12)
Vdayesvara or NlJakanthesvara Temple
The UdayeSvara temple was built by the Paramara king Udaya-
ditya at Udayapur temple in the old Gwalior state.
C05-BHOJA-TIMES-003   no page; Chapter VII, Economic Condition   also Ch 4
The mighty gable over the antarala, rising
again in successive levels, serves as an effective bond between the
super structures over the sanctum and the mapdapa, which are of
two divergent shapes. Every part is carved in a texture of exqui-
site delicacy and variety which emphasises its lineaments in the
most effective manner. The entire monument represents a unified
design.
C05-BHOJA-TIMES-006   no page; Chapter VII, Economic Condition
The ‘^ikhara’ rises with a slight curvature above the unbroken
vertical spines, in continuation of the central band below, with their
richly traccried ornamentation holding it in shape. The vertical as
well as the horizontal themes, as seen on the perpendicular cube,
are repeated on the ‘Sikhar’® The former finds eloquent expression

## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 12)
गली के आखिर में एक बहुत पुराना पत्थर का विशाल दरवाजा नजर आता है, जिसके दूसरी तरफ पर्मर राजाओं के दौर की इस इलाके की सबसे शानदार स्मारकों में से एक नीलकंठेश्वर महादेव का मंदिर है। वह संकरी और दम घोंटू गली एकमात्र रास्ता है, जो मंदिर के पिछले हिस्से में मौजूद दरवाजे तक ले जाता है। मंदिर का मुख्य पूर्व दिशा का दरवाजा न जाने कब से बंद है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* At the end of the lane a very old, huge stone gate is seen, on whose other side is the Nilkantheshvar Mahadev temple, one of the finest monuments of this region from the time of the Paramara kings. That narrow, suffocating lane is the only way to the door at the back of the temple. The temple's main east-facing door has been closed for who knows how long.
C05-JAGTA-HI-001   p. 9   also Ch 7
सही जगह स्थापित करने के लिए अनगिनत कुशल कारीगर और दक्ष श्रमिक परमारों की कार्यशालाओं से यहां आए। वास्तुविदों के शिविर में सबको अंतिम निर्देश दिए गए और देखते ही देखते मंदिर भूमि की सतह से ऊपर उठने लगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Countless skilled artisans and workers came from the Paramara workshops to set the carved stones and images in place. Final instructions were given in the architects' camp, and the temple began to rise above ground.
C05-JAGTA-HI-002   p. 28
मंदिर कब्जे में आने के बाद उसे तोड़ने का काम भी शुरू कर दिया गया था और पहली खेप में सामने आने वाली अंदर-बाहर की हर मूर्ति को मिटाया जा रहा था। मंदिर के पश्चिमी द्वार को बंद करके एक छोटी इबादतगाह तान दी गई थी और यह छह-आठ महीने में हुआ काम था। मंदिर के अंदर ले जाने वाला पश्चिमी दरवाजा उसी दौरान मंदिर परिसर में तोड़े गए मंडपों से निकले मलबे से खड़ा किया हुआ है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* After the temple came into possession, its demolition began too, and in the first round every image inside and outside was being erased. The temple's west gate was closed and a small place of worship erected, a job of six to eight months. The west doorway leading into the temple was raised in the same period from rubble of mandapas broken in the temple complex.
C05-JAGTA-HI-003   p. 108   also Ch 10
उदयपुर पत्थरों पर लिखी हुई एक कविता है, जो परमार राजाओं ने अपने आराध्य भगवान शिव की स्तुति में गाई थी। इस स्तुति को रचने में तीन साल का समय लगा था और हजारों शिल्पियों ने जाने कहाँ-कहाँ से आकर अपनी कला का सर्वश्रेष्ठ प्रदर्शन किया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udaypur is a poem written on stones that the Paramara kings sang in praise of their worshipped Lord Shiva. Composing this praise took three years, and thousands of craftsmen came from who knows where and displayed their art at its best.

*Gloss [my translation]:* This temple, completed over more than twenty years, must have been a topic of talk far and wide even while under construction.

*Gloss [my translation]:* The Nilakantheshwar temple, whose fame is far and wide, is recorded in the inscriptions of Udaypur as the Udayeshvara temple. Nowhere in contemporary inscriptions is the name Nilakantheshwar. In which century it became famous, nobody knows.
C05-JAGTA-HI-006   p. 152   also Ch 6

*Gloss [my translation]:* From the base to the shikhara several replicas of the temple itself are kept stacked one upon another. No place is empty of sculptures and carving.
C05-JAGTA-HI-007   p. 160
- उदयेश्वर महादेव परमारकालीन निर्माण का सर्वोत्कृष्ट नमूना माना जाता है। वास्तु की शास्त्रीय शब्दावली में इसे भूमिज स्थापत्य कहा जाता है जो समतल, चढ़ानी जमीन पर निर्माण कराने की विशिष्ट शैली थी। इसमें ईंट और पत्थरों से भित्तियाँ बनाई जाती थीं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Udayeshvara Mahadeva is considered the best specimen of Paramara-period construction. In the classical terminology of architecture it is called Bhumija architecture, which was a distinctive style of building on level and sloping ground. In it walls were made of brick and stone.
C05-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 164
जब मैं उनके पास आया तो देवी की एक मूर्ति पर नजर पड़ी। जमीन से करीब साढ़े छह-सात फुट ऊपर मंदिर की बाहरी भित्ति पर वह अनगिनत मूर्तियों में से एक ऐसी खंडित और बेहद खूबसूरत मूर्ति थी, जिसका सिर कलम किया गया था। जिसके हाथ बुरी तरह काट डाले गए थे। एक पैर का सुंदर पंजा भी कटा हुआ था।

The beautiful foot of one leg was also cut.

मैं हिल गया। जैसे वह बात कर रही थी। वह कुछ बोल या बता न सके इसलिए हमलावरों ने उसका सिर ही धड़ से अलग कर दिया गया था। कुछ इशारे भी न कर सके इसलिए उसकी दासों भुजाएं काट डाली गई थीं। वह मूर्ति में से निकलकर बाहर न आ जाए तो पैर के पंजे भी उड़ा दिए गए थे। हमलावरों ने बीच की किसी सदी में मंदिर पर हमला बोलकर अंदर-बाहर की मूर्तियों को भारी नुकसान पहुंचाया था।

In some intervening century attackers assaulted the temple and badly damaged the images inside and outside.

## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 10)
The temple stands in a spacious square court-yard with a rectangular projection in the centre of each side. It is enclosed with a dwarf compound wall the outer face of which was decorated with carving. A line of stone seats furnished with back rests ran all along the inner face of the enclosure wall. The compound was probably pierced with four entrances one in each cardinal point, the principal entrance being on the east towards which the temple faces.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 115
Each entrance consisted of a flight of steps guarded on either side by a figure of a dwarapala or door-keepers. All the entrances except the principal one are now closed up.
The temple was surrounded by eight attendant shrines at least six of which were crowned with spires, and sheltered
C05-MADHYABHARAT-003   p. 115
Two of these shrines have disappeared altogether, while the rest exist in the various stages of ruin. The shrine in front of the main temple is a square room which probably had a pyramidal roof. This shrine is locally known as Vedi. The exact purpose for which it was used is uncertain or it may have been a sacrificial hall (as the Sanskrit word Vedi means an altar) or else it may have been meant to shelter an idol of Shiva's bull (Nandi) which no longer exists. Occupying a corresponding position at the back of the main temple was either a similar Vedi or an ordinary attendant shrine, which has been cleared off to make room for a mosque.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-004   p. 116
This principal temple consists of a garbhagriha or shrine room a sabhamandapa or a hall and three praveshamandapas or entrance porches arranged on three sides of the hall, the main entrance porch being on the east. The idol worshipped in the shrine is a large Siva linga set on a high pedestal.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 116
In the long array of these sacred structures the Udayeshwar temple stands supreme representing the best of what the ancient architects of Madhya Bharat could achieve. Fortunately it had escaped from the ravages of the idol breakers. The crowning beauty of this temple lies in its well proportioned Shikhara and the most delicately carved medallions which adorn it.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-007   p. 48   also Ch 11
The hall and porches are surmounted with low pyramidal roofs while the shrine is crowned with a tall and beautifully designed shikhara or spire decorated with its own miniature repetitions arranged in vertical ribs and medallions inset with figures of gods.
A curious human figure fixed up near the pinnacle of the spire is variously interpreted as that of the architect, who designed … the edifice or as of the royal builder climbing up to heaven in virtue of the religious merit which he earned by constructing such a magnificent temple.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-008   p. 116–117
The temple is a fine example of the Aryavarta or Indo-Aryan style of temple architecture in its fully developed stage. The pink coloured sandstone employed in the building shows its grandeur to better effect.
The spire, doubtless the most fascinating feature of this monument, is perhaps unrivalled in beauty in the whole array of the Indian temples. As every part of this temple is carved with great precision and delicacy and as the whole is in a fairly perfect condition to the present day there are few examples of its class which give a better idea of the style of the medieval temple architecture in northern India.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-009   p. 117   also Ch 11
Artistically and architecturally the shikhārdā of the temple should be considered to be its most attractive feature. It closely resembles in style and execution the spire of the famous Nilakantheshvar Mahadeva temple at Udaypur in Bhilsa District. In both the spire is adorned with clusters of well arranged and proportioned miniatures of its own, a feature which is characteristic of most of the temples built under the Paramaras of Malwa in 11th or 12th centuries A.D. The temple here apparently belongs to this period and phase of temple architecture in Central India.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-012   p. 148   also Ch 4
In the country-side numerous temples are still standing at Suha-nia, Padhavli, Surwaya, Kadiwaha, Udaipur, Gyaraspur, Nemawar, Un, etc., which amply testify to the above observations.
It may, however, be added that, even in the midst of these peculiar circumstances, the sculptors of Madhya Bharat produced some of the finest works of which any art may well be proud. This is particularly true of the beautiful images from the temples in Gwalior fort, Suhania, Udaypur, Padhavli and Gyaraspur which can be favourably compared with similar works from elsewhere in India.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-014   p. 54
The exterior of the temple is profusely adorned with sculptures representing various gods and goddesses of the Hindu Pantheon, including Brahma, Vishnu, Ganesha, Kartikeya and the eight Guardians of the Quarters placed in their proper positions. But the figures of Shiva and his consort the goddess Durga in various forms predominate as the temple is dedicated to Shiva.
C05-MADHYABHARAT-015   p. 116

Kūtas flank also the central Laṭā, in Jhodga, a peculiarity due to its more ‘southern’ geographical position.

## Some Paramāra Temples From Madhya Pradesh: A Case Study of Village Un — Swati Mondal Adhikari (PARTEMPLES, 8)
The plan of the manḍapa of the Nilakanthésvara temple is elaborate. It has a gudhamandapa which has dvāramaṇḍapas on two sides. These dvāramaṇḍapas have kakśāsanas and their individual entrances with flanking dvārapālas.
C05-PARTEMPLES-001   p. 45
A discussion about the 'bhūmija' temple architecture by Krishnadeva in 'Studies in Indian Temple Architecture' (1975) incorporates a brief paragraph mentioning the temples of Un and brings them under the category of 'bhūmija architecture'. He describes that the Un temples are cotemporary with the Nīlkanthésvara temple of Udyapur, but the chronological sequence regarding the building of temples at Un was still wanting.
C05-PARTEMPLES-002   p. 3
In 'The Hindu Temple' (1946), a regional style was first identified by Stella Kramrisch which is classified as 'Bhumi-ja', i.e. country born. To narrate the architectural pattern, she preferred the temple of Nilkanțheśvara of Udayapur as a model and marked the essential feature of bhūmija prasāda. \( ^{1} \)  But temples of Un are ignored there.
C05-PARTEMPLES-003   p. 3   also Ch 11
Representing the quintessence of the Bhūmija temple style that was nurtured and developed by the Paramāras, the Nilakanthésvara provided the model to be followed by the later architects of Mālava.
C05-PARTEMPLES-004   p. 9
Of a saptaratha stellate plan and navabhūma in elevation, its socle is disproportionately small for its height. It also suffers from an excessive curvatures which are too much crowded. Thus inspite of being ornate than the Udayeśvara, it lacks grace and elegance.
C05-PARTEMPLES-008   p. 15
At the same time, the embellishments of other such Mālava temples of the Bhūmija class found at Un, Udaypur, Vidisha and Nimabar, the in situ and loose sculptures at Fuljhorì are static, less spontaneous and somewhat disproportionate.
C05-PARTEMPLES-009   p. 38
Temples of Un are noteworthy because artisans of Nilakanthesvara temple (Udayapur), Siddhesvara temple (Nimabar) and Śiva temple at Alirajpur were inspired by the aesthetic value of them. These later temples are more ambitious and ornate than those of Un.
C05-PARTEMPLES-010   p. 49
The number and variety of mouldings of its vedibandha are less than that of the Nilakan theśvara. In the Nilakan theśvara, the janghā presents a band of images in one horizontal row. The remaining space of the janghā is richly decorated with non-figural sculptures. Even the salilāntaras are ornamented with lotus scrolls.
C05-PARTEMPLES-011   p. 45
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 6)
The temple stands in a spacious square courtyard again enclosed with a dwarf compound wall, the outer face of which was decorated with carvings. Inner face of the enclosure wall had a line of seats furnished with back rests.
C05-GAZ-001   p. 346
to the east, all having flight of steps guarded on either side by a figure of a door-keeper.
Of the eight attendant shrines which surrounded the main temple only six are standing in ruinous condition and sheltered subsidiary gods. The main temple includes a shrine room, a hall and three entrance porches.
C05-GAZ-002   p. 347
It has an old temple assignable
to the 10th century A.D. It is known as Neelakantheshwara temple. It shelters some broken images, which resemble those at the famous Neelakantheshwara temple of Udaipur, ancient Udayapura in Basoda Tahsil.
C05-GAZ-003   p. 338   also Ch 7
The spire is tall and beautifully designed, while the hall and porches have pyramidal roofs. The spire is unrivalled in beauty and most fascinating. Precision and delicacy of carving are in perfect form. It is decorated with its own miniature repetitions arranged in vertical ribs and medallions inset with figures of gods.
C05-GAZ-004   p. 347
The temple is a fine example of Indo-Aryan style of temple architecture in its fully developed stage. The style of sculptures here closely resembles that exhibited at Khajuraho. The ornamental motives of flowers, etc., are finer than those at Khajuraho at some places.
C05-GAZ-005   p. 347
The exterior of the temple is profusely adorned with sculptures representing various deities of the Hindu pantheon, like Brahma, Vishnu, Ganesha, Kshetrapalas, etc. But among the sculptures those of Siva and Durga are predominant.
C05-GAZ-006   p. 347
## Temples of India (TEMPLES-IND, 3)
The finest temples were built at Un (District Nimar) and at Udaipur (District Vidisha). The last, known as the temple of Nilakantheshwara or Udayeshwara after its royal author Udayaditya, is the grandest specimen of Paramara architecture and was started in 1059 and completed in 1080. It is stellate on plan and consists of a sapta-ratha sanctum, a vestibule and a hall with three porches.
C05-TEMPLES-IND-005   p. 97   also Ch 4, Ch 1
surrounded by 7 subsidiary shrines, stands on an extensive platform-terrace which was originally approached through a stepped entrance flanked by large figures of Shaiva dvara-palas (doorkeepers). Great ingenuity has been employed in designing the shikhara of the sanctum which is decorated with 7 vertical and 5 horizontal rows of miniature shikharas in each quadrant, providing a picturesque setting for the play of light and shade. The sculptured medallions, inset in bold chaitya-dormers at the base of the central ratha (offset) of the shikhara on each side, forms a conspicuous feature of this monument, which vibrates with sculpture and ornaments of exquisite elegance and vivacity.
C05-TEMPLES-IND-006   p. 98
The voluptuous figures of apsarases represented on the balustrade decorating the three entrance-porches are notable for their grace and expression. This monument is remarkable for its rich ornamentation, the elegance of its accentuated shikhara design, as also for the organic unity and proportion of its constituent elements.
C05-TEMPLES-IND-025   p. 98
## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 2)
The Udayeshvara, which has no surviving precedents, is already the deluxe model, based on a 32-point star (equivalent to seven orthogonal projections), with seven bhumis (10.18b).
C05-HARDY-005   p. 194   also Ch 4
• Udayeshvara Temple, Udayapur
C05-HARDY-006   p. 2

Nine ruins of ancient temples lie here. The art here resembles the stone-craft of Udayeshvar.

## A Serpentine Scimitar of Letters (the varṇanāgakṛpāṇikā inscription at Udaypur) — Singh, A. (SERPENT, 1)
The Śiva temple of Udaypur in the Vidisha district of Madhya Pradesh, also known as Nīlakaṇtheśvara or Udayeśvara, is a celebrated example of India's medieval architecture, built around 1080 in the novel bhūmija style associated with the Paramāra dynasty of Malwa, c. 972–1305 (Deva 1975).

C06-CII-008   p. 131

\( ^{3} \)  This calculation is according to the Chaitrādi Vikrama expired; for the Kārttikādi Vikrama expired, Friday on the bright half of Kārtika fell on 11th October, 1230 A. C., and again on 18th October in the same year.
C06-CII-036   p. 281
Opening with the auspicious symbol for siddham, the inscription records the date. The year, which is expressed in numerical figures only, is 1286, which must obviously be referred to the Vikrama era: and, as the expired northern Vikrama year, it corresponds to 1229 A.C. The donation was made on the bright half of Kārttika, on a Friday; and according to the Indian Ephemeris, this week-day fell on the seventh of the bright half and again on the full-moon day of the month, respectively corresponding to the 26th October and 2nd November.
C06-CII-037   p. 281
Of the geographical names mentioned in the inscription, Bhriṅgāri (l. 8) is doubtless the same as Bhriṅgārīkā appearing in an inscription from the same place and recording the grant of a village by Lūṇapasāka in favour of the God Vaidyanātha in 1173 A.C. \( ^{5} \)  It appears to be represented by the modern village Bhiraṅgāvalī, about 6 kms. north-east of Udaipur. It is interesting to note that whereas in that inscription it is called a pathaka, in the present record it is mentioned as the principal village of the group of 64.
C06-CII-038   p. 285
Dēvadharmapuri (l. 10), if the reading is correct, seems to be the village of Udaipur itself, as we have already seen above.
C06-CII-039   p. 285
The inscription refers itself to the reign of the illustrious Dēvapāladeva, who is introduced here as endowed with all the royal titles including those of Parambhattāraka and Mahārājādhirāja. Its object is to record the donation of plots of land in some villages, the names of which cannot be definitely made out. One of the villages appears to be Dēvadharmapurī, which is probably Udaipur itself, and another, the name of which is not completely preserved, was situated in Bhringarika-64. The record is dated in the first line as on Thursday, the third of the dark half of Mārga, i.e. Mārgaśrīsha, and the year, which is expressed only in numerical symbols, is 1289; But the unit figure is indistinct owing to mutilation and also due to subsequent changes made by the scribe himself.
C06-CII-040   p. 285
\( ^{1} \)  Like that of the preceding inscription, the date must be referred to the Vikrama era, and, as calculated by Kielhorn himself, it regularly corresponds to the 2nd of December, 123[2] A.C.
Dêvapâla of the inscription is plainly identical with the homonymous Paramâra king of Dhârâ, in whose reign the preceding inscription, which is dated only three years before and was found at the same place, was incised. The present inscription, however, is important as it is the latest known epigraph of his time, though from some other sources he is known to have been living even thereafter.
C06-CII-041   p. 285
The village Kharahi (l. 9) may have been either Khiriyā, or Khadākhédī, both situated side by side at a distance of about 6 kms. and north-west of Udaipur; and Umarata (l. 7) is obviously the modern village Umarathā, in the vicinity.
C06-CII-042   p. 288; also at p. 288
denoted the town or village (pattana) after the temple of Śiva, i.e., Nilakanthésvara at Udaipur itself. Thus we have to take the same place denoted by two names, which is rather curious.
C06-CII-043   p. 288; also at p. 288   also Ch 8
While dealing with the Udaipur inscription of the time of Devapāla, dated V.S. 128[9], we have seen that the Paramāras, after their defeat at the hands of Iluttmish, succeeded in reconquering the town of Bhillasāmin (Bhilsā) from the Muslims: \( ^{3} \) and the present inscription which comes from the same place, corroborates the findings.
C06-CII-045   p. 316   also Ch 10
but it was recovered by the Paramāras, as we know from the Vidishā inscription of Trailōkya-varman, dated V.S. 1216 (1158-59 A.C.) and also from the two inscriptions of the time of Dēva-pāla, dated V.S. 1286 (1229 A.C.) and V.S. 1289 (1232 A.C.), \( ^{1} \) both found at Udaipur.
C06-CII-046   p. 320   also Ch 4, Ch 10
Of the three stanzas in the Anushṭubh metre which are incised in ll. 23-28 and below the alphabet on the left hand side, the first dedicates the Varna-nāgakṛipānikā, i.e., the alphabetical snake-scimitar, to Udayāditya: and the second stanza states that this sword of kings Udayāditya and Naravarman, the worshippers of Śiva, was ready equally for the protection of the (four) Varṇas (classes of society) and of the Varṇas (alphabet), (by encouraging learning), with a pun on the word Varṇa. The third and the last of these stanzas purports to state that this serpentine sword of king Udayāditya, intended for the protection of letters (learning) and social classes has been set up as a badge for the poets and kings (rulers). It also says that the string of (poetic) gems was composed by “the friend of the talented poets” (sukavibandhunā), which, as Sastri rightly pointed out, presumably refers to king Naravarman himself, who appears to have composed the prāsasti.² Thus the drift of all these three verses taken together goes to show, as already indicated by Sastri, that the prāsasti was incised by Naravarman himself, during his reign, to commemorate the restoration of a temple of Śiva (the Mahākāla temple itself where the stone was found),³ and also that he associated his father’s name with his own as an expression of honour and filial love.
C06-CII-052   p. 141   also Ch 4
To the proper right-side of the Sarpabandha are two short inscriptions. One of them, which is engraved at the top and measures 26 cms. broad by 6 cms. high, is the same verse beginning with Udayāditya-dēvasya, etc., and found in the allied inscriptions at Ujjain an Dhār.
C06-CII-059   p. 150
The record is inscribed on the sunken panel of a sand-stone slab measuring 95·5 cms. high and 78 cms. broad, with a broad border on all the four sides. The inscribed portion is about 60 cms. high by 53 cms.
C06-CII-060   p. 163
The characters are Nāgarī of the late eleventh or early twelfth century A.C., as shown by the usual peculiarities of the forms of letters like kh in mukha, l.
C06-CII-061   p. 164
A characteristic feature of the writing is that a slightly vertical stroke resembling a hook is attached to the left of the top strokes of some of the letters, e.g. in the initial lines, — a tendency which has changed their forms, e.g., chha appears as stha in l. 44, nā as mā in l. 46, and tuñiravaka as vunivaka in l. 48. This tendency appears also in some other inscriptions of the time, as in the Udaipur prasastr.
C06-CII-066   p. 432
Another interest of the inscription lies in its mentioning Dêvapâla with the paramount epithets of Paramabhattâraka and Mahârajâdhiraja in ll. 4-6. The Harsûd inscription, no doubt applies these titles to his name, but along with them he bears also that of Mahâkumâra; in his Mândhâtâ grant he is called only a Mahâraja; and in the preceding inscription too, though it claims for him the high-sounding epithets, we have for him the title only of a Mahâraja in l. 5. Thus the present record is the only epigraphic evidence to claim Imperial titles for Devapâla.
C06-CII-082   p. 285   also Ch 4
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 41)

*Gloss [my translation]:* Among the dozen inscriptions found in Udaypur it is the most important because it is of 1080. The oldest. From the time of Udayaditya.
C06-JAGTA-HI-002   p. 125
युवा इतिहासविद संतोष कुमार वर्मा के अनुसार यह शिलालेख विक्रम संवत् 1137 वैशाख सुदि 7 का है। इसमें दिन का उल्लेख नहीं है। इससे सूर्य के संक्रमण के साथ-साथ करण का उल्लेख है परंतु उसका नाम नहीं दिया गया है। विक्रम संवत् के आधार पर की गई गणितीय गणना के अनुसार यह तिथि 30 मार्च 1080 ईस्वी दिन सोमवार आती है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* According to young historian Santosh Kumar Verma this inscription is of Vikram Samvat 1137 Vaishakh sudi 7. It does not mention the day. Along with the sun's transit it mentions the karana but does not give its name. By mathematical calculation based on Vikram Samvat the date comes to Monday, 30 March 1080 CE.
C06-JAGTA-HI-003   p. 127   also Ch 11
शिलालेख लेखन की उस समय की परंपरा के अनुसार इन पंक्तियों के लेखक का नाम भी दर्ज है। यह पंडित श्रृंगवास के पुत्र पंडित महिपाल की महान रचना है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* According to the inscription-writing tradition of the time, the writer of these lines is also recorded. It is the great work of Pandit Mahipal, son of Pandit Shringvas.
C06-JAGTA-HI-004   p. 127
इस शिलालेख की अहमियत इसलिए सबसे ज्यादा है क्योंकि इसमें दर्ज तिथि महाराज उदयादित्य के शासन काल की पहली दर्ज तिथि है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The importance of this inscription is greatest because the date recorded in it is the first recorded date of Maharaja Udayaditya's reign.
C06-JAGTA-HI-005   p. 128   also Ch 4
उन्होंने अब तक पढ़े जा चुके शिलालेखों की बारीक छानबीन की है। सिर्फ अनुवाद ही नहीं बल्कि बहुत अच्छे विश्लेषण के साथ परमारों के समय के ही दूसरे शिलालेखों से उनकी तुलना करके एक हजार साल पुराने दौर को समझने और समझाने का प्रासंगिक प्रयास किया है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* He has closely examined the inscriptions read so far. Not just translation but, with very good analysis, comparing them with other inscriptions of the Paramara period, he has made a relevant attempt to understand and explain a thousand-year-old era.
C06-JAGTA-HI-006   p. 128   also Ch 11
लिखा है कि उक्त तिथि को महाराजा उदयदित्य द्वारा श्री उदयेश्वरदेव मंदिर पर ध्वजारोहण के मांगलिक कार्य को सम्पन्न कराया गया। वर्तमान मंदिर का नाम "उदयेश्वर देव का मंदिर" लिखा गया है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It says that on the said date Maharaja Udayaditya performed the auspicious flag-hoisting at the Shri Udayeshvaradeva temple. The present temple's name is written as 'Temple of Udayeshvara Deva'.
C06-JAGTA-HI-007   p. 129   also Ch 5
श्लोकोयं पंडित श्री श्रृंगवाससूनोः । (4)**
करण की। यह श्लोक पंडित श्री श्रृंगवास के पुत्र
**पंडित श्री महिपालस्य ।। संवत् 1137 वैसा(शा)ख सुदि ७ (5)**
*Gloss [my translation]:* This shloka is by Pandit Shri Shringvasa's son, Pandit Shri Mahipala. Samvat 1137 Vaisakha sudi 7.
C06-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 129
**स्वस्ति । एकच्छत्रां करोतु क्षमामुदयदित्य भूपति (1)**
अनुवाद - स्वस्ति । उदयदित्य नरेश पृथ्वी को एकछत्र बनावें ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Hail. May King Udayaditya make the earth under a single umbrella.
C06-JAGTA-HI-009   p. 129
संवत् 1137 वैसाख सुदि ७
**श्रीमुदयेश्व(श्र)र देवस्य ध्वजारोहः संपूर्णः ।। मंगलं महाश्रीःः।। (6)**
श्रीमत् उदयेश्वर देव का ध्वजारोह (समारोह) सम्पन्न (हुआ)। महालक्ष्मी मंगलकारी हो।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Samvat 1137 Vaisakha sudi 7. The flag-hoisting of Shri Udayeshvara Deva is completed. May great fortune (Mahalakshmi) be auspicious.
C06-JAGTA-HI-010   p. 129   also Ch 5
उदयपुर मंदिर के ही एक और शिलालेख में विक्रम संवत् 1116 और शक संवत 981 की तिथि अंकित है। अंग्रेजी कैलेंडर के हिसाब से यह साल 1059 ईस्वी है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In another inscription of the Udaypur temple the date Vikram Samvat 1116 and Shaka Samvat 981 is engraved. By the English calendar this year is 1059 CE.
C06-JAGTA-HI-011   p. 130   also Ch 4
प्रसिद्ध उदयपुर प्रशस्ति में परमार वंश के बारे में उदयादित्य तक का सर्वाधिक विस्तृत विवरण है। यह लेख स्वयं उदयादित्य ने तैयार कराया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The famous Udaypur prashasti has the most detailed account of the Paramara dynasty up to Udayaditya. This text was prepared by Udayaditya himself.
C06-JAGTA-HI-012   p. 132   also Ch 4
यह पूरी तरह पद्यमय है। पत्थर काफी घिसा हुआ है। बहुत मुश्किल से ही कुछ पढ़ा जा सका है। जितना भी पढ़ा गया, वह उदयपुर के अतीत की शानदार झलक दिखाता है।
इसका उल्लेख एनुअल रिपोर्ट ऑफ द आर्कियोलॉजिकल डिपार्टमेंट इन ग्वालियर स्टेट 1925-26, क्रमांक 71, पृष्ठ 12-13 पर है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is entirely in verse. The stone is much worn. Only very little could be read. Whatever was read shows a splendid glimpse of Udaypur's past. It is mentioned in the Annual Report of the Archaeological Department in Gwalior State 1925-26, no. 71, pages 12-13.
C06-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 136
परमार वंश की जानकारी का सबसे महत्वपूर्ण स्रोत 'उदयपुर प्रशस्ति' है। यह परमार वंश की उत्पत्ति से लेकर राजा उद्यादित्य तक उनकी उपलब्धियों का वर्णन पेश करता है। यह अभिलेख खण्डित है। उदयपुर के नीलकण्ठेश्वर मंदिर के प्रांगण में मिला यह पत्थर उदयपुर के इतिहास की सबसे महत्वपूर्ण गवाही है। उदयपुर का यह सबसे कीमती पत्थर ग्वालियर के संग्रहालय में रखा है। इसकी भाषा संस्कृत और लिपि 11 वीं सदी की नागरी लिपि है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The most important source of information on the Paramara dynasty is the 'Udaypur prashasti'. It presents an account of their achievements from the origin of the Paramara dynasty to King Udayaditya. This inscription is damaged. Found in the courtyard of the Nilakanteshwar temple of Udaypur, this stone is the most important testimony of Udaypur's history. This most precious stone of Udaypur is kept in the Gwalior museum. Its language is Sanskrit and the script is 11th-century Nagari.
C06-JAGTA-HI-014   p. 138   also Ch 4
ऊँ नमः शिवाय, जिसके मस्तक पर चन्द्रमा की निर्मल कांति युक्त कला कल्पवल्लि
के समान शोभायमान हैं तथा जिनके गले में गंगाजल से सिंचित सर्पमाला है, ऐसे शंभु
तुम्हारा कल्याण करें ॥१॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* Om Namah Shivaya. May Shambhu, on whose forehead the crescent of the moon shines like a wish-fulfilling creeper and around whose neck is a serpent garland sprinkled with Ganga water, bring you well-being.
C06-JAGTA-HI-015   p. 139   also Ch 8
मदमस्त नन्दी के हाथों से बजाये गये हुए (मूदंग) नाद से तथा नारद के मनोहर
गायनों के साथ स्वर्ग की अप्सराएं सदा जिसके आगे नृत्य करती हैं ऐसे शिव तुम्हारा
कल्याण करें ॥२।
*Gloss [my translation]:* With the sound of the (mridanga) played by the intoxicated Nandi's hands and with the charming songs of Narada, the heavenly apsaras always dance before whom, may that Shiva bring you well-being.
C06-JAGTA-HI-016   p. 139   also Ch 5, Ch 8
मगर उदयपुर के अपने अंधेरे गर्भ में बहुत कुछ समाया हुआ है, जो अपना परिचय देने को व्याकुल है। उसकी कहानी अनगिनत शिलालेखों पर है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* But in Udaipur's own dark womb much is contained, eager to introduce itself. Its story is on countless inscriptions.
C06-JAGTA-HI-017   p. 8
इस दरवाजे के एक तरफ एक शानदार चौकोर पत्थर के फ्रेम में आठ पंक्तियों का संस्कृत शिलालेख बचा हुआ है। दूसरी तरफ उतने ही आकार का शिलालेख खाली है। या तो उस पर कुछ लिखा नहीं गया या लिखा हुआ छील दिया गया। देखकर लगता नहीं कि कुछ लिखा गया होगा। वह बिल्कुल कोरी स्लेट की तरह साफ था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* On one side of this door, in a splendid square stone frame, an eight-line Sanskrit inscription survives. On the other side an inscription frame of the same size is empty. Either nothing was written on it or what was written was scraped off. It does not look as if anything was written; it was clean like a blank slate.
C06-JAGTA-HI-018   p. 11
मंदिर के पिछले हिस्से में दोनों तरफ हवा में दो दरवाजे बने हैं। यह मंदिर के ही खंडित टुकड़ों को जोड़कर खड़े किए गए, जिन पर अरबी या फारसी के दो शिलालेख हैं। डॉ. मिश्र ने इन्हें किसी के पास पढ़ने के लिए भेजा। एक दिन उन्होंने बताया कि मोहम्मद बिन तुगलक का नाम इनमें साफतौर पर पढ़ लिया गया। ये शिलालेख जगला हुआ कथा 1336-1338 के हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* At the back of the temple two free-standing doorways, built from the temple's own broken pieces, carry two Arabic or Persian inscriptions. Dr Mishra had them read; Muhammad bin Tughlaq's name was clearly read in them. They date to 1336-1338.
C06-JAGTA-HI-019   p. 24   also Ch 10
अब वे मंदिर के पीछे एक दीवार बनाने का प्रयास करने लगे। इसके लिए वो सारे पत्थर मंदिर से ही तोड़कर निकाल रहे थे। अपनी भाषा में दो शिलालेख बनाकर दो द्वारों में लगा दिए। एक पर लिखा नाम अब तक याद है-‘मोहम्मद बिन तुगलका।’ जब वह मेरी प्रतिमा के सामने आया तो देवता के एक कटे हुए सिर को जोर की ठोकर मारकर दो शब्द बोला-‘बुतपरस्ती हराम।’ मैं इसका अर्थ नहीं समझी, लेकिन उसकी घोर घृणा का अनुभव बहुत निकट से किया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Now they tried to build a wall behind the temple, breaking out all the stones from the temple itself. They made two inscriptions in their own language and fixed them in two doorways. One name I still remember: 'Mohammad bin Tughlaq'.
C06-JAGTA-HI-020   p. 29   also Ch 10
परमार वंश के राजा उदयादित्य के मंदिर में तीनों द्वारों के पत्थरों पर बीस से ज्यादा
शिलालेख हैं, जो उस समय की महत्वपूर्ण जानकारी देते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* On the stones of the three doorways of King Udayaditya's temple of the Paramara dynasty there are more than twenty inscriptions that give important information about that time.
C06-JAGTA-HI-021   p. 33   also Ch 11
किसी भी द्वार से गर्भगृह प्रवेश सबसे आखिर में करेंगे तो दोनों तरफ आठ-नौ सौ
साल पुराने कई शिलालेख मिलेंगे। इनमें महाराज उदयादित्य का नाम भी मिलेगा।
मूर्तियां अंदर तक तोड़ी गई थीं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Entering the sanctum last through any door, you find on both sides many inscriptions eight to nine hundred years old. Maharaj Udayaditya's name is among them. Images were broken even inside.
C06-JAGTA-HI-022   p. 40; also at p. 40
एक अधिकारी ने बताया कि एक पेज का एक दस्तावेज उनकी ओर से किसी वकील
ने तहसीलदार के समक्ष पेश किया था। किसी अफसर के हाथों वह कागज मुझे भी
भेजा गया। वह फारसी के किसी मूल लेख का साल 2008 में हिंदी में किया गया एक
अनुवाद था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* An official said a one-page document had been presented by a lawyer before the tehsildar. It was also sent to me by an officer. It was a Hindi translation, made in 2008, of some original Persian text.
C06-JAGTA-HI-023   p. 44–45   also Ch 10
कि यह मुगल हमलावर बाबर की सम्मानजनक उपाधि है। फिरदौस मकानी यानी
जिसका मकान जन्नत में हो। फिरदौस यानी जन्नत।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Know that this is the respectful title of the Mughal invader Babur. Firdaus Makani means one whose house is in paradise; Firdaus means paradise.
C06-JAGTA-HI-024   p. 47
परमार राजाओं के बारे में उदयपुर से प्राप्त एक सबसे पुराना लेख सन् 1080 का है और दूसरा सन् 1229 का, जो देवपालदेव का है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The oldest record about Paramara kings obtained from Udaipur is of the year 1080 and another is of 1229, which is of Devapaladeva.
C06-JAGTA-HI-025   p. 50   also Ch 4
वे कुछ शिलालेखों
को आर्किओलॉजिकल सर्वे ऑफ इंडिया के आर्काइव से निकाल लाए, जो उदयपुर
के थे और उनका अंग्रेजी अनुवाद काफी पहले किया जा चुका था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* He brought out from the archives of the Archaeological Survey of India some inscriptions that were of Udaipur and whose English translation had been made long ago.
C06-JAGTA-HI-026   p. 73   also Ch 11
आप मंदिर प्रांगण में देखेंगे कि मंदिर के पिछले पश्चिमी हिस्से में बिल्कुल मंदिर से सटाकर एक दीवार खड़ी करने की नाकाम कोशिश की गई थी। मंदिर के दोनों तरफ उत्तर और दक्षिण में दो दरवाजे बनाए गए थे। ये आज तक खड़े हैं, जिन पर तुगलक के शिलालेख हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the temple courtyard you will see an unsuccessful attempt to raise a wall right against the rear western part of the temple. Two doorways were built on both sides of the temple, north and south. They stand to this day, and bear Tughlaq's inscriptions.
C06-JAGTA-HI-027   p. 107   also Ch 5, Ch 10
मंदिर के पूर्वी प्रवेश द्वार के दोनों तरफ अनगिनत शिलालेखों को छूते हुए ही आप मंदिर में भीतर दाखिल होते हैं। इनमें बाईं तरफ ऊपर ही कोने में एक शिलालेख में "श्रीमान उदयादित्यनृपति" का नाम साफ देख सकते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* You enter the temple touching countless inscriptions on both sides of the temple's eastern entrance. In the upper corner on the left you can clearly see the name 'Shriman Udayadityanripati' in one inscription.
C06-JAGTA-HI-028   p. 128
यह अभिलेख उदयपुर के मंदिर परिसर में नहीं बल्कि चतुआ दरवाजा मुहल्ले में एक घर से प्राप्त हुआ था। पत्थर का यह कीमती टुकड़ा आज ग्वालियर के पुरात्व संग्रहालय में है। इसमें कुल 27 पंक्तियाँ हैं। उदयपुर प्रशस्ति की ही तरह इसकी भाषा संस्कृत है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* This inscription was obtained not in the temple complex of Udaypur but from a house in the Chatua Darwaza locality. This precious piece of stone is today in the Gwalior archaeological museum. It has 27 lines in all. Like the Udaypur prashasti its language is Sanskrit.
C06-JAGTA-HI-029   p. 136
विदिशा जिले में संभवतः जयसिंह ही पहला परमार शासक है, जिसका लेख यहां मिलता है। इसमें भी परमार राजाओं की वंशावली उदयादित्यदेव तक ही है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In Vidisha district Jayasimha is probably the first Paramara ruler whose inscription is found here. In this too the genealogy of Paramara kings goes only up to Udayadityadeva.
C06-JAGTA-HI-030   p. 137
उदयपुर के अभिलेखों में यह सबसे महत्वपूर्ण है और इसी क्रम में उदयपुर से कुल 11 अभिलेख मिलते हैं, जो इस काल की राजनीतिक, आर्थिक और सामाजिक स्थितियों के बारे में बताते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Among Udaypur's inscriptions this is the most important, and in this series a total of 11 inscriptions are found from Udaypur, which tell about the political, economic and social conditions of this period.
C06-JAGTA-HI-031   p. 143
यह अभिलेख मंदिर के पूर्वी द्वार के दाहिनी ओर एक स्तम्भ पर है।

## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 36)
The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{1} \) of the reign of Udayâditya (about 1072 A. D.) is the earliest known inscription of the main family of Dhârâ to register the mythical birth of the founder of the house.
C06-GANGULY-001   p. 18   also Ch 4
conjugal union of the Siddhas is perfect. There, Viśvâ-mitra forcibly took from Vasiṣṭha (his) cow. Through his (Vasiṣṭha's) power, a hero arose from the fire-pit, who worked the destruction of the enemy's army. When he had slain the enemies, he brought back the cow; then that sage spoke: "Thou wilt become a lord (of kings) called 'Paramâra'." The other inscriptions, which in general narrate the same story, are:—
C06-GANGULY-002   p. 19   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti tells us that he “gained the high honour of kingship by his valour.” \( ^{2} \)
C06-GANGULY-003   p. 33   also Ch 4
Vaidya denies the succession of Vairisimha I, Siyaka I, and Vákpati I, and remarks that the Udayapur Prasasti's account regarding those three kings is conventional (History of Mediaeval Hindu India, Vol. II, p. 123). I do not find any ground in support of this assertion. Padmagupta, who is one of the earliest authorities to give the history of the Paramāra dynasty of Malwa, tells us that Vairisimha II's father was Vákpati I. He further states, as we have already seen, that between Vákpati I and Upendra there came a number of other rulers. In these circumstances it cannot be asserted that the Udayapur Prasasti's account regarding the immediate successors of Upendra is altogether conventional.
C06-GANGULY-004   p. 43
The Udayapur Prasasti of the rulers of Malwa informs us that Upendra was followed by Vairisimha I, Siyaka I, and Vákpati I. \( ^{2} \)  Padmagupta, though he does not expressly mention the names of Vairisimha I, and Siyaka I, confirms the fact of their succession by stating that between Upendra and Vákpati I there came a number of rulers.
C06-GANGULY-005   p. 43   also Ch 4
His successor was Vakpati I, who probably ascended the throne about the year 890-91 A. D.
Verse 10 of the Udayapur Praśasti \( ^{2} \) describes this chief as “a sun for (those) water-lilies, the eyes of the maidens of Avanti.” This indirectly proves his supreme control over that country. He is compared with Šatamakha (Indra) \( ^{3} \) for his military valour, and his armies are said to have drunk the waters of Ganga and of the ocean.
C06-GANGULY-006   p. 45   also Ch 4
Verse 11 of the Udayapur Prasasti seems to give a hint to that effect. It records that "By that king (Vairisimha II) the famous Dhârâ was indicated, when he slew the crowd of his enemies with the edge of his sword." Mr. Buhler remarks that this expression means, "smiting the foe with the edge of his sword the king indicated that Dhârâ belonged to him."
C06-GANGULY-007   p. 50   also Ch 4
seems to have gone to war with him and to have won a decisive victory. The Udayapur prasasti records his victory over the people of Lāta.
C06-GANGULY-008   p. 70   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{2} \) relates Vākpati's victory over the Colas and the Keralas.
C06-GANGULY-009   p. 70
The Udayapur prasasti also records his victory over the Karnâta army. But all these preliminary victories gained by him, could not break down the strength of the Câlukyas.
C06-GANGULY-010   p. 72   also Ch 4
The fight with the Hûnas, which began during the reign of Siyaka, was now brought to a successful termination. Both the Udayapur prasasti \( ^{5} \) and the Navasâhasânka -carita \( ^{6} \) glorify his success over the Hûna king.
C06-GANGULY-011   p. 91   also Ch 4
Both the Kalvan inscription and the Udayapur prasasti record his victory over the Karnâtas. But his final attempt to establish supremacy over the Deccan seems to have ended in signal failure.
C06-GANGULY-012   p. 105   also Ch 4
Almost about the same time that Bhoja was at war with the Câlukya Jayasimha, he came into conflict with a powerful prince named Indraratha. His decisive victory over Indraratha is recorded by the Udayapur prasasti.
C06-GANGULY-013   p. 109   also Ch 4
The Kalvan inscription and the Udayapur prasasti both record Bhoja's conquest of Lāta.
C06-GANGULY-014   p. 110   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{4} \) records that Bhoja conquered the Turuşkas by means of his mercenaries.
C06-GANGULY-015   p. 114   also Ch 4
Bhoja, with his skilful warriors, marched against Tripuri and won a decisive victory over Gāngeya. Both the Kalvan inscription and the Udayapur prasasti extol Bhoja's victory over the Cedi king.
C06-GANGULY-016   p. 116   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti tells us that ' Bhoja conquered the lord of Cedi, Indraratha, Toggala (?), Bhima, Karpâtas, the lord of Lâta, the king of the Gurjaras, and the Turuşkas. The Bhima mentioned here was evidently the Caulukya king of Gujarat; hence the king of the Gurjaras referred to in the same connection, may be taken to mean some other ruling chief, who is to be identified with the Pratihâra prince of Kanauj.
C06-GANGULY-017   p. 121   also Ch 4
Bhoja's victory over Bhtma is described by the Udayapur prasasti.
C06-GANGULY-018   p. 130   also Ch 4
A general description of Bhoja's glorious victory over this caulukya king is given in the Udayapur prasasti. \( ^{2} \)  Though at present there is no direct evidence to prove that he wrested Mount Abu from the Caulukyas, the fact that Dhandhu's son and successor, Pūrnapāla, was ruling over it in the middle of the 11th century A. D., quite independently of Gujarat, \( ^{3} \)  leads us to that conclusion.
C06-GANGULY-019   p. 131   also Ch 4
Udayapur prasasti tells us that “when that devotee of Bharga (Śiva é. E. Bhoja), whose brilliance resembled that of the sun, had gone to the mansion of the gods, the earth, like Dhârâ, was filled with the dense darkness of his foes, (and) his hereditary warriors became infirm in body. Then arose king Udayāditya, as it were another sun, destroying the darkness of powerful foes, with the rays issuing from his sword, (and thus) gladdening the hearts of his people by his splendour. Lo! how easy was the rescue of this earth for that Paramâra, by whom the primeval boar was restored without difficulty.”
C06-GANGULY-020   p. 146   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti, \( ^{2} \) which has often been referred to above, is engraved on a slab of stone, and is now lying in front of the Nīla-kantheśvara temple, at Udayapur, in Bhilsa. It is highly important and interesting, inasmuch as it furnishes us with much information of great significance for the reconstruction of the early history of the Paramāras of Malwa. It is the only available record which supplies a complete genealogy of this family of rulers from Upendra to Bhoja. It is undated, and seems to have been issued during the early part of the king's reign.
C06-GANGULY-021   p. 149   also Ch 4
It is to be borne in mind that the 'Praśasti' was composed by the king Naravarman, the younger brother and successor of Laksmadeva. It contains fifty-eight verses, of which nineteen are devoted to the praise of Laksmadeva, three to Udayâditya and three to Bhoja, the glory of the Paramâras. The poet-king is extremely vague in describing the military achievements of all his predecessors and of himself, with the exception of Laksmadeva, of whose exploits he writes particularly definitely.
C06-GANGULY-022   p. 166   also Ch 4
The Udayapur prasasti tells us that Bhoja beautified the world by covering it with temples, dedicated to Kedâresvara, Râmesvara, Somanâtha, Sumḍîra (?), Kâla, Anala and Rudra. I do not think that this, by any means, refers to the king's participation in the building of the great temple of Somanâtha in Kathiawar or of Râmesvara in Southern India, as suggested by some historians. Besides the great temple of Râmesvara in Madura and the temple of Somanâtha in Kathiawar, there were others dedicated to the same gods in different provinces of India. At Belur, in the Bombay Presidency, there is an old temple of Râmesvara. Similarly, a temple of Somanâtha is to be found in Deotalao in the Rewa State. Consideration of these things leads me to believe that all the temples mentioned in the Udayapur prasasti were built in Malwa, though none of them can be traced now.
C06-GANGULY-023   p. 268   also Ch 4
His son Vākpatirāja, while doing full justice to his regal position by his care for the political interests of his realm, spared no effort to further the cause of the literary movement also. The Udayapur prasasti tells us that he cultivated eloquence, lofty poetry, the art of reasoning, and a complete mastery over the rules of the Sāstras.
C06-GANGULY-025   p. 285
The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{4} \) glorifies Bhoja as the king of poets.
C06-GANGULY-026   p. 288   also Ch 4
Two short inscriptions, \( ^{2} \) one of which is dated Sam 1137=1080 A. D., have been found in the temple of Nilakanthesvara.
C06-GANGULY-027   p. 149
Professor Kielhorn suggests the identification of Mahauḍa with the village Mahod, about 25 miles south of Satajuna.² (iii) The remaining two records² of the king's reign were discovered inside the entrance of the great temple of Udayapur in Bhilsa. One is dated Sam. 1286, Kârttika sudi, which corresponds to 1229 A. D.
C06-GANGULY-028   p. 221   also Ch 10
D., and the other is Sam. 128(9?), the last numeral being indistinct. Professor Kielhorn suggests with great doubt that it is "9". If he is right, the date corresponds to 1232 A. D. The second inscription was issued when Mahârâjâdhirâja Devapâla, "the ornament of kings", was residing in Udayapur.
C06-GANGULY-029   p. 221
4 Professor Kielhorn makes mention of an inscription, dated Sam. 1311=1255 A. D, found at Udayapur, in which he
C06-GANGULY-030   p. 234   also Ch 10

C06-GANGULY-031   p. 235
But for some time longer the banners of its independence was kept unfurled in Bhilsa by Jayasimha III, who was probably the successor of Mahlak Deo. An inscription inside the east entrance of the great temple of Udayapur, tells us that in Sam. 1366=1909 A. D. Mahārājādhirāja Śri-Jayasimha was ruling there. We know nothing further about him or of any of his successors.

He had a profound veneration for men of letters. The educational institute, established by Bhoja at Dhāra, flourished under his careful supervision. He engraved charts, containing alphabets and rules of grammar, for the guidance of the students, on the walls of Bhoja's school at Dhāra, and also in the Chauberdera Temple at Un, in the Nimar District. He declared that they were placed there for the gratification of poets and princes.

## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 36)
The oldest inscription about the Paramara kings obtained from Udaipur is of the year 1080, and the second is of the year 1229, which is of Devpaldev.
C06-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 51 (Hindi original)
He brought out some inscriptions from the archive of the Archaeological Survey of India, which [pertained to] Udaipur
were of Udaipur, and their English translation had been done quite a while ago.
C06-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 72 (Hindi original)   also Ch 11
Those countless inscriptions are the documents engraved upon the very stones of that relationship. Pandit Shringavas and Pandit Mahipal are saying something to us the very moment we enter the temple.
C06-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 121 (Hindi original)
It was written in six lines on a stone. It was inscribed on the right-hand side of the temple's east-facing *[the source reads "पूरे," apparently for "पूर्व" — east]* doorway. In it is recorded Vaishakh Sudi 7, Vikram Samvat 1137. According to the English calendar, this date works out to 30 March 1080.
C06-JAGTA-EN-004   ≈ p. 125 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4, Ch 5
In keeping with the inscription-tradition — like the print-line or byline printed in today's newspapers — the name of the gentleman who "broke" this news is also recorded in it. He is Pandit Mahipal, the illustrious son of Pandit Shringavas, who is telling of the flag-hoisting ceremony in the temple.
C06-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 125 (Hindi original)
According to the young historian Santosh Kumar Verma, this inscription is of Vikram Samvat 1137, Vaishakh Sudi 7. In it the day of the week is not mentioned. In it, along with the sun's transit (*sankraman*), the *karana* is mentioned, but its name is not given. According to the mathematical calculation done on the basis of Vikram Samvat, this date comes to Monday, 30 March 1080 CE.
C06-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 127 (Hindi original); also at ≈ p. 129 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
The composer of this writing is Pandit Mahipala, the beloved son (*chiranjeev*) of Pandit Shringavas. The sixth and last line of this very brief writing—
—is an important "live" piece of information. It is written that, on the said date, the auspicious act of flag-hoisting upon the Shri Udayeshvardev temple was accomplished by Maharaja Udayaditya. The present temple's name has been written as "the temple of Udayeshvar Dev." When, in Dr.
C06-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 128–129 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4, Ch 5
The importance of this inscription is the greatest, because the date recorded in it is the first recorded date of the reign of Maharaja Udayaditya. In the other inscriptions, this is the one deficiency."
C06-JAGTA-EN-008   ≈ p. 128 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
> *Translation —* The flag-hoisting of Shrimat Udayeshvar Dev was completed. May Mahalakshmi (*Maha-Shri*) be the bestower of auspiciousness.
C06-JAGTA-EN-009   ≈ p. 129 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
> *Translation —* Svasti (Hail). May King Udayadity make the earth [united] under a single umbrella (i.e., one sovereignty).
C06-JAGTA-EN-010   ≈ p. 129 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
In yet another inscription of the Udaipur temple itself, the date Vikram Samvat 1116 and Shak Samvat 981 is inscribed. According to the English calendar, this year is 1059 CE. This is a rather large writing. This inscription — of twenty-two lines in all, and teeming with a plethora of errors — has not only impure language and grammar, but has deficiencies from the standpoint of craft too. These are the sort of reasons that make a meaningful translation difficult. This one too, like the Paramaras' other inscriptions, begins with the worship of Shiva, and then goes on to begin telling about Udayaditya.
C06-JAGTA-EN-011   ≈ p. 131 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
In the famous Udaipur Prashasti there is the most detailed description of the Paramara dynasty, up to Udayaditya. This writing was itself got prepared by Udayaditya.
C06-JAGTA-EN-012   ≈ p. 133 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
Like the Udaipur Prashasti, its language is Sanskrit. It is entirely in verse. The stone is quite worn; only with great difficulty could anything be read. Whatever was read shows a splendid glimpse of Udaipur's past.
Its mention is in the *Annual Report of the Archaeological Department in Gwalior State, 1925–26*, serial no. 71, pages 12–13.
C06-JAGTA-EN-013   ≈ p. 137 (Hindi original)
This inscription was obtained not in Udaipur's temple precinct, but from a house in the Chatua Darwaza locality. This precious piece of stone is today in the Archaeology Museum of Gwalior. In it there are twenty-seven lines in all.
C06-JAGTA-EN-014   ≈ p. 137 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
This inscription is fragmentary. This stone, found in the courtyard of the Nilkantheshvar temple of Udaipur, is the most important testimony of Udaipur's history. This most precious stone of Udaipur is kept in the museum of Gwalior. Its language is Sanskrit, and its script the Nagari script of the 11th century. No date is inscribed on it, but according to the information obtained from this inscription, it appears to be of the time of King Udayadity, the successor of King Bhoj.
C06-JAGTA-EN-015   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)
This too was printed in the seventh and tenth reports of the survey (1874–75 and 1876–77) by Major General Alexander Cunningham, the first head of the Archaeological Survey of India. J. Bühler mentioned it in the first volume of *Epigraphia Indica*, 1888.
C06-JAGTA-EN-016   ≈ p. 139 (Hindi original)
He upon whose forehead the crescent-digit, endowed with the pure radiance of the moon, shines like a wish-fulfilling creeper (*kalpavalli*), and around whose neck is a garland of serpents sprinkled with the water of the Ganga — may that Shambhu work your welfare.
C06-JAGTA-EN-017   ≈ p. 140 (Hindi original)
Its story is on countless inscriptions.
C06-JAGTA-EN-018   ≈ p. 8 (Hindi original)
On one side of this door, in a splendid square stone frame, an eight-line Sanskrit inscription survives. On the other side, an inscription of the same size is blank. Either nothing was written on it, or what was written was scraped off. To look at, it seems nothing could have been written; it was clean, exactly like a blank slate.
C06-JAGTA-EN-019   ≈ p. 11 (Hindi original)
In the rear part of the temple, on both sides, two doors have been built in the air. They were erected by joining the very broken pieces of the temple, on which there are two Arabic or Persian inscriptions. Dr. Mishra sent these to someone to be read. One day he told me that the name of Mohammad bin Tughlaq had been clearly read in them. These inscriptions, [connected with a] recorded event, are of 1336–1338.

There is huge historical wealth and several inscriptions on the hill written in Devanagari script.

## A Serpentine Scimitar of Letters (the varṇanāgakṛpāṇikā inscription at Udaypur) — Singh, A. (SERPENT, 9)
Less well known is its status as an extraordinary epigraphic archive with a continuous sequence of inscriptions in Sanskrit, Persian, and Hindi spanning eight centuries—from the temple's consecration in samvat 1137 to its archaeological restoration in samvat 1985. Almost all the inscriptions have been painstakingly copied and systematically listed in the annual reports of the archaeology department of Gwalior State (1923-24, 1925-26, 1927-28, 1928-29) and those of the epigraphy branch of the Archaeological Survey of India (ARIEp 1961-62, 1981-82, 1982-83). Only a selection of these have been fully published (Trivedi 1978, 1989; Mittal 1979), but taken together the corpus offers a wealth of information about the biography of a north Indian temple.
C06-SERPENT-001   p. 1   also Ch 11
This paper presents a newly-discovered inscription from Udaypur (district Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh). Engraved on a rectangular sandstone slab in the parapet wall of the Udayeśvara temple, this inscription features serpent's knotted body comprising letters of the Sanskrit alphabet along with the grammatical endings of nouns and verbs. It is accompanied by 5 lines of writing on one side that refer to the diagram as varṇnanāgakrpānikā, a “serpentine scimitar of letters.” The compounded designation draws attention to the unusual synthesis of aurality, visuality and effi-cacy in this inscription.
C06-SERPENT-002   p. 1   also Ch 5
The centrepiece of the inscription is the diagram containing individual letters within separate compartments (Fig. 3), which graphically visualise all the sounds of the Sanskrit alphabet as well as the grammatical terminations of nouns and verbs. Read in a vertical sequence from top to bottom, its geometrical “head” has 14 vowels (svara) ordered in short and long pairs, from the simple (a, ā > i, ̄ > u, ̄ > r, ̄ > l, ̄) to the complex (ē, ai) and compound (ō, au). The tubular “body” below carries a series of consonants, from the 4 semivowels (antahstha) in its neck to the 5 classes of stops (sparsha) in the rhomboidal knot of its torso, and continuing with the 3 voiceless sibilants (ūshman) in its right leg. This series starts and ends with the voiced aspirate ha, occur-ring once before the semivowels and again after the sibilants, in conformity with the classical arrangement of the alphabet given in the Śivasūtras of Pāṇini’s Aṣṭādhyāyī. The horizontal base of the diagram has 3 aspirates, the labial upadhmānīya, guttural jihvāmūlīya and final visarga, but not the nasal anusvāra, which usually counts among the 4 dependent sounds (ayogaṇāha). The left leg contains the characters ra, ya, u, although it is unclear why they are repeated. The tail-end of the diagram displays grammatical endings arranged in triads in the rise and fall of a vertical loop. The former has 21 terminations for nouns of all 7 classes, and the latter, 18 terminations for verbs in present tense in both active (paramaipada) and middle (āmamepada) voice.
C06-SERPENT-003   p. 2
The shape of the block shows that it was part of this very parapet wall but originally faced into the courtyard.
The inscription features a striking visual composition of serpentine form and isolated letters, known in scholarly literature as nāgabandha or sarpabandha (Salomon 1998, 125-127), along with two short inscriptions on one side. The characters are worn off in several places due to the flaking of the sandstone, as seen in the photograph reproduced here, but are fairly well preserved to enable an understanding of the record. The palaeography bears close comparison to local records dating from the late-11th century, which are characterized by well-formed, inch-high characters in a rigidly rectilinear style and verticals that terminate in a sharp bend to the right (Singh 2019; Trivedi 1978, 65-6, 75-82, 98-101). The orthography exhibits the usual peculiarities seen in contemporary inscriptions, such as the use of va for ba and sa for ša, and the doubling of class consonants following r, as in varnṇa.
C06-SERPENT-004   p. 2
These two lines form a metrical verse that reads:
1. bhāradvāṃ jīvihitv=ēmāṃ varnṇa-nāga-krppānikāṃ [l] hitvāpāshanicāṃalā_
2. pālah pālayatu gām || l || ja°abha
The action specified by the imperative verb pālayatu follows a causative construction, having as its agent of causation –pālah and its agent of action, varnṇa-nāga-krppānikām, giving the following sense: “may the protector cause this serpentine sword of letters to protect...” The object of protection is unclear but the main agent along with its pronoun, imāmvarnṇa-nāga-krppānikām, clearly refer to
C06-SERPENT-005   p. 2
This compounded designation would have us conceptualise the graph as a weapon for protection—a sword that is simultaneously serpentine and letterist. To follow its imagery, vowels constitute the hood of a snake, consonants its coiled body, and grammatical terminations its tail; in like manner, the snake’s hood would correspond to the barbed blade of a sword, its knotted body to an ornamental hilt, and its winding tail to a strap. The inscription, thus, presents an unusual synthesis of aurality, visuality and efficacy.
C06-SERPENT-006   p. 3
The abraded surface of the stone does not afford a complete reading, but this very designation appears in dedicatory verses accompanying an identical composition at Ujjain, which is further described as a “unique magical sword” (siddhāṣiputrikā) of the kings Udayāditya (c. 1070–93) and his son Naravarman (1094–1134). Three similar serpentine inscription-tions associated with these Paramāra kings are known from Dhar and Un, allowing us to place the one from Udaypur in the late-11th century. Taken together, the epigraphic evidence suggests that such enigmatic diagrams were inscribed at royal Shiva temples as complex devices in the service of dynastic power.
C06-SERPENT-007   p. 1   also Ch 4
Further information about this inscription may be gleaned from another diagram, identical in all details and bearing the same designation, from the famous Mahākāla temple of Ujjain (Sastri 1960). A verse accompanying this diagram states it to be a “snake-scimitar of letters marked with Udayāditya’s name” (udayāditya-nāmānka-varṇṇa-nāga-krpānikā), which was “composed along with other poetic gems by the friend of talented poets,” apparently an epithet of Naravarman himself. This indicates that the graph was composed in the late-11th century, perhaps not long after king Udayāditya’s death in 1093 and the succession of his son Naravarman (Trivedi 1978, 84, 103). The purport of the diagram is specified in lines 24-26 of the inscription, which describe it as “an ornament (vesha) to be affixed on the chest of both poets and kings, a unique magical sword (siddhāsiputrikā) of Śiva’s worshippers, kings Udayāditya and Naravarman, meant for the preservation of language and social order (varṇnasthityai).”
C06-SERPENT-008   p. 3   also Ch 4
What this shows is that the serpentine graph was not so much an educational tool devised to school young boys in Sanskrit, as scholars have routinely remarked (Trivedi 1978, 88-89; Pollock 2006, 177). Rather it was a complex device pressed into service of the Paramāra kings who owed their allegiance to the cosmic overlord Śiva, praised in the Ujjain inscription as a personification of language itself (Salomon 1982, 556-557). This is perhaps why a copy of the remarkable composition was inscribed at the leading Śiva temples of the Paramāra kingdom—at Ujjain the nerve centre of Malwa but also at Udaypur, a town whose temple and tank were founded and named after king Udayāditya in 1080. The grammatical inscription from Udaypur, together with those from Ujjain, Dhar, and Un, thus, forms an important addition to the corpus of “serpentine scimitars of letters” attributed to Udayāditya and dateable to the last decade of the 11th century.
C06-SERPENT-009   p. 3   also Ch 4
## Some Paramāra Temples From Madhya Pradesh: A Case Study of Village Un — Swati Mondal Adhikari (PARTEMPLES, 4)
The Udaypur temple prasasti of Udayāditya is composed in a style, which speaks of the high literary standard the age had attained.
C06-PARTEMPLES-001   p. 9
For example v.20 of the Udaypur Prasāsti of Udayāditya eulogies Bhōja for beautifying the world with temples dedicated to Śiva under such names as Kedāreśvara, Rāmeśvara, Somanātha, Sunḍira, Kāla, Anala and Rudra. \( ^{2} \)  While Ganguly believes that all these temples were built in the Mālava region, \( ^{3} \)  Trivedi asserts that the first three correspond to the famous temples of similar names respectively in the north, south and west. \( ^{4} \)  Trivedi further assumes that the fourth temple, Sunḍira, must have been built somewhere in the eastern quarter though no trace of it can be found today.
C06-PARTEMPLES-002   p. 9   also Ch 4
In contrast, the inscription on the Nilakaṇthésvara temple at Udaypur (Vidisha district), contains in elaborate details. It was a prasasti composed by Pandita Mahipāla, who was the son of Pandita Śringāvāsa. Thus prasasti of Udayādītya, the builder of the Nilakaṇthésvara contains information regarding the construction and consecration of the temple. The inscription starts with the customary word 'svasti' and ends with the expressions "mangalam, mahāsri" (auspiciousness, great fortune). It is a dated royal inscription and like it most of the inscriptions of Udayādītya are dated and detailed.
C06-PARTEMPLES-003   p. 44   also Ch 5, Ch 4
The Udaypur Prasasti informs that Paramāra king Bhoja the great, a zealous devotee of Śiva, covered the world all around with temples dedicated to Kēdāra, Rāmeśvara, Somanātha, Sundira, Kāla, Anala and Rudra \( ^{3} \) .
C06-PARTEMPLES-004   p. 45   also Ch 4
## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 3)
According to the Udaipur Prashasti (उदयपुर प्रशस्ति), King Bhoj defeated the Chedi king, Indraratha, Toggala, Bhima, and the kings of Karnata, Lata and Gurjara, as well as the Turushkas (Turks).
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 4   also Ch 4
According to the Udaipur Prashasti, Vairisimha alias Vajratasvami, having slain the enemy host with the edge of his sword (*asidhara*), named the town Dhara in memory of his valour.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 55   also Ch 4
In the Bhailasvami Mahadvadashaka province, the provincial governor of Udaipur in 1171 CE was Lunapasaka;² this is clear from the inscription of Vikram Samvat 1229.
