# Chapter 7. The Wider Built Heritage of Udaypur: Fortifications, Shrines, Mosques and Water Structures
## Geo-Heritage of Udaypur, Vidisha District, Madhya Pradesh: The Third Eye (GEOHER, 65)
It sprawled over a few acres and housed the royal palaces, temples, open spaces, and a triple gateway (Teen Darwaza) leading to the royal square, all within a fortified wall with several gates and watch towers.
This area is often referred to as the walled city. The gates still stand, but the walled city is not seen in continuity. Parts of the broken city wall can be seen at various places.
C07-GEOHER-001   p. 7
Talking about this particular hill he writes- "To the south of the city, at a distance of about half a mile, is a hill of a very remarkable appearance; a huge mass of rock rises sheer and abrupt from the top of the conical hill below, presenting at a distance the exact appearance of a fortified castle. This, however, is not a castle, but a natural solid mass of rock, though it is evident that it could, and probably was, made use of as a fort.
At the foot, or rather some little way above the foot of the cone, however, there runs a wall built of huge uncemented stones, 18 feet thick at the present crest, which, however, is not the original crest of the wall, that having disappeared long ago; several gates existed in this wall, which did not go quite round the hill, but enclosed only a portion, in the form of a circular sector, the two radial lines of the sector being represented by two similar massive walls running straight the side of the hill up the to meet the tower-like mass above. The entire place is deserted, and the inhabitants of the adjoining city could give me no information whatever regarding it; it has not even a name that I could hear of".
C07-GEOHER-002   p. 9   also Ch 11
At about 100 meters from this Natraj sculpture, we see a long wall that measures 1400 meters. It is a very thick wall encircling the hillock from all side, a close examination of this 1400 m wall show that this is a fortification wall, meaning that the encircled land is part of a fort complex, which existed prior to Udayaditya, or could have been constructed during the time of Udayaditya as a military Cantonment, because this was the most secured hillock, with very steep slopes.
Walking along this hill fortification wall, we see bastions of different dimensions, the semicircular bastion, the rectangular bastion, and gateways on both sides of the entry gate.
This entire fort complex is a mystery because historical records are silent about it. It is also an architectural marvel that is standing undisturbed over a span of 1400 meters, maybe the longest one anywhere in Madhya Pradesh.
C07-GEOHER-003   p. 19   also Ch 4
This is a 169-acre complex enclosed inside the wall.
C07-GEOHER-004   p. 19
History shows that in those days there used to be a constant war between the Parmar, the Chalukyas, and the Kalachuris, and such massive fortification complexes in one part of the hill, on the upper reaches of the village gave a strategically important advantage. The vantage point of high hills to attack any approaching enemy from a distance. The open landscape all around was also an advantage. The army was staying inside this fortification.
The thickness of the fort's wall, almost 5 m thick, was not easy to break. Interestingly, in the last 1000 years it has not been breached even at a single place.
C07-GEOHER-005   p. 50   also Ch 4
This wall is an excellent example of dry-stonemasonry which has stayed for 1000 years and it speaks about the excellent qualities of workmanship their masons possessed. 1400 m long wall also symbolizes the abundance of Stone in this part of the village, which had been used in the construction of this wall.
C07-GEOHER-006   p. 50   also Ch 2
Some of these antiquities are also seen installed in the fortification wall. It is lightly that these broken pieces of temples and other historical artifacts masturba from adjoining Temple and fitted into the wall.
During our discussion on other aspects of the entire area, we will be talking about the other remains of ancient temples available in this area.
The grand temple must have been on the mid or top level of the hill because the broken parts are found on the lower sides
C07-GEOHER-007   p. 60
According to Chanakya's Arthashastra, there are 6 types of forts in India and he treats forts as one of the major pillars of a king's ascendancy.\(^{10}\)
This research site will fall into the category of Giri-durga typology among the above-mentioned types of fortifications.
C07-GEOHER-008   p. 79
Perimeter-1788 meters & Area-226,593 sq. meters (56 acres)
C07-GEOHER-009   p. 81
The wall is made of sandstone. The stones used are arranged in dry stone rubble masonry, the walls protect the hills from three sides, North, East, and West, while the south side of the hill is too steep to climb, hence, it does not contain any forms of built structures. The wall comprises of two types of stone sizes first the well-shaped and more defined stone used in the outer face of the wall to make it presentable where on the other hand inner void of the thick wall are filled with comparatively smaller size stones which gets easily fixed in the gaps and hold the entire wall tightly.
The Hill Fort in Udaypur has a massive boundary wall made of sandstone running along the periphery of the hill with a total length of about 1400 m. This fortification wall is made from locally available red sandstone, which is found in abundance in Udaypur.
C07-GEOHER-010   p. 87   also Ch 2
The entry gate to the fortification complex is located at 463 meters above mean sea level, and it divides the fortification wall into two parts, the west side being about 810 meters, and the east side is about 600 meters.
The present condition of the fortification wall and other built structures is now completely in ruins, suggesting a strong probability that the hill must have been not merely a settlement but also had served a defensive purpose, emphasizing its strategic significance in the region.
C07-GEOHER-011   p. 87
Length - 1.45 km (avg.)
Width - 4.5-5 meters (at entrance)
Width - 2.5-3 meters
Height varying from 4.3m to 5.2 meters
Circular Bastions-7 in no.
Rectangular Bastion-4 in no.
C07-GEOHER-012   p. 88
The shapes of the bastions found on the Udaypur hill fortification wall are semi-circular, rectangular, and circular in design.
They consist of three stories, which have solid blocks of sandstone firmly placed together or bound with iron clamps on the topmost layer, which is the coping. The weight of the blocks is in tons. There are numerous peepholes on the bastion which were used for defense purposes, either to shoot arrows and guns from higher altitudes, providing an advantage to the soldiers.
C07-GEOHER-013   p. 90
One small rear gateway is present in the Udaypur fortification wall on the western edge. A long sandstone block of length 2300 mm and thickness 150-200 mm forms a lintel supported by two vertical posts on either end.
Height of vertical stone member/post = 1600-1700 mm
Size of openings = 1800 X1567mm
C07-GEOHER-014   p. 92
Rampart: The fortification wall at Udaypur hillfort has a very important feature, i.e., the rampart is the broad-topped part of a defensive wall around a fort. It served as a walkway to facilitate the movement of soldiers during an attack.
It also provided space to stretch and take better aim from the wall, at an attacking army.
C07-GEOHER-015   p. 94
The finished floor surface at Middle Hill, comprised between butte and mesa houses some of the stone structures, which can be the later interventions and encroachments done by locals. This finished platform extends itself to surround the small hill. However, upon careful observation from height and as seen from the lowest elevation, this level could have been the second fortification level, as a bastion-shaped plan forming can be seen in the figure. This space is now used by locals as a picnic spot where they come and spend leisure time, enjoying the surrounding views at this level.
C07-GEOHER-016   p. 97
These were mostly constructed with wood and iron, but the gates of Udaypur town hold contradicting evidence from the rest of the common practices in fort architecture.
The shapes of the bastions found on the Udaypur hill fortification wall are semi-circular, rectangular, and circular in design. They consist of three stories, which have solid blocks of sandstone firmly placed together or bound with iron clamps on the topmost layer, which is the coping. The weight of the blocks is in tons. There are numerous peepholes on the bastion, which were used for defense purposes, either to shoot arrows and guns from higher altitudes, providing an advantage to the soldiers.
C07-GEOHER-017   p. 98
Percolation of water into soil is very low and has induced bulges and the loss of structural integrity. Because the fortification wall was originally built for arid conditions utilizing dry construction, water seepage is a particularly acute problem.
C07-GEOHER-018   p. 103   also Ch 11
Following a steep stone staircase to reach the middle hill level at about 520 meters, INTACH Bhopal's survey team climbed up the rocky terrain. After exploring the upper surface, our survey team importantly found:
Remnants of the boundary wall enclosing various structures
• Stone formations resembling bastions and/or watch towers at the edge of the boundary wall.
Large Water Reservoir with size 11X15.5 meters with four vertically stable erections in the middle of the reservoir.
C07-GEOHER-019   p. 109
Gateway to the Geo Heritage Reserve: Udaypur Hill Fort site already has a well-defined historical gate, existed there for more than 1000 years.
C07-GEOHER-020   p. 150
The next point is the hill fortification wall, which is 1400 meters long. This site has very interesting architectural and archaeological antiquities preserved inside.
C07-GEOHER-021   p. 151
Udaypur Hill is very distinct from a distance by the very large semi-circular fortification thick wall all around it. This is the first time that such extensive documentation of this extremely significant wall has been carried out, and more research needs to be done to go behind the history of the dynasty that occupied this territory before the Parmars of Malwa.
C07-GEOHER-022   p. 153
Apart from that, rock-cut sculptures of Saptamatrika, Navagraha, Siva, Lakshmi, Bhairava, and Ganesha, like Hindu deities, are all seen on this hill. A Jain Tirthankara image is also present in meditative posture in a hill cave with a two-line inscription.
Two other deities are also positioned facing each other on the inside of this cave wall.
C07-GEOHER-023   p. 13   also Ch 5
The antiquities scattered around the stepwell and on the slope- It may be seen that the bunds of the stepwell have shown significant antiquities of the temple of the ancient pre-Parmar era, probably 8th century CE.
C07-GEOHER-024   p. 62
It may be seen that the most significant part of this top-level, apart from being at a height of 567 meters from sea level, also shows the existence of a temple centuries ago at this height. It is not easily accessible.
C07-GEOHER-025   p. 69
However, the long tile-like stone fragment found along the way suggests that this pathway must have once been used as the main entranceway to reach the hilltop. Many mounds of broken stones or debris were found along both sides of the pathway while reaching the staircase flight, depicting the possibility of collapsed structures at these spots. Several temple fragments made of red sandstone with different moldings and carved designs on them were also discovered. A separate research study has also been undertaken to identify and analyze these important temple architecture features.
C07-GEOHER-026   p. 108   also Ch 5
Ruins and debris of carved stones found in a pattern suggesting a much older built structural complex adjacent to the water reservoir.
C07-GEOHER-027   p. 110
Around the outer boundary of Baoli, some of the carved stones were found, out of which four separate fragments seem related to each other because of the similar carved designs engraved on them, and when joined and placed together, they formed a vertical supporting pillar-like element. The identification of the design style and its period of existence will be studied in depth separately.
C07-GEOHER-028   p. 118
At our site, on the eastern surface of the large hill, we found various mythological and religious sculptures engraved on it. One of them is Jain tirthankaras, which were found at two places, the first sculpture installed on a substantially large and elevated platform. The second place where a tirthankara sculpture was found was at some distance from the previous one, as moving forward towards the south side of the hill. The second sculpture was heavily worn out and defaced.
C07-GEOHER-029   p. 141
We also come across a man-made reservoir and broken pieces of the one grand temple at the top. This was a Shiv temple, which is evident from the Shivling found at the top.
C07-GEOHER-030   p. 151
The north gate of this strong fortress (for it must have been very strong in its day) is still in tolerable order, but it shows signs of having been repaired or altered by the Muhammadans: close to this gate is a dargah of a Muhammadan saint, respected vaguely by the people, who, however, being mostly Hindus, do not seem to care much about it.
I could not ascertain the name of the saint, who is generally known as Pir.
C07-GEOHER-031   p. 9   also Ch 10
Villagers come to this part of the hill periodically to pay reverence to the cultural complex, which has a dargah inside along with several Hardol figures, sculptures of Hindu belief kept there.
C07-GEOHER-032   p. 61   also Ch 8
On the western side of this structure, a Female Mazar is also built of size 3.7x4.2 meters by placing stones in an irregular pattern. These dry masonry walls are structurally bound by using tie stones to reinforce it. Female Mazar has an arched stone placed at the entrance gateway leading inside.
C07-GEOHER-033   p. 117
At the middle hill level, a few burial chambers are covered with stones on all sides, creating a rectangular or square sheath over them.
C07-GEOHER-034   p. 118
The Parmar Kingdom placed significant emphasis on the water systems therefore, many stepwells, large reservoirs, and other forms of water systems are found in the village as well as on the geo heritage site, which will be discussed when we do a level-wise analysis. The 2 large water bodies relevant to Udaypur are the Udaysagar Talab, built by Udaya Aditya in 1059 CE, and the Bhujariya Talab, which existed before his time. The architectural details of these 2 tanks are also very different. Bhujariya talab may belong to the 8th or 9th century CE.
C07-GEOHER-035   p. 11   also Ch 4
In the topmost part of the hill, in the middle of the flat hillock, there is a rectangular chamber with lime-plastered pillars inside. These pillars have geometrical shapes and are built of stone sheets and bricks. It is interpreted as a water body, but the locals say it was used as a jail for captives.
C07-GEOHER-036   p. 14
As one approaches from the Udaypur village toward the eastern direction, we see the Udaysagar talab, built by King Udayaditya. Half a kilometer ahead is the Bhujariya Talab, which derives its name from the Bhujariya festival, very popular in rural areas of Madhya Pradesh. This tank predates the Udayaditya Parmar period.
The construction also shows that the stone masonry is not that evolved from an architectural perspective and is finely cut, as we see at the Udaysagar Talab.
C07-GEOHER-037   p. 19
Bhujariya Talab is a natural water body located to the southeast of the village Udaypur. This large water reservoir is located on the outskirts of the village near a live stone quarry covering an area of 8.04 acres. This talab is one of the “chappan baoli, bavan kuae and saadhe barah talab”, or 56 stepwells, 52 wells and 12 and half lakes, according to the locals. When King Udayaditya laid the foundation for the city of Udaypur and Udayeshwar, also known as Neelkantheshwar Temple, in the 11th century CE, it is also mentioned in the Udaypur Prashasti that he built a lake called Udaysagar.
C07-GEOHER-038   p. 49   also Ch 4, Ch 6
There is a small drainage made of stone slabs and covered by it but the rest of it is not excavated.
C07-GEOHER-039   p. 51
The Stepwell -The most significant part of the middle hill is the large stepwell, which was constructed using one side of the hill face and the other three sides. It has systematically built steps over the centuries.
C07-GEOHER-040   p. 61
Therefore, at present, no water is found in this step, well built along the slopes. The period of this stepwell structure could be later Parmar era.
This is a significant source of water storage in the hill region as it used to cater to the needs of the large army maintained inside this fort. Such a stepwell on slopes is unique and found nowhere else in MP.
C07-GEOHER-041   p. 62
The large rectangular water structure with proper steps going down and four pillar-like formations inside is very significant. What could have been the reason … for constructing this reservoir. This is a mystery and will need more research, as at this height, it is not easily accessible. What is sure is that it is a big structure that is an enclosed underground area with thick lime plaster at the base and all along its surface.
C07-GEOHER-042   p. 69–70
Udaysagar Lake is a historically very significant water body of Vidisha district built by the famous Parmar King Udayaditya. Udaysagar Lake is located on the outskirts of Udaypur village in the north with an area of 71 acres.
C07-GEOHER-043   p. 74
This talab is one of the “chappan baoli, bavan kuae and saadhe baarah talab”, or 56 stepwells, 52 wells and 12 and half lakes, according to the locals. When King Udayaditya laid the foundation for the city of Udaypur and Udayeshwar, also known as Neelkanthswar Temple, in the 11th century CE, it is also mentioned in the Udaypur Prashasti that he built a lake called Udaysagar.
C07-GEOHER-044   p. 75
The steps of the bund reveal that it's a structure older than the Udaysagar Talab, 1059 CE. Few sculptures are placed together under a Peepal tree (Ficus religiosa) near the lake. The sculptures include idols of deities like Shiv-Parvati and broken sculptures belonging to Jains.
C07-GEOHER-045   p. 75
On the eastern side of the fortification, there is the Baoli, which holds a strategic position. Baolis are step wells to obtain underground water and to collect rainwater during the monsoon. It is an intelligent and durable technique for obtaining water. Traditionally, baolis have a unique and beautiful architectural importance with impressive appearances.
C07-GEOHER-046   p. 99
The slopes of Pindi Hill on the western side have a large three-sided stepwell, which sometimes must have been used as a reservoir to cater to the needs of users of this hill area. This water retention structure is unique in many aspects, such as:
• It has a regular shape on three sides
• The steps going down to the base are 20 in number.
• The eastern side of the baoli has been used as the fourth wall of the baoli
• The baoli is made of well-chiseled sandstone with some stones projected outwards called muddies, which have been found on steps, which signifies that this stepwell was in abundant use for religious and other purposes.
C07-GEOHER-047   p. 111   also Ch 2
• The embankment of the water system has elements of some temple, which, after a preliminary examination, indicate it to be vertical column elements of a 7th-8th century CE temple.
• The water body has dried out due to structural damage to its framework, and the steps have collapsed.
C07-GEOHER-048   p. 111
This rectangular-shaped depression collects water, having an area of 1072 sq. m and dimensions 24.5x44.5 meters, with around 20 steps leading to its base level. The steps are responsively designed with structurally stable blocks of stone laid with an interlocking technique on intervals, providing the necessary support on the lateral axis as well.
The steps on the northern end are comparatively intact and in place than on the other ends.
C07-GEOHER-049   p. 113
Towards the bottom bed of the cylindrical hill, a small water detention pond of size 1.5x3 meters is formed by cutting and chiseling of the sandstone bed present on site. The water retention basin is used to hold stormwater runoff. It is found in close proximity to the engraved panels of historical and archeological importance, which date back to the 8th-10th century CE.
C07-GEOHER-050   p. 114   also Ch 2
Udaypur's allure extends beyond the temple, with a distinctive feature being its abundance of dry masonry stone structures in abundance all over. These traditional dwellings, constructed from locally sourced materials, showcase the architectural prowess of the region. What sets them apart are the interesting courtyards that bring life to the stone-cladded structures. These courtyards serve as communal spaces where residents gather, fostering a sense of community and warmth.
C07-GEOHER-051   p. 7
There is a huge Mahal complex in the present village habitation. It is in ruins, yet it shows the grandeur it possessed when it was in complete shape. It is a fortified residential palace. It's not dated, but the site examination shows that it had been the seat of power for centuries with different rulers. Who built it and modified it over time is all shrouded in layers of history. It is said to have been built by King Udayaditya when he established the city.
C07-GEOHER-052   p. 7   also Ch 4
A large water reservoir (the drawing of which is in Annexure 10),
2. Several general small stone cubicles (which must have been the house sites centuries ago),
3. Remains of Hindu Temple antiquities are scattered over.
4. Remains of the bastions at the periphery of the hill.
C07-GEOHER-053   p. 69
The rectangular cubicles made of Stone with traces of lime mortar indicate that this place was a habitation site. The systematic layout of these cubicle rooms all around the central reservoir indicates that there must have been a planned project on this side. The remains of the temple, particularly the Shiv temple, show that this was a very significant Hindu pilgrims' site.
C07-GEOHER-054   p. 72   also Ch 8
The Pracheen Mahal Fort was built by King Udayaditya when he established the city of Udaypur. It sprawled over few acres and housed the royal palaces, temples, open spaces, and a triple gateway (Teen Darwaza) leading to the royal square; all within a fortified wall with several gates and watch towers. This area is often referred to as the walled city.
C07-GEOHER-055   p. 74   also Ch 4
Inside the fortification wall, there are ruins of structures and elements that are beautifully decorated with relief carving, floral inlay work, calligraphy, and a highly ornamented pillared hall.
C07-GEOHER-056   p. 99
Structure 4- An enclosed room structure opened from one side, walls made of coursed stone rubble masonry, the roof supported by iron joists which have been corroded. Two vertical pillars support the structure's stability. The capitals of these pillars have carved designs of the chaumukha style.
C07-GEOHER-057   p. 115
These have all been captured in the book “The Architectural Splendor of Udaypur”; the most significant and biggest of them is the Udayeshwar Mandir, also known as Neelkantheshwar temple, a monument protected by the Archaeological Survey of India. Other significant structures of this area are the Udaypur Mahal, Bhujariya Talab, Udaysagar Talab, Ganesh Temple, etc.
C07-GEOHER-058   p. 10
